**Every scheduled job is registered through `scheduler._guarded_job` (DR-59, fixed 2026-09).** `schedule.Job.run()` assigns `self.last_run` and calls `self._schedule_next_run()` only AFTER `job_func()` **RETURNS** (verified against the installed library's source), so an exception that ESCAPES a job leaves `next_run` in the past. `scheduler.main()`'s poll loop catches it (`Scheduler tick raised — daemon continues`) and keeps the daemon alive, but the job is still overdue — so `run_pending()` re-enters the SAME job on the very next 60-second poll tick, turning a weekly production trading run into a once-a-minute one for as long as the fault persists. `run_job` really can raise: the slot-CLAIM `_save_state()` sits OUTSIDE its `try`, the finalizing `_save_state()` sits after that `try` has closed, its two `except` clauses (`TimeoutExpired`, `OSError`) are scoped to the `subprocess.run` call alone, and `subprocess.run(..., capture_output=True, text=True)` decodes the child's streams strictly, so one non-UTF-8 byte raises `UnicodeDecodeError` (a `ValueError`, matching neither clause). The same mechanism defeats the blind-retry one-shot contract: `_blind_retry` signals "run me once" by RETURNING `schedule.CancelJob` — `schedule._run_job` cancels on the value RETURNED to it, never on the identity of the registered function — so a retry that raises returns nothing, is never deregistered, AND never advances the `SCHEDULER_BLIND_MAX_RETRIES` cap, which rides solely on the `retries` argument each attempt hands its successor. **The guard sits at the REGISTRATION boundary, not inside any one helper**, which is what makes it cover every escape path a job has rather than the ones an individual helper happens to know about; `.do(_guarded_job, job, *args, **kwargs)` forwards everything after the first argument through `functools.partial`, so the wrapper is transparent on the healthy path. Two registration sites, and both must stay wired: `main()`'s `schedule.every().monday.at("09:00").do(_guarded_job, run_job)` (no `on_error` — a RECURRING job must be rescheduled by a failure, never cancelled by one) and `run_job`'s blind retry `.do(_guarded_job, _blind_retry, retries + 1, on_error=schedule.CancelJob)` (a ONE-SHOT must deregister itself even when it raises). `_save_state`, `_load_state`, `_maybe_catch_up`, `_startup_catch_up`, `run_job`'s body, the exit-code mapping, the retry cap and BS-17 slot accounting are all deliberately unchanged — in particular `_save_state()` still RAISES, so a failed slot CLAIM still prevents the subprocess spawn (a real-money run with no recorded slot would break BS-17's invariant that a claimed slot always has a record). `KeyboardInterrupt`/`SystemExit` are not `Exception` subclasses and are deliberately not caught, so Ctrl-C still stops the daemon. **The weekly wiring is pinned by AST, and must stay pinned:** `main()` spawns a real prod run and is never invoked by the suite, so no runtime test can reach that line — without `tests/test_scheduler.py::TestGuardedJobRegistration::test_ast_weekly_job_is_registered_through_the_guard` (and its `on_error` sibling for the retry), an edit reverting to `.do(run_job)` restores DR-59 with a green suite. On a healthy host nothing changes; the only behaviour change is that a raising job abandons its slot and waits for the next scheduled fire instead of re-firing every minute. **That trade-off is deliberate and is the accepted cost:** a TRANSIENT fault at 09:00 that the old code would have re-entered 60 seconds later now costs the whole Monday slot, and a raising blind retry ENDS the retry chain for that slot (`on_error=CancelJob` deregisters it) — either is recoverable only by `_maybe_catch_up` on a daemon RESTART, never while the daemon keeps polling. It is still strictly better than the alternative it replaces, which is a real production trading run every 60 seconds for the duration of the fault.

**Strategy change (2026-09) — the time-series bet was inverted.** Until 2026-09 `find_time_series_pairs` fired when the EARLIER-closing contract was priced higher than the later one (`pA - pB >= tier`) and bought NO on the earlier (`market_a`) and YES on the later (`market_b`), on the reasoning that a cumulative-deadline pair must satisfy P(A) ≤ P(B) so a pricier earlier contract is a mispricing with three profitable cells. The bot now trades the opposite anomaly: the LATER contract priced at least the tier ABOVE the earlier (`pB − pA >= min_price_diff_for_gap(gap_days)`), buying YES on the earlier (`pA`) and NO on the later (`nB`), on the reasoning that an outsized `pB − pA` is the market overstating the probability that the event first happens between the two deadlines. `market_a` is still the earlier contract; only the sides and the direction of the filter flipped. Three consequences are real-money facts. (1) **It is a directional bet, not an arbitrage.** Three settlement cells exist — event by A (A=YES ⇒ B=YES, the YES on A pays), never by B (A=NO, B=NO, the NO on B pays), in between (A=NO, B=YES, both worthless — the whole stake is lost). At market prices its expected value is zero minus fees; it only profits if the market systematically overstates the in-between mass. "Risk-free"/"guaranteed" wording is gone for time-series everywhere; same-title keeps its co-resolution framing and its wire bytes unchanged. (2) **The Kelly model is `p = 1 − k·(pB − pA)`** (`config.time_series_profit_prob(pA, pB, k=None)`, the single definition that `strategy._kelly_p`, `backtester._simulate_at_discount` and `dashboard._kelly_fraction(pA, nA, pB, nB, pair_type, k=None)` all call — pinned by value and by a two-link AST chain, see the calibration note below), with `k = TIME_SERIES_INTERVAL_PROB_DISCOUNT = 0.75`: the operator believes 75% of the market-implied in-between mass ("prices converge by 25%"). This is an operator-tunable ESTIMATE. With `k = 1` (take the market at face value) or an independence model of the new loss cell, Kelly is ≤ 0 for every time-series pair and nothing ever trades (illustrative fixture 0.30 → 0.60, NO ask 0.40: `k=1` ⇒ p 0.70, f* −0.12; independence ⇒ p 0.58, f* −0.56; `k=0.75` ⇒ p 0.775, f* +0.16). Leaving the LITERAL old expression `1 − pA·(1−pB)` in place would NOT have disabled trading — it would have modelled the impossible A=YES/B=NO cell and sized real trades on it, which is why the formula is pinned rather than merely retuned. (3) **Kelly, not the 20% cap, now sizes typical pairs.** With `k=0.75`, reading off an earlier contract at `pA` = 0.30 on a tight book (`nB = 1 − pB`), the uncapped fraction is ≈ 0.05 at the minimum short-tier gap and ≈ 0.16 at a 0.30 gap, so `BUDGET_FRACTION` (0.20) only binds for gaps of roughly 0.45 or more; **name the reference `pA` whenever quoting these** — the fraction is strongly `pA`-dependent (at the same 0.15 tier it is ≈ 0.14 at `pA` 0.10, ≈ 0.08 at 0.20, ≈ 0.03 at 0.40); a wide book (large `pA + nB` for the same YES-ask gap) drives Kelly negative and the pair is skipped (the same fixture with the later NO ask at 0.50 instead of 0.40 returns `None`). And because a pricier later contract is NORMAL term structure, far more time-series groups qualify than before (one best pair per normalized title+outcome key still applies — `scanner.time_series_group_key`, see the cross-strike gotcha — keyed on the largest `pB − pA`, which no longer bounds a whole daily family to one pair). **What to look for in outputs:** the prod Excel log keeps 18 columns but its x/y headers are now "x — A leg"/"y — B leg", its "Total Cost ($)" column is "Total Cost incl. fees ($)" and its "Min Profit ($)" column is "Profit if won ($)" (headers on new workbooks only — an existing shared `trade_log.xlsx` keeps its old header row, since `_append_locked` writes headers only when creating the file; the VALUES change in every workbook) and every row's Notes cell is prefixed `[<pair_type>: <SIDE_A> A / <SIDE_B> B[ nB=0.xxxx] fees=$x.xx] ` — the `fees=` suffix is what makes a row readable under an old header, since the cost beside it is now fee-inclusive (TS-12); for time-series rows the retained "nA (NO ask)" column is A's best NO ask (reporting only, not a traded price) and the traded `nB` lives in Notes. The dev-simulation candidates sheet gains an "nB (NO ask)" column (13 headers) and its "Price Diff" is `pB − pA` for time-series, `pA − pB` for same-title; `main.print_pairs_table` gains an "nB (NO)" column and its "Min Profit" header is now "Profit (win)" (and, from DR-17, "Outcome A"/"Outcome B" columns carrying each leg's subtitle). `BacktestTrade` carries `entry_nB` (from the candle's `no_ask_close` — old caches need no refresh and there is no `SETTLED_PREFILTER_CACHE_TAG` bump) and `deadline_gap_days` (the calendar-day gap between the two legs' DEADLINES that `_find_entry` selected the price tier and applied the `MAX_DEADLINE_GAP_DAYS` cutoff on, carried out rather than recomputed — the legs' `close_time` gap for a cross-event pair, the two STATED deadlines' gap for a same-event ladder (DR-73), which for a ladder routinely differ (measured on a 1,506-market archive corpus: 101 of 408 ladder pairs have a ZERO-day close gap, 37 closing at the identical instant, while every stated gap is in [1, 30]); it is also what `_interval_calibration` bands `k̂` on, through `CalibrationObservation.gap_days`; `None` for same_title, which has no deadline-gap concept, and for any trade built without it, e.g. test fixtures — reporting only, nothing sizes, prices or settles on it), its `profit` is negative only in the in-between cell, and its `slippage` for time-series is zero in both win cells and negative in the loss cell (there is no positive-slippage cell). `run_backtest` SKIPS (never trades or pays) any time-series candidate that settled A=YES/B=NO and logs ONE summary WARNING with the count (silent at zero): most likely a wording false negative — a snapshot family, or a recurring window worded "before <date>", read as cumulative — though DR-72 widened the WARNING to also name legs ordered on an early REALIZED close (cross-event pairs only — a same-event ladder is ordered on its stated deadlines), and strike-blind grouping on a cache without subtitles, rather than pointing at the wording heuristic alone. **Since DR-67 that is no longer the only signal** — both finders screen the legs' wording first (`scanner.cumulative_deadline_pair`), so the counter is defence in depth behind a heuristic and a non-zero count means one of those causes fired, most likely the wording one. Expect it at or near zero on a post-DR-67 run. That signal is only trustworthy because `_find_entry` orders a time-series pair's legs on the close **DATETIMES**, like the live finder's `scanner.find_time_series_pairs` sorting its group members on `m.close_time`: two contracts closing on the same UTC date at different times of day are a valid zero-day-gap pair, and deciding the swap on the parsed DATES made them a tie, leaving "market A" as whichever the group list held first — so the pair was either dropped (the direction test goes negative) or replayed with its legs inverted, whose genuine in-between settlement then read as the impossible A=YES/B=NO cell and was booked as a *false* premise violation, corrupting the interval-discount calibration's denominator too (TS-06, fixed 2026-09). **That parity is on the DATETIME-vs-DATE fix only, not on what `close_time` means.** The two paths read the same field name on the close datetimes, but not the same MOMENT: live reads the SCHEDULED close of a still-open market, while the backtest reads the REALIZED close Kalshi recorded for a settled one, which for an event that resolved early (a sports market decided before its listed deadline) can sit before the original schedule. A later-deadline leg that resolved early is therefore ordered first, on the realized rather than the scheduled gap — a recorded, pre-existing residual (backtest-only; it needs a scheduled-close field via a refetch, or span-date parsing off the wording), not something this fix addresses. `_extract_pairs`' close-time sort deliberately stays a DATE sort (a datetime sort would raise on a naive/aware mix from a hand-edited cache); only the swap decision reads datetimes, keeping the date comparison as its `TypeError` fallback. **Not updated by this change:** `kalshi_bot_flowchart.pdf` predates it (it already showed `|pA−pB|` and the wrong dedup order) and is NOT regenerated; `BUG_SWEEP_FINDINGS.md` is a dated, frozen record and is left untouched; `scheduler.py`'s cosmetic "arbitrage" wording is left alone (it never parses trader or main log text). **Deferred follow-up:** fractional-contract sizing remains outstanding (`_format_count` still always emits `.00`). Follow-up (a) — calibrating `TIME_SERIES_INTERVAL_PROB_DISCOUNT` from the backtester — shipped 2026-09; see the next note for what it built.

**Interval-discount calibration (2026-09 follow-up) — `k` is measurable now, but only inside the backtest.** `config.time_series_profit_prob(pA, pB, k=None)` takes an optional per-call override, resolved at call time (`discount = TIME_SERIES_INTERVAL_PROB_DISCOUNT if k is None else k`, so a test that monkeypatches the constant still takes effect whenever `k` is omitted — mandatory, since a `k: float = TIME_SERIES_INTERVAL_PROB_DISCOUNT` default would bind at def time instead), and `backtest.py` exposes it as `--interval-discount K` (`parser.error`s outside `[0, 1]`; omitted/`None`, the default, reads the config constant) plus `--no-sweep` (skips only the extra per-`k` re-simulation grid — the calibration measurement below still runs and logs either way). The live sizer is untouched by construction, not by discipline: `strategy._kelly_p` calls `time_series_profit_prob(pA, pB)` with no `k`, so live sizing always reads `config.TIME_SERIES_INTERVAL_PROB_DISCOUNT` no matter what any backtest run passes — calibration writes a recommendation into the backtest log and the dashboard, and nothing anywhere writes `config.py`. `backtester.run_backtest()` keeps its exact signature and `tuple[list[BacktestTrade], pd.DataFrame]` return by becoming a thin wrapper split at the `k` boundary, so every pre-existing caller of `run_backtest()` (all of `tests/test_backtester.py`, unchanged) still gets byte-identical behavior: `_prepare_entries(hist_client, live_client, start_date, use_cache, max_horizon_days) -> tuple[list[dict] | None, OutcomeLabelCoverage | None]` is the `k`-INDEPENDENT prologue (the Monday-feasibility pre-check, `fetch_all_settled_markets`, the eligibility prefilter, both groupings, `_extract_pairs`, the parallel candlestick fetch, and the `_find_entry` sweep — none of it touches a probability model, so it runs exactly once no matter how many discounts get simulated), and `_simulate_at_discount(raw_entries, start_date, initial_balance, k=None, spread_band=None, population="all", *, size_cap=None, quiet=False, end_date=None) -> SweepPoint` is the `k`-DEPENDENT tail (the Kelly gate, the outcome-validity and premise-violation checks, the one-pair-per-group dedup, `_drop_cross_type_duplicates`, chronological Pass 2, and `_build_equity_curve`; `spread_band` and `population` only label its completion line and stamp the point), re-run once per swept discount over the SAME prepared entries. `_prepare_entries` is itself the composition of `_prepare_candidates` (everything through the candlestick fetch — band- and `k`-independent, returned as a `_Candidates` that carries the candles, the census and the start date / horizon / unresolved ladder flag every entry pass must reuse) and one `_entries_for_band` `_find_entry` pass at the default band; `run_backtest_sweep` does NOT call `_prepare_entries` — it runs `_prepare_candidates` once and then `_sweep_from_candidates`, which makes one `_entries_for_band` pass per spread band before any simulation. **The prologue's return is a PAIR, and the infeasibility sentinel lives on element 0** (DR-66b): a caller that forgets to unpack holds a 2-tuple, which is never `None`, so its `if raw_entries is None` guard silently goes false and an infeasible window walks into `_simulate_at_discount` holding a tuple. Element 1 is the run's `OutcomeLabelCoverage` census, carried out to the dashboard, and is `None` on exactly the feasibility-short-circuit path, where the fetch never ran — distinct from a censused corpus of zero records, which carries `total=0`. `run_backtest` unpacks and discards it (`raw_entries, _ = ...`), keeping its exact signature and two-tuple return. Each swept point of the primary band ANNOUNCES itself, and every simulation names its resolved `k` (and the band and population it simulated) on its `Backtest complete at k=..., band ..., <population>:` line, with `k` and each band bound printed exactly whenever the short form (`%.3f`, `%g`) would print two different values alike, so every prefix within one sweep is unique: that line is emitted once per swept discount — thirteen times on a run without the band sweep (`--no-band-sweep`, or `run_backtest_sweep`'s own `band_sweep=False` default), and once per band x `k` x population plus each split-half and ex-top run on a band sweep, which the CLI runs by default — and used to carry no `k` at all, while the primary's slot went unnumbered so the `Sweeping i/13` counter appeared to start at 2 (TS-21). A simulation at any per-trade size cap other than `BUDGET_FRACTION` names it BEFORE the colon (`..., <population>, cap 35%:` / `..., no cap:`, through the injective `backtester._cap_label`), so the default-cap line is byte-identical to the pre-cap one; the lazy size-cap runs are `quiet=True`, which logs that line — and the premise-violation WARNING, text unchanged — at DEBUG, so a run's INFO log carries exactly the eager lines it did before (see the size-cap gotcha below). The premise-violation WARNING's PREFIX (up to "(earlier YES, later NO)") and its pinned substring "snapshot markets" are held across DR-72's rewrite; its full wording is no longer pinned byte-for-byte — DR-72 rewrote the CAUSES it names — and it is labelled by the lines around it. **`_build_equity_curve` opens every curve ONE DAY BEFORE `start_date` at the untouched initial balance**, so `run_backtest`'s `equity_df` runs from `start_date − 1 day` through today (UTC) and a future `start_date` yields exactly two rows, not one. That leading row is what makes a day-0 entry visible: the default window starts on a Monday (`--start-date 2024-01-01`), `_find_entry` can open a trade on `start_date` itself, and applying that day's charges to the FIRST row hid them from `pct_change` and `cummax` — the dashboard reported max drawdown 0.0% and Sortino 0.00 on a fixture that ended day one holding $1.84 of CASH out of its $10,000, while `_section_interval_discount`'s per-`k` row divided by the depleted opening (`iloc[0]`) and the performance card divided by `initial_balance`, reporting one run two ways on one page (DR-03). DR-61 later changed WHAT day 0 costs (see the mark-to-cost note below) without touching that guarantee — on the same fixture the day-0 step is now its $476.56 of taker fees rather than the whole $9,998.16 stake, and the leading row is still what keeps the `cummax` peak at $10,000 instead of at the already-charged $9,523.44 (measurably: dropping it shallows the reported drawdown from −75.3616% to −74.1287%). Don't re-anchor the curve on `start_date`: `dashboard.py` reads `equity_df` whole-column, by date, or by `iloc[0]`/`iloc[-1]` — never by positional index against a separately-built array — so the leading row needs no positional realignment anywhere (the order-dependent whole-column reads, `cummax` and `pct_change`, are exactly the ones it is meant to change), and `_section_benchmark` opens its `^GSPC` download on `start_date − 1 day` so the benchmark window starts on the same date as the strategy trace (yfinance serves trading days only, so its first bar can land later). Two things change in the return series, not one: it gains a leading `0.0`, and `start_date`'s own return stops being forced to `0.0`. **Every Sharpe/Sortino figure DR-03 and DR-56 recorded on this fixture is now HISTORICAL and does not reproduce** — DR-61 (next paragraph) replaced the cash-only series they were measured on, so the whole before/after log that stood here (`_sharpe` 1.53 → 1.52 and `_sortino` 0.00 → 2033.35 at a hardcoded `sqrt(252)`; their DR-56 rebases to ~1.83 and ~2447 at 365; the per-row-count tables at 40/60/79/100/109 rows) describes a curve whose settlement-day rebound no longer exists. Do not diff against those numbers; three mechanisms from them are still live and are the reason the log is summarized rather than deleted. (1) **The annualization base:** `_sharpe`/`_sortino` take a caller-named `periods_per_year` defaulting to `config.CALENDAR_DAYS_PER_YEAR` (365), because `_build_equity_curve` emits one row per CALENDAR day while the `^GSPC` benchmark row is a TRADING-day series and passes `config.TRADING_DAYS_PER_YEAR` (252) explicitly; at `rf = 0` the 252 → 365 change is an exact `sqrt(365/252)` = 1.2035 rescale of MAGNITUDE that leaves the sign alone (at `rf != 0` it is not a constant factor at all, since the per-period hurdle moves too). (2) **Both ratios drift with the CURRENT DATE** — the curve runs through `datetime.now(UTC)`, so the fixture gains a flat row every day — which is why **every re-measurement must be labelled with WHICH ratio it is and how many rows it covers**; an unlabelled `x/y` pair beside a sentence quoting a Sharpe AND a Sortino reads as sharpe/sortino and manufactures a false regression signal. (3) **A Sortino of exactly `0.00` is base-INDEPENDENT** (the pre-DR-03 series had no negative day at all, so `_sortino` takes its `dd > 0 else 0.0` early return at every base and every length — verified 2026-09-16 at bases 1/252/365/1000 and at 40/79/109/200 rows); a `0.00` is therefore a structural reading, never a rescale. **Re-measured on the current (DR-61) curve, 2026-09-16, 110 rows** (the curve runs to today UTC, which was already 2026-09-17 when this was measured — the row count, not the date, is the label that matters)**:** the DR-03 fixture is monotone declining — its only two non-flat days are the entry-day fees and the settlement write-off — so BOTH ratios are NEGATIVE, `sharpe@252` −1.6085065 / `sharpe@365` −1.9358378 and `sortino@252` −1.6075614 / `sortino@365` −1.9347004, with max drawdown −75.3616% on 2026-06-30 (equal, as it should be on a monotone decline, to the run's −75.4% total return). The cash-only curve read that same fixture, at the same 110 rows, as `sharpe@365` +1.8202136 and `sortino@365` +2435.9905 (`sharpe@252` +1.5124332, `sortino@252` +2024.0882) — a strongly positive risk-adjusted return on a run that lost three quarters of the balance, which is the sharpest single illustration of what DR-61 fixed. The ~+1.83 / ~+2447 pair the DR-56 log carried is the same reading one row shorter (109 rows); both drift daily, which is why neither may be quoted without its row count. `SweepPoint(k, trades, equity_df, spread_band=None, population="all", halves=None, ex_top_event=None, size_cap=None, peak_kelly_fraction=None)` stamps the RESOLVED discount (never `None`), the resolved band its entries were detected under, its population, the resolved per-trade size cap and the largest uncapped Kelly fraction any candidate reached (cap-independent — see the size-cap gotcha below), but deliberately carries no `total_return`/`max_drawdown`/Sharpe of its own — `dashboard.py` imports FROM `backtester.py`, so the reverse would be circular, and the dashboard derives both per point from `equity_df` with the `_max_drawdown`/`_sharpe` helpers it already owns; the only returns it does carry, `halves` and `ex_top_event` (set on a band sweep's "all" and "time_series" points), belong to re-simulations whose curves are not kept. `backtest.py` itself was switched to call the richer entry point instead of `run_backtest`: `run_backtest_sweep(..., interval_discount=None, sweep=True, spread_band=None, band_sweep=False, cap_sweep=False) -> BacktestSweep` runs one `_prepare_candidates` pass and one `_entries_for_band` time-series pass per spread band (one band unless `band_sweep`; on a band sweep only the no-band pre-pass scans every pair, and every other band rescans only the pairs that entered there), one `_interval_calibration` measurement per band (see below — this report is produced ONLY through this entry point, never through the plain `run_backtest()` wrapper), simulates the primary `k` with the caller's override passed through verbatim (sentinel included, so a no-override run prices identically to `run_backtest()`), then — unless `sweep=False` — runs one more `_simulate_at_discount` per point of `sorted(set(config.INTERVAL_DISCOUNT_SWEEP) | {effective_k})`, so the primary is always an EXACT grid member (`--interval-discount 0.62` still gets its own entry in the dashboard filter bar's `k` select rather than the nearest standard point) and is the SAME `SweepPoint` object inside `BacktestSweep.points`, never a duplicate. `BacktestSweep(primary, points, calibration, label_coverage=None, scenarios=[], same_title_point=None, calibrations_by_band={}, same_event_ladders=None, split_date=None, corpus_provenance=None, cap_sweep=None)` — `points`/`calibration` are always the PRIMARY band's, `scenarios`/`same_title_point`/`calibrations_by_band`/`split_date` are the band sweep's payload, `same_event_ladders` the resolved ladder setting and `cap_sweep` the lazy size-cap sweep (`None` unless asked for); when `_prepare_candidates` hits its Monday-feasibility short-circuit, `run_backtest_sweep` still returns one empty point, `calibration=None`, `label_coverage=None`, `scenarios=[]`, `calibrations_by_band={}` and `cap_sweep=None`, so no caller needs a special case for that path. `label_coverage` hangs off the sweep for the SAME stated reason `calibration` does — one measurement over one corpus, `k`-independent, valid at every point — and never off a `SweepPoint`, where thirteen copies of one measurement would invite thirteen disagreements. It is DEFAULTED so no hand-built construction breaks, which is the accepted residual: a caller that omits the keyword renders "not measured" on the page rather than the caveat, so the two production constructions — `run_backtest_sweep`'s infeasible branch and `_sweep_from_candidates` — must always pass it. The empirical measurement, `_interval_calibration(raw_entries, spread_min=None) -> IntervalCalibration | None` (`spread_min` only LABELS each gap band's tier with the band floor its entries were detected under) (logged by `_log_interval_calibration` at INFO; since DR-72 `None` logs one explanatory line — "no time-series candidate entry with a readable settlement in this window — empirical k_hat is not measurable" — rather than nothing at all; the wording says "with a readable settlement" because the `None` branch also fires when time-series entries existed but every one was dropped for a non-binary outcome, though the sub-counts inside a non-`None` report, e.g. the excluded-premise-violations line, stay silent at zero), computes `k_hat = realised in-between rate / mean(pB − pA)`, pooled and per deadline-gap band (`0-7d` / `8-15d` / `16-30d`, edged on `SHORT_DEADLINE_GAP_DAYS`/`MAX_DEADLINE_GAP_DAYS` so no band straddles a price tier) as `IntervalCalibrationBucket(label, tier, n, realised_rate, mean_implied, empirical_k)` rows, where `empirical_k` is `None` — not `0.0` — whenever `mean_implied <= 0`, since the ratio is undefined rather than zero. The report also carries the population it pooled: `IntervalCalibration.observations`, one frozen `CalibrationObservation(gap_days, implied, in_between, event_ticker, category)` per measured candidate in measurement order (market A's event ticker as the entry carries it, and `infer_category` of it — the fallback `BacktestTrade.category` carries), so `_calibration_bucket(label, 0.0, observations)` on that very tuple reproduces the pooled row exactly (a regrouped, reordered or reassembled copy agrees only to float rounding) and a report can regroup the same population through the same arithmetic instead of re-deriving which entries count — the dashboard's `k̂` breakdown does, by Kalshi category, tag and spread band. `event_ticker`/`category` are REQUIRED (the DR-71 reasoning: a forgotten field would file an observation under a category no market has), while `observations` defaults to `()` so hand-built calibrations still build — a consumer tells "not carried" from "no candidate" by comparing `len(observations)` with `pooled.n`, never by testing `observations == ()`. It holds strings and scalars only, at entry scale, never a market record, so the per-band calibrations a sweep keeps pin no record alive (TS-07) — which is why it does not collide with DR-66b's rule that `OutcomeLabelCoverage` must carry no per-category breakdown: that rule is about a CORPUS-scale census holding record examples. Its population is deliberately `k`-INDEPENDENT: read straight off the prepared entries (`_prepare_entries()`'s, or one band's `_entries_for_band()` output inside a sweep), never off `_simulate_at_discount()`'s Kelly-surviving candidates, because filtering by the Kelly gate first would make the estimate circular — a wrong `k` would confirm itself by excluding exactly the candidates that would have disproven it; this is also why an `IntervalCalibration` is computed ONCE per spread band (the primary band's as `calibration`, every swept band's in `calibrations_by_band`) and hangs off `BacktestSweep` rather than off any single `SweepPoint` — it is valid for every `k` at that band. Two same-shape, differently-named premise-violation counts now exist and must never be conflated: `_simulate_at_discount`'s own local `premise_violations` (its WARNING level — now a `logging.log(...)` that drops to DEBUG only on a `quiet` size-cap re-run — and its statement order unchanged from before the split; DR-72 rewrote the wording's named causes, not its position in the pipeline) counts candidates AFTER the Kelly gate — load-bearing statement order, preserved exactly — so it is itself `k`-DEPENDENT, since a different `k` changes which candidates even reach the check; `IntervalCalibration.excluded_premise_violations`, logged separately by `_log_interval_calibration` at INFO, counts over the WHOLE `k`-independent population instead, so it is generally the LARGER of the two. Both exclude the same cell (earlier YES / later NO, impossible for a cumulative-deadline pair) from their own denominator and neither pays it; the distinct names exist precisely so a reader never mistakes one count for a bug in the other. The AST pin that used to assert `run_backtest` calls `time_series_profit_prob` directly is now a two-link chain, since the priced call moved into `_simulate_at_discount`: `tests/test_strategy.py::test_ast_backtester_prices_through_helper` asserts both `_function_calls(backtester, "_simulate_at_discount", "time_series_profit_prob")` and `_function_calls(backtester, "run_backtest", "_simulate_at_discount")` — same intent as the single-link pin it replaced (the backtester must price time-series through the shared config helper, never reimplement the formula), preserved across the extraction. `dashboard.py` gained a seventh section, `_section_interval_discount(sweep, kd=None, *, bar=True, kd_failed=False)` (the outcome-label coverage line or banner — see the next sentence — then KPI cards for the `k` selected / pooled empirical `k̂` / their delta ("k̂ − k (primary band, pooled)"), the bucket table, ONE equity-curve trace (`div_id="kd-equity"`) and a per-`k` trades/return/final balance/drawdown/Sharpe table (`<tbody id="kd-rows">`), all at the primary spread band). It used to carry a native Plotly `updatemenus` dropdown that switched the curve between one trace per swept `k` and drove that section alone; since the filter bar gained its `k` select (C3) the bar's `k` and size cap drive the section instead — see the `k̂`-cards sentence below. A later commit added `_section_scenario_explorer(sweep)` after it (the eighth section added, described in full at the end of this paragraph; the `k̂` breakdown now sits between the two), with its own independent band/`k` `<select>`s — so of the nine sections on the page today, the other SEVEN — six trade-derived ones (performance, decomposition, calibration, diagnostics, risk, benchmark) and the `k̂` breakdown — are RENDERED at the primary scenario, meaning the primary `k`, the primary spread band (`--spread-min`/`--spread-max`) AND the run's own per-trade size cap (`config.BUDGET_FRACTION`) (the `k̂` breakdown is `k`- and cap-independent; the `k` is only its reference line); the scenario explorer's own selects are scoped to that section alone. **A third set of controls, the sticky page-wide filter bar (2026-09), re-scopes exactly those seven** (plus the header's trade count and the performance section's two `k̂` cards) **and moves the interval-discount section to its `k` and size cap — nothing else**: its Spread band, `k` and Size cap choices pick a SCENARIO — that band's OWN run at that `k` and per-trade size cap, a standalone simulation, so every figure is genuine: the band sweep's `population="all"` point at the run's own cap, or, for any other cap, the lazy `BacktestSweep.cap_sweep`'s point, simulated as the page is built (see the size-cap gotcha below) — and its Category and Tag choices show a SLICE of that run, filed by `dashboard._series_labels` (Kalshi's category and the series' FIRST tag, the rule the Returns Decomposition already used, so every breakdown partitions), whose figures drawn from an equity curve — return, drawdown, Sharpe, Sortino, the median monthly return and the benchmark's strategy row — come from `backtester._build_equity_curve` over the slice's trades alone: the slice's contribution, not a standalone simulation, and the bar's summary line says so. Every view is computed in Python by the helpers the sections render with (`_performance_kpis`, `_performance_series`, `_decomposition_aggregates`, `_category_table`, `_reliability`, `_best_and_worst`, `_kelly_points`, `_capital_deployed`, `_strategy_row`) — `dashboard._list_payload` per scenario trade list, inside ONE walk over the whole grid (`dashboard._grid_source` picks it: the size-cap sweep's bands x `k`s x caps when the sweep carries one, else the eager points at the run's own cap, else one "not recorded" scenario; `dashboard._walk_grid` reads it one (band, `k`) cell at a time, the primary cell and cap first, handing each scenario to visitors that keep only what they build, so only one cell's points are ever alive; a size-cap cell that cannot be simulated — or a size-cap sweep whose axes or entries' events cannot be read — costs the cap axis alone: one WARNING, the eager points walked instead, and the header's run-settings line saying the sweep could not be used, so the one-option Size cap select has its reason on the page; every point's curve is cut to the page's last date first, never modified in place) — and every list is its own gzip + base64 chunk (`<script id="dash-chunk-N">`, `_packed_json_script`) beside one base block (`id="dash-data"`, `dashboard._filter_payload`: the axes, a `[band][k][cap]` grid of chunk ids, the labels, templates and styles, the `k̂` breakdown per band, and the shared trade-row heads). Scenarios whose trade lists are equal at an equal `k` share one chunk (`dashboard._list_key` — `k` is in the key because the Kelly scatter is priced at it; every cap at or above a cell's peak Kelly fraction trades the same list), curves ship as change points only, and a best/worst row ships as its size-independent head (`_trade_row_head`, once per page, in the base block) and its tail (`_trade_row_tail`: `n`, cost, profit), which the script joins, so `_trade_row` — and the diagnostics section — stay byte-identical. Measured 2026-09-26, after the `k̂` cards and the interval-discount section's data joined the base block (C3): `TestFilterPageSize`'s fixture (36 bands, each a different 40-trade list) is an 819 KB page of 36 chunks (812 KB the same day before them; 745 KB as the one block it was before the chunks, since each list compressed alone forgoes what one shared block compressed across lists), and a synthetic 36-band x 13-`k` x 20-cap grid whose cells share 4 lists packs 52 chunks and a 56 KB base block — 707 KB of JSON once inflated, 254 KB of it the section's `"kd"` and 216 KB the `k̂` breakdown with its `k̂ − k` per `k` — into a 1.23 MB page in 5.0 s (on the same harness before C3: a 35 KB base block of 320 KB JSON, and a 1.28 MB page, since the section then drew a trace per `k`). The page is streamed to disk piece by piece through one open handle (every chunk, then the base block, then the script) and renamed over the old one, never joined into one string. `_FILTER_JS` computes no figure and words nothing about the data itself (its one sentence of its own is the line it shows when the base block cannot be loaded) — the summary line is `dashboard._SUMMARY_TEMPLATES`, filled by `_filter_summary_text` for the rendered view and by the script for every other, and the `k̂` chart's words are `_KHAT_TEXT`, used the same way — and draws each trace by copying the one Python drew and swapping its data (the trade-type lines, which another band may hold where the primary holds none, from the styles Python drew them with). On load it inflates the base block and the primary scenario's chunk with the browser's `DecompressionStream`, sets the five selects back to the rendered view (a browser can restore a stale choice on reload; they are also rendered `autocomplete="off"`) and only then enables them — Python renders them disabled, so a category or tag change never meets an unloaded chunk — and it redraws nothing until a `<select>` changes: the page as rendered IS the primary scenario's unfiltered view. Any other scenario's chunk is inflated when first chosen (a `loading` line meanwhile) and kept while among the last 16 drawn (`KEEP`; the primary's always — a chunk that arrives after the reader has chosen another scenario was never drawn, so it is dropped rather than counted, and the chunk on screen is never pushed out); a choice made while a chunk is still loading supersedes it (`SEQ` — a chunk arriving after a later choice is never drawn); a scenario the run never simulated shows the empty view and says so (the `missing` template); and a chunk that cannot be loaded is named on the summary line (`unavailable`), every select — the category and tag too, since one chosen while it loaded was never drawn either — goes back to what is still shown (`SHOWN`), and choosing it again retries it. The `k̂` chart reads `SHOWN`, not the selects, so a Group-by change while a chunk is loading describes the sections on screen. Each redraw starts from the chart's layout as Python drew it, captured on load, so a reader's zoom, hidden legend line or box selection never carries into another view; `sizeTo` writes a row-dependent height on both the chart's box and the one around it, since plotly.py puts it on one or the other by version. `generate_dashboard` resolves ONE `k` for the page as rendered — the `interval_discount` override, else the sweep's primary `k` — for the Risk section's Kelly scatter, the primary scenario's chunk, the summary line and the `k̂` chart's reference line (with neither, the scatter prices at the config constant, the summary says "k not recorded" and the `k̂` chart draws no line); with a sweep the bar's `k` axis is the sweep's own, its primary entry `sweep.primary.k`, so an override that differs from it (never in production — `backtest.py` passes `result.primary.k`) is unsupported: the scatter, the primary chunk and the `k̂` line as rendered price at the override while the summary names the sweep's `k`, and a WARNING says so (the performance section's `k̂ − k` card is priced at the sweep's `k`, like the interval-discount section's and the bar's); a chosen scenario's chunk is priced at its own `k`, and the `k̂` chart's line moves to it (drawn from the base block's `khat_ref` line, so the script's line and Python's cannot differ). A filter that cannot be built — the base block (`_filter_payload`, the seam the tests break), or any chunk (the chunk visitor catches its own failure) — costs the bar, never the page: it is written without the bar, its chunks and its script, with `dashboard._FILTER_UNAVAILABLE_HTML` in the bar's place and a WARNING in the log. `tests/test_dashboard.py::TestFilterScript` and `::TestFilterKAndCap` run the script itself over the rendered page under `tests/js/filter_harness.js` (node in CI, macOS's JavaScriptCore `jsc` locally, skipped when neither is present; some in its strict mode, where a missing element is null as in a browser). The `k̂` breakdown (`dashboard._section_khat`, the section right after this one) is the one of the seven that does not describe trades: it regroups each band's carried `IntervalCalibration.observations` through `backtester._calibration_bucket`, filed by `_series_labels` on market A's event ticker (the leg `BacktestTrade.event_ticker` records), by Kalshi category, by tag or by spread band — a `Group by` `<select>` of its own, outside the chart's body so it stays reachable when a view has nothing to draw, chooses the axis while the bar chooses the band and the category or tag, and grouping by one of them shows every value of it with the selection highlighted. Each bar names its entries and its distinct events (a ladder's rungs share one event; one question listed as several events still counts each), a dashed line marks the page's `k` (the run's own as rendered, the bar's `k` once one is chosen), and its cells, bar labels and hover figures are pre-formatted in Python (`_khat_stat`), so neither the script nor Plotly's hover can round a figure differently from the table. A band with no calibration reads "not recorded", never "none measured". Of the interval-discount section the bar reaches the `k` and size cap only, always at the primary spread band (its band, category and tag never reach it), and it never reaches the scenario explorer; the summary line says so (`dashboard._BAR_REACH`). **The `k̂` cards and the interval-discount section follow the bar (C3).** Portfolio Performance carries two more cards, "Empirical k̂" and "k̂ − k (this selection)" (`dashboard._khat_kpis`, appended with a caption through `_section_performance(..., extra_kpis=)` — the call without it is byte-identical, which the golden pins): the `k̂` of the band and category or tag shown, and it minus the `k` shown, through `dashboard._khat_delta`, the one definition of that figure (red when positive — the sizer sized too big — green otherwise, "—" in the default colour when either value is unknown; the difference is rounded to the three decimals shown before its sign and colour are read, so it never prints "-0.000" or a red "+0.000"). As rendered they come from the primary calibration itself (`_calibration_khat_stat(_primary_calibration(sweep))`, against `sweep.primary.k` — the `k` the bar shows for the primary scenario, never the `interval_discount` override — so the card as rendered, the script's redraw of the same view and the interval-discount section's own `k̂ − k` card are one figure even on an unsupported override), never from the filter's data, so they render even when the bar cannot be built; the script (`renderKhatCards`) rewrites them from the `k̂` breakdown's own group for the selection — the category, or the tag when one is chosen — each group carrying its `k̂ − k` per `k` (`_khat_band(..., ks=)`), so a card is the `k̂` chart's highlighted bar whenever that bar is the selected group: grouped by tag or by spread band always, grouped by category whenever no tag is chosen (with a tag chosen, grouping by category highlights the tag's category, which the cards do not show). On a tainted corpus the `k̂` card's label is suffixed " (see caveat in Interval Discount)" and recoloured. The interval-discount section's data at every `k` and cap of the primary band comes from the SAME walk (`dashboard._KdVisitor`, a third visitor, so a size-cap sweep's cells are still simulated once), shipped as the base block's `"kd"`; the script (`renderKd`) redraws the curve and its title, rewrites the "k selected" and "k̂ − k (primary band, pooled)" cards and rebuilds the table at that cap, the `k` shown in bold ("(primary)" still marks the run's own). Both renderers read `SHOWN`, so they describe the scenario on screen (a chunk that fails to load leaves them as drawn), and both return at once on a page without their elements or data (tested under the harness's strict mode). A section that cannot follow the bar renders statically from `sweep.points` at the run's own cap, with a grey line saying why — the bar could not be built (`bar=False`) or the section's own data could not be (`kd_failed`, one WARNING, costing nothing else); a hand-built sweep that records no band follows the bar at its one "not recorded" band, which is its primary, and with no sweep the section is its placeholder. The summary line's closing sentence never claims a reach the page lacks: it is `dashboard._BAR_REACH` when the section follows the bar and `_BAR_REACH_STATIC` (the bar reaches neither that section nor the scenario explorer) when the base block carries no `"kd"` — chosen in `_filter_payload`, so Python's render and the script's fill say the same. It takes the sweep WHOLE rather than its parts precisely so its inputs cannot drift, which is why carrying the census through `BacktestSweep.label_coverage` needed no signature change; `dashboard._label_coverage_html(coverage)` renders it in three distinguishable states — `None` ⇒ "not measured for this run" (an older caller, a hand-built sweep, or the feasibility short-circuit), `total == 0` ⇒ "no eligible markets to census" (the fraction is UNDEFINED there, never `0%`), otherwise this run's own counts and percentages. **A healthy run renders the figure too, not just a silence:** absence of a warning must never be the only signal, since that is indistinguishable from the feature not existing — the exact failure DR-66 closed on the log side. Below the floor it renders a red banner ABOVE the KPI cards, and the `Pooled empirical k̂` card is recoloured and its label SUFFIXED (`… (see caveat above)` — suffixed, never replaced, since `tests/test_dashboard.py` substring-matches that label), so the caveat travels with the number even in a screenshot of the cards alone. The verdict is the CARRIED `below_floor` flag, never re-derived: the log's WARNING and the page's banner must fire on the same condition, and `BACKTEST_OUTCOME_LABEL_WARN_FRACTION` is imported into `dashboard.py` only to PRINT where the floor sits. `generate_dashboard` additionally emits a one-line header notice under the "Period:" line on the same verdict, reading the `sweep` it already receives (no new parameter), because a strike-blind corpus changes WHICH PAIRS EXIST and therefore taints all nine sections, not only the one that renders the census. `_kelly_fraction` gained the same optional `k` (fixing a real latent bug: it previously always priced the config constant, so an `--interval-discount` run's Risk-section Kelly-vs-actual scatter would have silently plotted the wrong model against override-sized trades), threaded through `_section_risk(..., k=...)` (since the page-wide filter, the one `k` `generate_dashboard` resolves for the whole page — see the filter-bar sentence above); `generate_dashboard()` gained `sweep`/`interval_discount` as keyword-ONLY params with defaults, so its pre-existing 4-positional-argument call still works verbatim. **The scenario explorer (2026-09 follow-up on the follow-up) is the backtest-only band × `k` × population sweep this section's `spread_band`/`population` fields were built for.** `run_backtest_sweep(..., spread_band=None, band_sweep=False)` resolves and validates the PRIMARY band before anything is fetched or logged (`config.time_series_spread_band`), then composes `_prepare_candidates()` (fetch through candlesticks — depends on neither the band nor `k`) with `_sweep_from_candidates()`: one time-series `_entries_for_band()` `_find_entry` pass per band (one band unless `band_sweep`) plus one same-title pass, one `_interval_calibration(spread_min=band[0])` per band (`BacktestSweep.calibrations_by_band`, labelled with that band's own floor), and — per band × swept `k` — a standalone `population="all"` simulation plus, when `band_sweep`, standalone `"time_series"`/`"ladder"`/`"cross"` simulations for every non-empty population, a split-half out-of-sample check on the "all" and "time_series" points (`HalfSplit`, split at `BacktestSweep.split_date`, each half simulated alone from the initial balance) and, on every "all" and "time_series" point that traded at least one event, a re-simulation excluding the single most P&L-concentrated event's entries (`SweepPoint.ex_top_event`, `None` on a point with no traded event) — collected as `BacktestSweep.scenarios`, alongside the band/`k`-independent `same_title_point` (simulated once) and the resolved `same_event_ladders`. **On a band sweep only ONE time-series pass scans every pair** (2026-09-23): the no-band band `(0.0, 1.0)` is scanned first, and every other band rescans only the pairs that produced an entry there, through `_entries_for_band`'s private keyword-only `_pairs` (a new list; `candidates.all_pairs` is never mutated). That changes no band's entries, because every band's accepted Mondays are a subset of the no-band band's — the floor only rises (`max(tier, floor) >= tier`), the leg-price-sum ceiling `1 − threshold` therefore only falls, the spread ceiling only drops, and nothing else `_find_entry` tests reads the band — pinned against a full scan per band by `tests/test_backtester.py::TestBandSweepPhaseOneSubset` — including on its own fixture, a pair `_find_entry` hands back with its legs SWAPPED (so the pre-pass must match entered pairs on an UNORDERED ticker pair) and a 16–30-day pair at a 0.25 spread that only a band floor loosening the 0.30 tier could admit — and checked on the DR-73 calibration corpus (all 36 bands identical to a full scan, 330 of its 10,733 pairs rescanned). A single-band run keeps its one full scan. **The populations, and which one the explorer reads.** "All" is every entry at the band, same-title included (`entries_by_band[band] = ts_entries + st_entries` — the run's actual result); "time_series" is every time-series entry at the band — ladders and cross-event together, same-title excluded, simulated alone — and is the population the dashboard's heatmap, fragility banner and equity curve read, because the band and `k` act on time-series pairs only and a same-title result (band- and `k`-independent) would otherwise dilute the comparison; the DR-73 calibration evidence the grid is read against was time-series only (its "All" row was 330 = 299 ladder + 31 cross-event entries). On a corpus with no same-title pair the two are identical cell for cell (measured on the calibration corpus: all 468 time-series cells equal their "all" cells). "Ladders" and "Cross-event" split the time-series entries further, and "Same-title" is simulated once, independent of band and `k`. **The split date is the median_low of the primary band's TIME-SERIES entry dates** (same-title entries left out, so they cannot move where the time-series halves split); because median_low is one of the dates, H1 (strictly before it) is EMPTY whenever AT LEAST half of those entries share the earliest date (one of two is enough) — and, because that ONE date serves every cell, any other band's cell or an "all" point carrying same-title entries has an empty half whenever all of its own entries fall on one side of it (unwarned, but blanked on the page like the rest) — and an empty half's `0.0` return is not a measurement — so `HalfSplit` carries `h1_entries`/`h2_entries` (appended with a `None` default, meaning "not recorded" on a hand-built instance), `_sweep_from_candidates` logs a WARNING naming the split date when it leaves a half of the primary band's time-series entries empty ("split date X leaves H1 empty; the split-half check is not measurable for this window"), and the dashboard renders such a half's return as "—" and leaves the cell out of its split-half Spearman. **`band_sweep` defaults to `False` in `run_backtest_sweep()` itself, but `backtest.py`'s CLI turns it ON by default** (`band_sweep = not args.no_band_sweep`, so `--no-band-sweep` is the opt-out) — a hand-built caller gets the pre-sweep single-band behavior unless it asks for more, while the shipped CLI always computes the explorer's grid unless told not to. `dashboard._section_scenario_explorer(sweep)` is the section this grid feeds (6th of nine on the page, see above), and every population on it is labelled from one `dashboard._POPULATION_LABELS` map: a fragility banner (the cells computed, and, for the time-series population only, the share with a positive return and the split-half Spearman rank correlation of H1-vs-H2 cell returns over the cells whose two halves both had entries — the point of the banner is that the best of that many correlated cells overstates what a reader should expect, "that many" counting the time-series cells the heatmap shows a value in, never the whole grid), a band (row) × `k` (column) heatmap of the time-series population, its title naming that population, with a native `updatemenus` metric toggle (mean per trade — the default — median per trade, total return, H1 return, H2 return, trade count, and empirical `k̂`, which repeats each band's pooled `k̂` from `calibrations_by_band` across every `k` column because it is measured per band and cannot depend on `k`; the same per-band figures also render as a one-row-per-band table under the heatmap), two `<select>`s (band, `k`, preselected to the primary) driving a per-population KPI table (mean AND median return per trade beside win rate and total return; Time-series, with an H1/H2-and-top-event sub-row; Ladders; Cross-event; All — time-series + same-title — with its own sub-row; Same-title, independent of band and `k`) and the selected band's own calibration table, and the time-series equity curve rendered once and restyled in place by a small inline script (`_SCENARIO_EXPLORER_JS`) reading one `<script type="application/json">` data block — a native `updatemenus` dropdown cannot express two independent axes of selection, which is why this part is hand-scripted rather than Plotly-native. **The test seam moved with the split**: seven tests that used to monkeypatch `_prepare_entries` around `run_backtest_sweep` now patch `_prepare_candidates` instead, with no assertion line changed: `TestOutcomeLabelCoverageIsCarried::test_the_sweep_carries_the_census` and `::test_the_infeasible_window_carries_no_census`, `TestRunBacktestSweep::test_preparation_and_calibration_run_once`, and `TestSimulationsAreLabelledWithTheirDiscount`'s `::test_every_swept_point_is_distinguishable`, `::test_the_primary_slot_is_announced`, `::test_the_ladder_flag_reaches_prepare_entries_unresolved` and `::test_the_resolved_ladder_setting_is_logged_with_its_source`. The tests of `run_backtest` itself (`TestOutcomeLabelCoverageIsCarried::test_run_backtest_keeps_its_two_tuple`, `TestSimulationsAreLabelledWithTheirDiscount::test_run_backtest_leaves_the_ladder_flag_to_the_config`) still patch `_prepare_entries` and are untouched, because `run_backtest` still calls it directly. **The 5,000-candle cap no longer truncates a long window (fixed 2026-09-23).** Kalshi rejects a candlestick request spanning more than 5,000 hourly candles (~208 days) with HTTP 400 ("max candlesticks: 5000", observed during the DR-73 calibration fetch), and `historical.fetch_candlesticks` fail-softs every fetch error to `[]`. It used to send each ticker's whole window as ONE request, and `_fetch_candles_parallel` opened every window at the run's global `--start-date`, so on a window longer than ~207 days every ticker closing later than that got no candles and could never enter — visible only in `_fetch_candles_parallel`'s `N of M tickers returned no candles` line — and the explorer, like every other strategy figure, described a truncated population (the default `--start-date 2024-01-01` is far past it). `fetch_candlesticks` now pages any window longer than `config.CANDLESTICK_MAX_CANDLES_PER_REQUEST − 1` candle periods (`historical._candle_request_windows`: requests of at most that many periods, overlapping by one period so the merged result equals one uncapped request whichever way the endpoint treats a window's ends) and merges them ascending by timestamp with the overlap's repeats dropped (`_merge_candle_pages`). Each request is the same retried read-only GET; the window is all-or-nothing — any failed request returns `[]` and caches nothing, never a partial series cached as the whole window — and it is cached as ONE entry tagged with the whole `[open_ts, close_ts]`, so the covers-the-window cache test is unchanged. A window that fits is still one request with the same parameters. `_fetch_candles_parallel` also opens each ticker's request at the later of `--start-date` and that market's own `open_time`, floored to the hour (`backtester._candle_window_open`; a missing or unreadable `open_time` — every cache record written before `_market_to_dict` carried it — keeps `--start-date`), so a market that opened after the window began costs one request for its own life instead of several for months of candles it never had. That is result-neutral: across the 573 cached series of the DR-73 calibration corpus whose request opened before the market did, no candle ends at or before the start of the hour the market opened in; the open is floored because 58 of 1,093 series requested from an off-the-hour `open_time` start later than the opening hour, and a request starting on the boundary gets that hour's candle however the endpoint reads a mid-period `start_ts`. PB7's `dashboard._candle_cap_notice` (a red header line, repeated as the explorer banner's first line) described exactly this defect and was removed with it. **One caveat the fix does not reach:** the DR-73 calibration corpus the explorer is read against (`.git/dr73-calib/candles_ts_all2.json.gz`) was fetched outside the production path, one request per leg from the leg's own `open_time`, so it still lacks candles for the 115 of its 3,704 legs whose own open-to-close-plus-a-day span exceeds the cap (up to 15,527 hours); refetching them through the paged `fetch_candlesticks` would fill it. **Picking a scenario here changes nothing the live bot does:** live trading has no band setting at all, and `k` and the ladder switch stay whatever `config.py` says. Applying a chosen band and `k` live is a separate change that must add live enforcement first.

**The per-trade size cap is a backtest parameter, and the cap sweep is LAZY (2026-09, backtest-only).** `backtester._simulate_at_discount(..., *, size_cap=None, quiet=False, end_date=None)` sizes every candidate at `min(cap, f*)`, where `size_cap=None` resolves `backtester.BUDGET_FRACTION` at CALL time (`_resolve_size_cap`, which accepts any `numbers.Real` in `(0, 1]` — numpy scalars included — returns it as a builtin float, and refuses everything else before any entry is scored: a bool or numpy bool, NaN, or out of range with a range message, a string or `Decimal` with a message naming the type; it reads the module binding, so `TestCheckpointOpeningBalanceSizing`'s monkeypatch of `backtester.BUDGET_FRACTION` still binds); every existing call therefore sizes, stamps and logs exactly as before. The point is stamped with the resolved `size_cap` and `peak_kelly_fraction` — the largest UNCAPPED `f*` over the candidates that passed the Kelly gate (counted before the outcome and premise checks, 0.0 when none passed), which is cap-independent because Pass 1b scores every entry before any sizing. `run_backtest_sweep(cap_sweep=True)` — the CLI's default, `--no-cap-sweep` the opt-out, `False` in the function itself — returns `BacktestSweep.cap_sweep`, a `backtester.CapSweep` over `backtester.SIZE_CAP_SWEEP` (`0.05`…`0.95` and `1.0` = no cap, unioned with the run's own cap) that **simulates nothing during the run**: every eager point, completion line and simulation of the run is identical with the flag on or off (pinned by `tests/test_backtester.py::TestCapSweep::test_off_is_the_default_and_adds_no_simulation`), and each other cap is simulated only when a reader calls `cell(band, k)` / `same_title()`, one (band, `k`) cell at a time, with no memo — keeping every band × `k` × cap × population point would hold gigabytes of trades and curves (the plan ESTIMATED 2.5–5 GB; the per-object figures behind it, re-measured 2026-09-26 with tracemalloc on synthetic records: one `BacktestTrade` retains ~2.3 KB, one equity curve ~27 KB at 370 rows and ~114 KB at 2,463 rows), so the reader is meant to summarise each cell and drop it. The dashboard's page-wide filter is that reader: `dashboard._walk_grid` reads every cell once as the page is built — so a default CLI run's dashboard phase simulates the whole cap grid (reading the golden fixture's full grid took 23.9–25.6 s against its eager run's 2.3–2.4 s, see below) — and `--no-cap-sweep` is the way to skip it. **The reuse rule, and the one correction to its plan:** every cap at or above a point's `peak_kelly_fraction` sizes identically (`min(cap, f*) == f*` for every candidate), and so do that point's split-half and ex-top re-simulations (subsets of its entries, whose peaks are no higher). `CapSweep._by_cap` therefore returns, per population, (1) the eager point ITSELF at the primary cap — this branch comes first, so identity holds even where the peak sits below the cap (the golden fixture's time-series population at `k = 1.0`, peak ~0.106); (2) a `dataclasses.replace(eager, size_cap=cap)` copy sharing its trades, curve, halves and top-event check for every cap at or above the eager peak — **but only when the primary cap is itself at or above that peak**; (3) otherwise a quiet simulation, whose first run at a cap at or above its own peak is then shared by every larger cap. The plan this was built from seeded (2) whenever `cap >= peak`, which is wrong when the eager point was CAPPED below its own peak: on the golden fixture the "all" population at `k = 0.75` has a same-title `f*` of ~0.77, so the 20%-capped eager point does not size as the 80%–100% caps do, and parity with a fresh simulation fails (pinned by `TestCapSweepSeeding::test_a_capped_eager_point_does_not_seed_the_larger_caps` and by `TestCapSweep::test_every_cap_equals_a_fresh_simulation`, which checks every cap of every cell of a narrowed 12-cell golden sweep against a fresh `_simulate_at_discount(..., size_cap=cap)` on trades, equity, halves and ex-top). Reading every cell of the golden fixture's full 36 × 13 grid (measured 2026-09-26, synthetic data, not a production estimate) returned 34,860 cap points: 1,743 eager identities, 19,769 shared copies and 13,348 simulated cap points — `CapSweep.simulated` counts one per cap point, and those points made 41,581 `_simulate_at_discount` calls in all once their split halves and ex-top re-simulations are counted, against 4,479 calls for the eager run — in 23.9–25.6 s against 2.3–2.4 s (the call ratio, ~9.3x, is what tracks the time ratio, not the 13,348). **Everything that forwards to `_simulate_at_discount` forwards only what differs from its defaults** (`_sim_options`: nothing on a default call, a cap equal to `BUDGET_FRACTION` included), so the fixed six-parameter test spies (`golden_band_sweep`'s `simulate_spy` and three `fake_simulate`s) and `TestSweepHelpers::test_ex_top_event_picks_the_largest_summed_profit`'s verbatim kwargs assertion keep passing; `_half_split` takes `*, size_cap=None, quiet=False, end_date=None` and `_ex_top_event` `*, quiet=False, end_date=None`, re-simulating at the point's own `size_cap`. **Every lazy cap simulation is pinned to its eager point's last curve day** (`backtester._curve_end_date`, forwarded as `end_date` to the point, its halves and its ex-top run, and on to `_build_equity_curve(..., end_date=)`): `_build_equity_curve` otherwise ends every curve on today (UTC) as read at simulation time, and a dashboard reads every cell long after the run, so a cell read across UTC midnight gave its simulated caps one row more than the eager point and the copies sharing its curve — neighbouring caps' Sharpe/Sortino over different spans, and the class-scoped parity test failing spuriously (pinned by `TestCapSweepEndDate`, which reads a cell four minutes after a run frozen at 23:58 UTC). The eager points still read the clock as they are simulated, exactly as before, so an eager phase that straddles midnight can still end two cells' (or two populations') curves on different days; every cap of ONE population ends on the same day. A hand-built `CapSweep` whose eager point has no readable `date` column simulates unpinned. The eager seeds are recorded by `_sweep_from_candidates` as each point is simulated — the "all" point BEFORE the `if not band_sweep: continue`, so a `--no-band-sweep` cap sweep still has its seeds and its cells carry "all" alone, without halves — and `_population_subsets` is the ONE split into the time-series / ladder / cross populations, shared by the eager loop and `CapSweep.cell` (an empty population is skipped by both). **Retention (declared, TS-07):** a `CapSweep` keeps `entries_by_band` alive — one small entry dict per band per entry, and every entered pair's two market dicts, bounded by the no-band band's entries since every band's entered PAIRS are a subset of its: 405 (399 time-series + 6 same-title; 60–405 across the 36 bands) on the 2026-09-26 01:22 365-day run's log, so at most ~810 distinct market dicts, a few MB; nothing else from the corpus. `backtester.max_trades_simulated` counts the EAGER points only; a reader that simulates cap cells and shows them beside the post-cutoff verdict must add their counts, since a larger cap can turn an `n < 1` skip into a trade — the dashboard does: its walk's `_MaxTrades` counts every cap point it simulates, and the header passes the larger count to `_corpus_provenance_html(sweep, traded=...)`, so on a run where only a non-default cap traded the page calls the verdict stale while the log's closing line (written before the dashboard is built, over the eager points) still repeats it. `run_backtest_sweep` logs `Per-trade size cap (backtest): 20% (config.BUDGET_FRACTION); size-cap sweep on/off` right after the band line, and a `Size-cap sweep: N caps (...) x B band(s) x K k, ...` summary once the sweep is built; the cap runs' own completion lines — a simulated cap point's AND its split halves' and ex-top re-simulation's, each forwarded `quiet=True` — and their premise WARNINGs are DEBUG (pinned on a checked band-sweep cell by `TestCapSweep::test_a_checked_cell_s_cap_runs_stay_out_of_the_info_log`, and on `_half_split` directly by `TestSizeCap::test_half_split_quiet_sends_both_halves_to_debug`), so reading every cell adds nothing to the INFO log (TS-02). **Live sizing never reads any of this** — `strategy.py` never imports `backtester`, and `config.BUDGET_FRACTION` stays the live (and eager-backtest) cap. **The grid lives in `backtester.py`, not `config.py`,** by the operator's instruction for this change (only the dashboard and backtest code could move) — the one named exception in the constants rule; moving it is a one-line change. `_cap_percent` renders a cap as the exact decimal shift of the `repr` of its builtin float value (`0.55` → `55`, `0.19999999999999998` → `19.999999999999998`; the `float()` first matters because numpy's `float64` reprs as `np.float64(0.35)`, which `Decimal` cannot parse), which is injective; the plan's float-product fallback (`repr(cap * 100)`) is not — `0.003` and the double below it both print `0.3` there — so it was not used.

**The equity curve is a PORTFOLIO VALUE, not a cash balance — open positions are carried at COST (DR-61, fixed 2026-09-16).** `backtester._build_equity_curve` used to accumulate cash alone (`-= total_cost + fees` on the entry date, `+= actual_payoff` on the exit date), so an open position was carried at **ZERO** for its whole holding period and the curve dived on entry and recovered at settlement *whether the trade won or lost*. It now tracks cash PLUS the cost basis of everything still open, which leaves a trade exactly two moves: **`-fees` on the entry date** (cash falls by `total_cost + fees`, the contracts enter at `total_cost`) and **`+(actual_payoff − total_cost)` on the exit date** (the position is written off at cost, the gross receipt credited). The two sum to the trade's own `profit`, so **`total_return`, the final balance and the trade list are identical to within float noise — only the PATH between entry and settlement moves.** ("Identical to the last cent", not literally byte-identical: the position accumulator adds `+cost` on entry days and `−cost` on exit days, so when entries and exits interleave the pair need not cancel to exactly `0.0` in IEEE754. Fuzzed worst case ~4e-12 on a $10,000 balance — under a ULP of the rendered `%.2f`, which is why the parity test uses `pytest.approx(abs=1e-9)` rather than `==`.) That endpoint invariance has one unstated precondition: every trade must settle inside `[start_date, today]`. It does in production — `exit_date` comes from the `settlement_ts` of two already-settled markets — but a trade exiting past the curve's last row would stay carried at cost forever, ending at `initial − fees` where the cash-only curve ended at `initial − cost − fees`. Fees are deliberately NOT capitalised into the carrying value: they buy nothing that can be sold on, so they land on the day they are charged. **Why it mattered:** that series is the sole input to the "Max Drawdown" KPI, the "Drawdown (%)" chart, `_sharpe`/`_sortino` (via the derived `daily_return` column), the per-`k` sweep table's drawdown and Sharpe columns, and the benchmark row that sits in the SAME COLUMN as `^GSPC`'s genuine mark-to-market drawdown — so every one of them was reporting capital DEPLOYMENT as loss. The real 2026-05-01 run is the proof: its **`k = 1.00` point had 3 trades, all 3 profitable and a +4.8% return, and rendered "Max Drawdown −60.0%"**, while its `k = 0.40` point rendered **−100.0% (total ruin) against a final balance of $4,655.87**; the decoded `k = 1.00` trace was flat at $10,000 through 2026-05-24, ONE declining step to $4,001.03 on 2026-05-25 (a pure deployment day), then monotonically non-decreasing. The primary `k = 0.750` card meanwhile showed Sharpe 0.73 and Sortino 2.09 beside a Total Return of −27.0% — the same inversion from the other end. **The accepted cost** is that carrying at cost is not a true daily mark-to-market: an unrealized swing inside the holding period is invisible, so the reported drawdown is REALIZED drawdown and a lower bound on the intraperiod one. Mark-to-market was considered and rejected as materially larger, but **not because the data is missing**: `backtester._fetch_candles_parallel` already fetches each leg's WHOLE hourly series (from the later of `start_date` midnight UTC and the market's own `open_time`, floored to the hour, through one day past its close) and disk-caches it per ticker (a window longer than Kalshi's 5,000-candle-per-request cap is paged — see the scenario-explorer paragraph above). What mark-to-market actually costs is PLUMBING plus a policy — `_find_entry` returns entry-checkpoint prices only, so `candles_by_ticker` lives on the `_Candidates` object only until the last entry pass (it dies with `_prepare_entries` on `run_backtest`'s path, and `_sweep_from_candidates` deletes it before its first simulation), and a per-day series would have to be threaded through `raw_entries` and `_simulate_at_discount` into `_build_equity_curve`, with a rule for the days a leg has no candle. Price it as a data-threading change, never as a new multi-hour fetch. Cost-basis carry is the minimal change that makes each metric mean what its label says; if it is ever revisited, the direction is a strict refinement of this, not a return to cash-only. **DR-03 is untouched and is the constraint any future change here must keep**: the curve still opens one day BEFORE `start_date` at the untouched initial balance, and that row is still what keeps the `cummax` peak at the initial balance rather than at the already-charged day-0 value (see the DR-03 sentences above for the measured before/after). **Every backtest drawdown, Sharpe and Sortino figure ever recorded before this change measured peak deployment and does not reproduce** — including the DR-03/DR-56 fixture log in the paragraph above, which is now marked historical. `_simulate_at_discount`'s own `cash`/`checkpoint_cash` Pass-2 accounting is SIZING and is deliberately untouched: the equity curve is reporting-only, and the two must not be unified.

**Live fetching bypasses the SDK's response models (2026-07 API drift).** The Kalshi API (a) rejects events page sizes above 200 with HTTP 400 (`MARKET_PAGE_SIZE` is now 200 and must stay ≤ 200), and (b) no longer populates the legacy integer fields the pinned SDK's response models type as required: markets lost `yes_ask`/`no_bid`/… (prices now only in `*_dollars` strings) and positions lost `position`/`market_exposure`/… (count now in the `position_fp` string) — so modeled calls that deserialize nested markets, any non-empty positions page, or any order (`Order` lost `yes_price`/`fill_count`/…) raise pydantic `ValidationError`. Therefore `scanner.fetch_open_events_with_markets()`, `scanner.get_held_tickers()`, `trader._position_count()`, and `trader._submit_order()` call the SDK's `*_without_preload_content` raw-response variants and parse the JSON themselves (markets into `scanner.ApiMarket` dataclasses). Order submission is the highest-stakes case: the modeled `create_order` raises AFTER the order is placed, which would shove every real fill into the ambiguous-exception path and unwind successfully filled legs. Two rules: (1) the raw variants do NOT raise on 4xx/5xx, so every raw call must go through `_http.fetch_json_page()`, which re-raises non-2xx as `ApiException` to keep prior error semantics (`api_call_with_retry`'s 429/5xx backoff for market data; order submission stays retry-free per the rule above); (2) don't "simplify" back to the modeled calls (`client.get_events(...)`, `client.get_positions(...)`, `client.create_order(...)`) — they crash on live responses. **Event listings drifted too (2026-08).** They were previously safe through the modeled calls (no nested markets), but the live API now sends `category: null` on some events while the pinned SDK types `EventData.category` as a required `str` — so `get_events` and `get_multivariate_events` raise pydantic `ValidationError` mid-listing. Verified 2026-08-03: this killed a backtest *after* a successful 28-minute fetch. `historical._load_or_build_event_titles` therefore uses `get_events_without_preload_content` / `get_multivariate_events_without_preload_content` through `fetch_json_page` for BOTH bulk listings; the modeled `get_event` (singular) was already unusable — it embeds nested `Market` models — so the per-ticker fallback continues to use a raw GET. There is now no modeled SDK call left in the codebase at all — `verify_auth` (`auth.py`) was the last one, migrated to `get_balance_without_preload_content` for the same reason (BS-10); don't reintroduce a modeled call anywhere. Related drift: the MVE events listing is effectively unbounded (hundreds of thousands of auto-generated collection events) and on prod currently returns zero nested markets regardless of `with_nested_markets` — the MVE pull in `fetch_open_events_with_markets()` bails out after `MVE_MAX_EMPTY_PAGES` (25) consecutive pages that contribute no ACTIVE nested market, and the backtester's title lookup caps its MVE scan at `MVE_TITLE_LOOKUP_MAX_PAGES` (500); without those caps the scan hangs for hours (this is likely why the 2026-07-06 prod run logged nothing after auth). Don't remove the bail-outs. **Every cursor loop in `scanner.py` is bounded twice over (TS-05)** — the two in `fetch_open_events_with_markets()` and the one in `get_held_tickers()`: each keeps a `seen_cursors` set, so a keyset that CYCLES (`A, B, A, B, …`) and therefore never repeats *consecutively* is caught as well as the plain stuck cursor the old `new_cursor == cursor` guard proved; and each stops unconditionally at `SCANNER_MAX_PAGES` (config.py, 5000 — ~1M markets at `MARKET_PAGE_SIZE`, against a ~135k-market prod ingest), which bounds the walk against any cursor pathology whether or not anyone enumerated it. These were the last unbounded scans in the ingest path, and `get_held_tickers` runs before any pairing, so an unbounded walk there means the run never scans at all. The MVE loop's `MVE_MAX_EMPTY_PAGES` productivity bail-out is independent of both and keeps its own counter and reset semantics — don't fold them together. The warning text still contains `cursor did not advance` (now "(repeated)"), which tests pin. **The counter must track productive (active-market) pages, not raw nested-market presence (fixed 2026-08, found live in sandbox).** The bail-out originally reset on the raw `len(markets)` per page regardless of status; on sandbox, the MVE listing serves thousands of pages whose nested markets exist but are all non-active (closed/settled/determined) — those pages kept resetting the counter forever, so the bail-out never fired and a sandbox validation run paged 4,600+ times flat at the same appended-market count. On prod this bug was invisible because prod happens to return zero nested markets per page either way. The fix counts only markets actually appended (status == "active") toward the reset decision. Same drift hits the orderbook: `scanner._fetch_orderbook()` also uses a raw GET, and the book arrives in one of two **matched** key generations tracked by `scanner._ORDERBOOK_SIDE_KEYS`, where each container key owns an ordered tuple of candidate `(yes_key, no_key, unit)` side-key sets and is NEVER read with another container's sets:
- `orderbook_fp` ⇒ `yes_dollars`/`no_dollars` only, **dollar strings**. This is what production actually receives today; its handling must stay behaviour-identical (a prod dry-run should log zero mismatch warnings).
- `orderbook` ⇒ `yes_dollars`/`no_dollars` (**dollars**) if present, else `true`/`false` (**integer cents**). Both shapes come straight from the pinned SDK's `models/orderbook.py`: the `Orderbook` model *requires* `yes_dollars`/`no_dollars` and aliases its legacy int arrays as `"true"`/`"false"` — it never had `yes`/`no`, which the table wrongly assumed until this was corrected (BS-03).

`_fetch_orderbook` picks the container by key *presence* (not truthiness), then takes the first candidate set of that container whose **both** keys exist — a set is only ever taken whole, so a unit can never be inferred from the wrong keys. Cents levels are validated (whole cent in `[1, 99]`) and converted `/100.0` to dollars **before** `_bids_to_ask_levels`, which stays dollars-only by contract; an invalid level is dropped with a WARNING naming the ticker and the raw value, because cents fed straight through that parser yield `1 - 45 = -44` and get silently discarded, turning a full book into a *silent* empty book (BS-12).
