**Cross-strike time-series pairs — the group key must carry the outcome label (DR-01, fixed 2026-09).** A Kalshi daily price family lists dozens of strikes under ONE market title (event `KXBTCD-26SEP1517`: 80 markets, all titled "Bitcoin price on Sep 15, 2026?", the strike only in the subtitle — "$67,500 or above" … "$87,250 or above"). While the time-series key was `normalize_title(pair_key(m))`, every strike of every deadline in such a family landed in ONE group, and the one-best-pair rule (tradeable first, then largest `pB − pA`) then selected the highest earlier strike against the lowest later one: two different questions sized and traded as one cumulative-deadline pair, with no in-between cell at all. Measured on the 2026-09-15 prod dry run's market snapshot, roughly 974 of 979 tier-qualifying combinations in one BTC group were cross-strike; a second read of that snapshot counted 1,021 of 1,027, the two differing in how the group was delimited rather than in the finding — both are over 99%, and it is the proportion, not the count, that the fix rests on. And **four of the six trades that dry run selected were cross-strike** (a fifth was the same-series pair DR-02 names); the six together were ~98% of the cash balance. No money was lost only because the scheduler daemon has never been started. **The fix is one helper on both paths.** `scanner.time_series_group_key(combined_title, subtitle)` returns `"<normalize_title(pair_key)> | <normalized subtitle>"` (the title alone when the subtitle is empty, `""` when the title itself normalizes away), and BOTH `scanner.find_time_series_pairs` and `backtester._group_by_normalized_title` call it — the latter, since SS-1, through `backtester._ts_group_key`, the backtester's one definition of the key, which `_index_eligible_keys` also reads to decide which records are materialized for grouping at all — pinned by `tests/test_strategy.py::TestTimeSeriesKellyParity::test_ast_both_finders_group_through_the_shared_key_helper` (a two-link chain on the backtester side), because the backtester previously keyed the same wrong way and therefore reproduced the defect rather than detecting it. Three details are load-bearing. (1) **The subtitle is normalized by `scanner._normalize_subtitle`, NOT `normalize_title`.** That pass strips any bare `20xx` token as a year, and a subtitle is exactly where a bare 4-digit strike ("2050 or above") lives — running it over an outcome label would merge every strike in 2000–2099 into one key, reintroducing DR-01 through the subtitle. `_normalize_subtitle` strips only the unambiguous DATE shapes — `_EXPLICIT_DATE_PATTERNS`, every entry taken BY INDEX from `_DATE_PATTERNS` so a copy can never drift: the month-name / abbreviated-month / numeric / ISO shapes that carry a year, the deadline-anchored month + day shape from detail (2), and the pre-existing bare abbreviated month + day — plus trailing punctuation, so "$80,000 by June 30" and "$80,000 by July 31" are one strike at two deadlines and "Donald Trump" and "Donald Trump." are one option label. Whitespace and punctuation are trimmed in ONE `rstrip`, because a stripped date leaves a space in FRONT of any trailing punctuation ("$80,000 by June 30." → "$80,000 by .") and trimming them in sequence left that space in the key, silently dropping a genuine same-strike pair. (2) **`_DATE_PATTERNS` gained ONE shape — deadline preposition + full month name + day ("by June 30") — placed AHEAD of the `by/before/until/through/after <Month>` clause** — that clause's optional day group only matches when a year follows, so from behind it consumed "by June" and stranded the day, leaving "30" against "31" to split two titles that differ only in their deadline. **It is anchored to the preposition deliberately.** A bare `<Month> <day>` also erases the date from SNAPSHOT titles ("... on June 30"), which would put two snapshot markets of one family into a single time-series group — the shape DR-67 now screens at pair formation on the legs' wording, with the backtester's counter as defence in depth. Anchored, the pattern fires only where that clause already fired and stranded a day, so no title that used to stay apart is merged; the ABBREVIATED spelling ("by Oct 1") is deliberately left behind the clause and still splits, unchanged. `tests/test_scanner.py::TestNormalizeTitle` is the gate CLAUDE.md names for any `_DATE_PATTERNS` change, and it now pins both directions ("by June 30"/"by July 31" merge; "on June 30"/"on July 1" do not). (3) **Non-string subtitles read as absent, never raise** (a `MagicMock` auto-attribute, `None` from an old cache record) — the same fail-safe-by-type rule `leg_sides` and `strategy._depth_levels` follow. **Cache caveat:** backtest day slices written before the 2026-08 subtitle fix carry `subtitle=None`, so a backtest over them still groups time-series candidates by title alone and stays strike-blind; refreshing them is the deletion procedure in the subtitle-drift gotcha below. **And the guard is only as good as the field.** `subtitle` is normalized in at ingest from `subtitle`/`yes_sub_title` (see that same gotcha, which records that the field comes and goes): if BOTH ever go missing again, every market keys with an empty outcome label and the time-series key silently reverts to the pre-fix, strike-blind `normalize_title(pair_key(m))` — with real money on it and no warning of any kind; and when only ONE leg of a genuine pair carries the label, the two legs key apart and the pair is silently dropped (a missed trade, also unlogged). **The live and backtest paths have DIFFERENT symptoms, and the long-standing counter covers only one of them.** `scanner.find_time_series_pairs` — the LIVE finder, and the counter's only home — logs `Distinct normalized title+outcome keys with >= 1 market`; a count collapsing toward the market count, or a time-series pair count dropping to zero, is the symptom to check first on the live path. That is the ONE-leg-labelled case, where the two legs key APART and keys SPLIT. It says nothing about a backtest, for two independent reasons: the backtester never calls that function (it calls `_group_by_normalized_title` directly), and when BOTH legs are unlabelled the keys MERGE instead, so the count moves the other way entirely. `backtester._log_outcome_label_coverage` is the backtest-path signal (DR-66; since SS-1 `_prepare_candidates` runs its two halves, `_OutcomeLabelTally` and `_report_outcome_label_coverage`, directly — see below): an INFO census of `subtitle` and `event_title` coverage over the eligible markets on every run, escalating to a WARNING naming the consequence and the cache-refresh remedy when subtitle coverage falls below `config.BACKTEST_OUTCOME_LABEL_WARN_FRACTION`. It exists because a backtest over a pre-2026-08-14 cache reported pair counts, trades, a return figure and an empirical `k̂` recommendation for `TIME_SERIES_INTERVAL_PROB_DISCOUNT` that all described the strike-blind strategy, while being indistinguishable in its own output from a run on a good cache — measured on the assembled 2026-05-01 cache: 2,344,886 records, 98.14% with a blank subtitle and 87.89% with a blank event title, against ZERO blank subtitles on either post-fix assembled cache. **The DASHBOARD says so too, and that half is DR-66b.** The log alone was not enough: `backtest.py`'s own closing line points the operator at the HTML, and a dashboard generated from a run whose census had logged 0.00% coverage contained none of the strings "subtitle", "coverage", "strike-blind" or "cache" anywhere in its 140 KB, rendering a bare `Pooled empirical k̂` card — a recommendation for the real-money `TIME_SERIES_INTERVAL_PROB_DISCOUNT` — with no caveat of any kind. (A reviewer was fooled by exactly this and reported a pooled `k̂` of 1.114 upstream as a finding about the strategy. "The number is arithmetically correct for the corpus it was handed" is the same defence that would have excused the log silence, and it is not enough.) `_log_outcome_label_coverage` therefore RETURNS the frozen `OutcomeLabelCoverage(total, with_subtitle, with_event_title, subtitle_fraction, event_title_fraction, below_floor, cumulative_markets, snapshot_markets, unknown_deadline_markets)` it just logged — same single pass, same position in `_prepare_candidates` (the first half of `_prepare_entries`), same log wording byte-for-byte (since SS-1 `_prepare_candidates` uses the census's two halves directly: it feeds an `_OutcomeLabelTally` inside its first walk over the corpus and logs through `_report_outcome_label_coverage` at that same position; `_log_outcome_label_coverage` is their one-shot composition and still accepts any iterable, walked exactly once) — which rides out on `_Candidates.label_coverage` onto `BacktestSweep.label_coverage` and into `_section_interval_discount` (`_prepare_entries` also returns it as element 1 of its pair, which `run_backtest` discards). Four things are load-bearing. (1) **A RETURN VALUE, not an optional sink.** An `emit`-style callback (the idiom `_fetch_archive_day` already uses) would have cost zero test edits, but a caller can FORGET it and silently lose the caveat — reproducing DR-66's own failure mode inside the mechanism built to close it. The tuple cannot be omitted; the defaulted `BacktestSweep` field is the one remaining place an omission is possible, which is why both production constructions pass it together — true only since DR-71 (fixed 2026-09) made the three phrasing fields (`cumulative_markets`/`snapshot_markets`/`unknown_deadline_markets`) REQUIRED, with no default: before it, a construction that forgot them silently defaulted to `0, 0, 0`, a second, unmentioned place an omission was possible — and a bad one, since it made the page's strongest sentence ("could not have produced a time-series trade at all") render as if measured. A forgotten phrasing keyword is now a `TypeError` at construction, the same "cannot be forgotten" reasoning this return value already used. The nine constructions that pass all three explicitly (counted 2026-09-23 by grepping `OutcomeLabelCoverage(` over `kalshi_betting/` and `tests/`): two in `backtester.py` (the empty-corpus branch passes `0, 0, 0` because that IS a measurement, and the main branch reads them off the census); one in `tests/test_backtester.py` (`TestOutcomeLabelCoverageIsCarried::test_the_sweep_carries_the_census`); five in `tests/test_dashboard.py` (the module-level `_coverage` and `_scn_coverage` helpers, `TestOutcomeLabelCoverageIsRendered::test_the_verdict_is_carried_not_recomputed` and `::test_an_empty_corpus_is_not_reported_as_zero_percent`, and `TestDeadlinePhrasingIsRendered._coverage`, whose `**base` dict carries all three); and one in `tests/dashboard_golden.py` (`_coverage`). A tenth, `tests/test_backtester.py::TestOutcomeLabelCoverageIsCarried::test_the_phrasing_fields_are_required`, deliberately omits one of them per case to prove the `TypeError`. (2) **ONE measurement, so the page and the log can never disagree** — including the threshold, which is evaluated once and carried as `below_floor`. Measuring over `_interval_calibration`'s entries instead was rejected for the sharper reason that it would measure the SURVIVORS of the very grouping under suspicion, and would vanish entirely on a same-title-only run — exactly the run where the lost same-title discriminator (TS-11) is the whole exposure. (3) **The rendered figure describes the ELIGIBLE-MARKET CORPUS, not the `k̂` population**, which is a far smaller, differently-selected subset (entered, binarily settled, non-premise-violating time-series candidates). Phrase it as "N of M eligible markets", never as "of the pairs behind `k̂`", and never put it inside the calibration table, whose rows are per-bucket counts of the `k̂` population. The banner's consequence sentence is the one claim that legitimately spans both. (4) **`subtitle_fraction`/`event_title_fraction` are `None`, not `0.0`, on an empty corpus** (undefined rather than zero), and the carrier holds scalars only (counts, fractions and one verdict) and no reference to any market dict — enriching it with sample tickers or a per-category breakdown would pin every record alive past `_prepare_candidates`' release of the corpus and the groupable subset and undo TS-07's residency work. Every number rendered is this run's own count or the configured floor; the historical figures above stay in `config.py` and this file, never in an emitted string (TS-07). **Concentration moved too:** the one-best-pair-per-group rule used to bound a whole daily family to ONE pair, and now bounds one STRIKE to one pair, so an N-strike family can contribute up to N pairs — all on the same underlying over the same window, and `strategy.select_portfolio` dedups tickers only, with no per-event or per-family cap. **Sibling (DR-17): the outcome label is now visible.** `scanner.display_title` appends " — <subtitle>" unless the subtitle IS the label already (`market_title` falls back to it when the title is empty), which is what the prod Excel "Market A"/"Market B" cells render; and `main.print_pairs_table` gained its own "Outcome A"/"Outcome B" columns, because `main._truncate` cuts the two title cells at 40 characters and a real `display_title` is well past that — so appending alone would have left the four cross-strike rows of that dry run looking identical, which is exactly why nobody caught them in review. `main._print_portfolio` — the log of the trades actually SELECTED — names both tickers for the same reason: it prints `canonical_title[:55]`, and a time-series canonical title now ends with the outcome label, which that cut drops. **A strike spelled inside the TITLE is a different case and is NOT addressed here** (DR-26, deliberately out of this plan): a `$80k`-shaped strike survives `normalize_title`, so two such markets already key apart, but a bare 4-digit one is eaten by the standalone-year pattern and nothing added here guards that.

**Identical wording across two events of ONE series is two fixtures, not one question (DR-02, DR-54, fixed 2026-09).** A Kalshi event ticker is a series prefix followed by an instance stamp (`KXNPBRFI-26SEP160500FUKORI`, `KXTTELITEMATCH-26SEP101835KKAMOL`, `KXMVECROSSCATEGORY-SHARD1-S6471E4699E9`), so two events of ONE series are two instances of one recurring fixture — two ball games, two 15-minute price windows, two combos of different games. The date lives only in the event ticker, the event sub-title, the close time and the rules text — none of which any grouping key reads — so the `event_ticker` inequality guard both finders already had *passes* on exactly this shape. Identical wording across them is the same question asked about two DIFFERENT events, and neither strategy's premise survives it: the 95% co-resolution prior is simply false (the 2026-09-15 dry run's NPB pair — `KXNPBRFI-26SEP160500FUKORI-Y` and `…150500FUKORI-Y`, identical title, subtitle and event title, closes 24 h apart — was quoted 0.97 and 0.01, and `strategy._kelly_p` priced it at the flat `SAME_TITLE_CO_RESOLVE_PROB` 0.95, sizing 16 contracts a side for $12.39 against a reported "$3.61 profit floor" whose true expected value, on the two markets' own prices with independent games, is negative), and there is no cumulative-deadline premise either, because the deadline lives entirely outside the wording. 71 of that run's 75 same-title rows were this shape. **The rule is fixture identity, not close time** — for the ONE-SERIES rule; since DR-74 a close gate sits ON TOP of it for CROSS-series same-title pairs (see the next gotcha), as a second condition, never as a replacement. The sweep's adversary critic measured 117,801 identical-wording NON-combo pairs off the settled cache: they co-resolved 96.3–98.2% of the time in EVERY close-gap band, so no threshold isolates the bad ones (that figure pools both sides of this rule — the same-series pairs it now rejects and the different-series ones it keeps — and it is an UNCONDITIONAL rate, whereas the finder only ever prices a pair the market has already diverged 5% on; treat it as the reason a close-gap gate was rejected, never as a co-resolution estimate for what survives) — while identical-wording COMBO pairs closing within one hour of each other still settled differently 13.8% of the time (an admitted example closed 96 seconds apart and settled yes vs no). A one-hour gate was therefore rejected: it would drop pairs that co-resolve almost always and still admit two matches of one table-tennis player (`KXTTELITEMATCH-26SEP101835KKAMOL` vs `…101810MOLJMI`, about half an hour apart, settled yes and no) and two consecutive 15-minute gas windows. (That rejection was of a one-hour gate as a REPLACEMENT for the series rule, measured on a different corpus. DR-74's one-hour gate is an ADDITIONAL condition on pairs the series rule already admits, and on its 365-day corpus identical-wording non-combo pairs settle the same way 68.2% of the time same-series and 65.6% cross-series, while cross-series pairs settle the same way 99.2% of the time at a 0 s close gap against 60.7% beyond 1 min — so on the cross-series side proximity DOES separate the two, which is all DR-74 relies on.) **Both finders carry it, and that is the whole point.** `scanner.find_same_title_pairs` skips a candidate when `_same_series(m_outer, m_inner)` (its group key already guarantees identical wording), counting the skips and reporting them once as `Same-title candidates skipped as two instances of one event series (identical wording, different fixture): N`; `scanner.find_time_series_pairs` skips when `_identical_wording(mA, mB) and _same_series(mA, mB)`, and since M10 counts those skips too (`Time-series candidates skipped as two instances of one event series (...): N`), as `backtester._extract_pairs` does on both of its branches — see the DR-67 observability paragraph for the exact lines. Every one of these lines is silent at zero, and counting moves no control flow: M10's pair lists were compared byte for byte with its parent's on seven synthetic corpora (one combo-heavy, six seeded fuzz), ladders on and off, through both `_extract_pairs` branches and both live finders — 28 cases, every one identical. Gating only the same-title finder would have RELABELLED the trade rather than removed it: the same two NPB tickers clear the time-series filter too (`pB − pA` = 0.96, gap 1 day), and `main._dedup_pairs` only ever dropped that copy because a same-title copy existed — 39 of that run's 75 same-title rows would have come back as time-series pairs. `backtester._extract_pairs` mirrors both branches through `_same_series_dicts` / `_identical_wording_dicts`, which derive the prefix from the same `scanner.event_series`, so live and backtest can never disagree about what a series is; all of it is pinned by AST in `tests/test_strategy.py::TestTimeSeriesKellyParity::test_ast_both_finders_apply_the_one_series_rule` and `::test_ast_the_series_prefix_has_one_definition`. **`event_series` fails CLOSED.** An empty, hyphen-leading or non-string event ticker yields `""`, and `_same_series` reads an unknown prefix on either side as the SAME series — a pair whose fixture identity cannot be read must not be priced on the co-resolution prior. **What still pairs under THIS rule:** two DIFFERENT series asking one question (the shape the same-title strategy was built for) — and, since DR-74, only when their two markets also close within 1 hour of each other; and any pair of ONE series whose deadline is IN the wording — `KXSOLD-26SEP14` / `KXSOLD-26SEP18` titled "Solana price on Sep 14/18, 2026?", or the FIDE Oct/Nov markets from the same run, make `_identical_wording` false. Note both of those named examples are nonetheless refused as TIME-SERIES pairs by DR-67, on the separate ground that "price on \<date\>" and "top-10 in \<month\>" are snapshot wordings; `tests/test_scanner.py::TestOneEventSeriesIsTwoFixtures` pins both verdicts on the same fixture, so a reader can see which rule decides what. **This rule decides fixture identity only, in both directions.** It does not certify that a dated one-series pair is genuinely CUMULATIVE — "price on <date>" is exactly the SNAPSHOT shape the time-series paragraph above names (SOL ≥ $180 on Sep 14 does not imply SOL ≥ $180 on Sep 18; top-10 in October does not imply top-10 in November). **DR-67 now answers that separately**: the cumulative-deadline rule refuses both of those examples on their wording, so the `KXSOLD` Sep 14/18 pair and the FIDE Oct/Nov pair named above as "what still pairs" are eligible under THIS rule and refused by that one. And different series is a NECESSARY condition for a same-title pair, never a sufficient one: the same prop listed for two different games in two different leagues shares its wording too (`KXBRASILEIROB1HTOTAL-26SEP10VINGGO-1` and `KXUCL1HTOTAL-26SEP10MUNSBH-1`, both "Over 0.5 1H goals scored", settled no and yes), and only the `event_title` component of the same-title key separates them — which live ingest populates but pre-`event_title` cache records leave `""`, so the backtest path grouped them where the live path usually did not. Since DR-74 they are refused on BOTH paths unless the two games close within 1 hour of each other: the close gate reads no `event_title`, so it does not depend on the cache carrying one. **The accepted cost:** a genuine cumulative-deadline pair of ONE series whose deadline appears *only* in the event ticker — never in the title, subtitle or event title — is dropped along with the recurring fixtures, because at that point nothing in the data the finders read separates the two. That is deliberate, not an oversight: the same-title finder had exactly this ambiguity before and resolved it the other way, with real money on it. **What stops entirely:** every same-title pair between two MVE combo EVENTS. Kalshi lists combos under several series prefixes, all beginning `KXMVE` — a census of `backtest_cache/event_titles.json` (3,996,906 keys, 2,444 distinct prefixes, measured 2026-09-16) found exactly four: `KXMVECROSSCATEGORY` (2,960,840), `KXMVESPORTSMULTIGAMEEXTENDED` (906,157), `KXMVECROSSCATEGORY0` (94,230) and `KXMVENBASINGLEGAME` (61), with no `KXMV*` prefix that is not also `KXMVE*` — so `scanner.event_series` collapses the whole family onto one series (`config.MVE_SERIES_FAMILY_PREFIX = "KXMVE"`, DR-55) and the one-series rule treats any two combo tickets as two instances of one recurring fixture. Reading the literal prefix DID refuse two combos listed under ONE of those prefixes; what it could not see was a pair spanning TWO of them, which read as two different series and was priced on the 0.95 co-resolution prior. That exposure is measured, not assumed: in `backtest_cache/live_days/2026-09-14.json.gz` (9,009,087 records, 8,939,229 of them `KXMVE*`), 10,643 distinct `(event_title, title, subtitle)` wordings appear under TWO literal `KXMVE` prefixes — every one of them `KXMVECROSSCATEGORY` x `KXMVECROSSCATEGORY0` — forming 26,072 cross-prefix market pairs of which 17,829 co-resolved and **8,243 settled DIFFERENTLY, a 68.4% co-resolution rate against the 0.95 prior**. That 68.4% carries the SAME caveat as the 96.3–98.2% above and for the same reason: it is an UNCONDITIONAL rate over every record in each cross-prefix wording class, with no price, liquidity or activity filter, whereas the finder only ever prices a pair the market has already diverged 5% on — treat it as the measured reason the family rule exists, never as a co-resolution estimate for the pairs that would actually have been traded. A FAMILY PREFIX rather than an enumerated allowlist, because enumerating the combo series would BE an allowlist and the codebase's own prior belief ("every combo sits under `KXMVECROSSCATEGORY`") was already such an allowlist with one of the four entries — the artefact that was measurably wrong here — and it rots every time Kalshi adds a combo series (`KXMVENBASINGLEGAME`, 61 keys, appears in no day slice at all yet). Over-collapsing is the safe direction since `_same_series` only ever REFUSES pairs, so its worst case is a missed trade between two genuinely independent `KXMVE*` series, or a still-eligible runner-up promoted where the refused pair was a group's best candidate, never a trade priced on a false premise. The family literal is deliberately narrow — prefix containment among ordinary series is common (`KXART` vs `KXARTISTSTREAMS` are unrelated), so it must never be widened to `KXMV` or to a generic containment rule. That is a pairing rule, not an ingest exclusion, and it is the intended reading of DR-54; if combo duplicates are ever wanted the discriminator is `mve_selected_legs`, which ingest does not carry today. **Backtest impact:** the `Potential pairs` count drops (fewer, correct pairs), `_can_ever_enter` is untouched so there is no `SETTLED_PREFILTER_CACHE_TAG` bump, and any later profiling baseline must be taken after this change. **Skipping is not only subtractive.** Both finders keep ONE best pair per group, so removing a group's top candidate PROMOTES the runner-up: a group whose widest gap was a one-series look-alike can now yield a time-series pair on tickers the previous code never proposed at all (pinned by `tests/test_scanner.py::TestOneEventSeriesIsTwoFixtures::test_skipping_the_widest_candidate_promotes_the_next_one`). That is intended — the promoted pair is the one the group actually supports — but it means this rule can ADD a trade as well as remove one, so a diff of two runs' pairs tables will not be purely a deletion. **Test fixtures moved with the rule:** every synthetic same-title pair a FINDER actually forms was re-pinned onto distinct series — the one-prefix `EVT-A`/`EVT-B` and `EV-A`/`EV-B` fixtures in `tests/test_scanner.py` and `tests/test_backtester.py` became `EVA-1`/`EVB-1` or `EA-1`/`EB-1`, and the eight event tickers in `tests/test_main.py`'s `_build_events` became eight distinct series. Fixtures that only exercise GROUPING, or that build a `CandidatePair` directly without going through a finder (`_st_candidate`, the enrichment and reachability pairs), keep their one-prefix tickers because no finder ever reads them. The synthetic TIME-SERIES fixtures also kept their one-series tickers and had their titles dated instead (`_ts_pair_markets`, both `TestTimeSeriesBestPairPerGroup` groups and `TestTimeSeriesGrouping::test_pricier_earlier_contract_is_not_a_candidate` now spell each market's own close date, the shape a real two-deadline family has) — that last one matters because without the dated titles the one-series rule, not the directional price filter, was what made its assertion pass. No assertion changed in either group.

**Identical wording on two DIFFERENT series is one question only when both markets close at the same moment (DR-74, fixed 2026-09).** The one-series rule above is NECESSARY for a same-title pair, never sufficient, and this is the shape it misses. The 365-day backtest (`--start-date 2025-09-24`) made 21 trades, all same-title, for +4.7%, max drawdown −48%, and −37.5% without its best event. Rebuilt with the backtester's own functions, 17 of them paired a **men's** and a **women's** college basketball game between the same two schools — `KXNCAAMBGAME-26JAN13WIUEIU-WIU` vs `KXNCAAWBGAME-26JAN13WIUEIU-WIU`, identical title ("Western Illinois at Eastern Illinois Winner?"), identical subtitle and event title, on two different series, so the one-series rule let them through. One more paired the Champions League and La Liga "Atletico vs Barcelona" matches, 10 days apart; only the 3 Brent/copper weekly-vs-monthly trades asked one question. The 95% co-resolution prior (`SAME_TITLE_CO_RESOLVE_PROB`) is false for such pairs, and **the exposure is live**: the live finder uses the same key and the same series rule, and the 2026-09-23 prod dry run (`kalshi_arb.log:13954-13961`) formed 4 same-title pairs whose two legs close on different days — among them "India A Women vs Australia A Women", 2026-10-06 vs 2026-09-27, and "Texas vs Tennessee", 2026-09-27 vs 2026-09-28. None traded only because the price filter's fee check found them untradeable (each A leg was quoted 95% on both outcomes — an empty book — so their fixture identity is inferred from the dates, not the quotes). Each of the four closes at least 16 h apart.

**The rule.** A same-title candidate is refused when its two markets close more than `config.SAME_TITLE_MAX_CLOSE_GAP_SECONDS` (`60 * 60` — 1 hour) apart, or when their closes cannot be compared: it fails CLOSED like `_same_series`, on anything that is not a datetime and on one naive close beside one aware close. `scanner.closes_apart(close_a, close_b)` is the one definition, with a strict `>` (a gap of exactly the bound is admitted); `scanner._closes_apart(mA, mB)` is the live reader (reading `.close_time` by TYPE, so a `MagicMock`'s auto-attribute fails closed), called in `find_same_title_pairs` immediately AFTER the `_same_series` check, so the two older counts do not move and each candidate lands on exactly one line. `backtester._extract_pairs`' same-title branch mirrors it after `_same_series_dicts`: `backtester._comparable_closes_dicts` parses both cached close strings (`None` when either is absent or unparseable, or one naive and one aware) and the branch calls `closes_apart` on the result. Pinned by `tests/test_strategy.py::TestTimeSeriesKellyParity::test_ast_both_paths_apply_the_same_title_close_gap` (which also asserts the ABSENCE of the gate from `find_time_series_pairs`) and `::test_ast_the_close_gap_has_one_definition`, and by value in `tests/test_scanner.py::TestSameTitleCloseGap` / `::TestClosesApart` and `tests/test_backtester.py::TestSameTitleCloseGapBacktest`. The gate reads `scanner`'s binding of the constant, so a test narrows it by patching `scanner.SAME_TITLE_MAX_CLOSE_GAP_SECONDS`, never `config.*` — the `TIME_SERIES_SAME_EVENT_LADDERS` precedent — and that one patch narrows the backtest's gate too, since there is no second copy. It also moves the bound both refusal lines PRINT: each formats it through `scanner.close_gap_bound_text()`, which reads that same binding at call time (whole minutes as minutes, anything else in seconds, so a patched bound is never truncated into a false one), and `backtester` imports no copy of the constant at all — pinned by `tests/test_backtester.py::TestSameTitleCloseGapBacktest::test_the_gate_is_the_scanner_definition`, which asserts the backtest line under a patched bound, by `tests/test_scanner.py::TestClosesApart::test_the_bound_text_never_misstates_the_bound` / `::test_the_bound_text_reads_the_gates_binding_at_call_time`, and by the AST pin `tests/test_strategy.py::TestTimeSeriesKellyParity::test_ast_the_close_gap_has_one_definition`, which asserts both line sites call the helper.

**Why close time, and why 1 hour.** Checked against live and archived Kalshi payloads on 2026-09-25. The event's `product_metadata.competition` label separates both bad shapes ("College Basketball (M)" vs "(W)"; "Champions League" vs "La Liga") but is absent for commodities, and over the 1,906 backtest candidates both sides carried it on 1,377, neither on 459 and one side on 70. The event `sub_title` is identical for a same-day M/W doubleheader. A series' `settlement_sources` cannot tell UCL from La Liga and costs an extra `/series/{t}` call. Series titles always differ and tags never do. A market's `rules_primary` is free text the backtest cache lacks, and so are `occurrence_datetime`/`expected_expiration_time`. A series-ticker allowlist rots (DR-55). `close_time` is carried by both paths already. A read-only replay of the real `_extract_pairs` → `_find_entry` → `_simulate_at_discount` over the 365-day corpus's 1,906 same-title candidates (competitions from the public `/events/{t}` for 1,101 events; the regression critic independently reproduced the first and "close" rows):

| Rule | Candidate pairs | Settled the same way | Entries | Trades | Return | Max DD |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Today (no extra gate) | 1,906 | 65.6% | 25 | 21 | +4.7% | −48.0% |
| Same competition (refuse when both named and different) | 635 | 79.1% | 6 | 3 | +4.8% | −1.9% |
| Same competition, strict (one-sided name refused) | 565 | 80.2% | 6 | 3 | +4.8% | −1.9% |
| Same event `sub_title` only | 358 | 91.6% | 22 | 18 | +30.2% | −41.5% |
| **Close within 1 min / 15 min / 1 h** | 245 | 99.2% | 6 | **3** | +4.8% | −1.9% |
| Close within 3 h | 280 | 95.0% | 18 | 14 | +0.9% | −41.8% |
| Close within 24 h | 313 | 92.3% | 24 | 20 | +2.3% | −48.0% |

Both rules leave the same 3 trades — the Brent/copper weekly-vs-monthly pairs, 6 entries of which cash funds 3. Every candidate closed either at the identical instant or at least 1.17 h (4,217 s) apart: 245 pairs at a 0 s gap, 243 of which settled the same way (including 226/226 commodity and 15/15 financial; the other 2 are the Black Phone 2 anomaly below), and 1,661 pairs at least 1.17 h apart, ~60.7% of which settled the same way — the nearest of those a men's/women's UMES at NCCU pair, which settled the same way, and the nearest that settled DIFFERENTLY 2.17 h apart. What the close rule refuses and the competition rule keeps — 102 `KXMLBGAME`/`KXMLBSTGAME` pairs (39% settled alike) and every unlabelled category (e.g. Mentions) — makes no trade difference here: of the 1,661 refused pairs only 196 were ever open together (183 at a Monday checkpoint), 0 of the 153 mention pairs and 4 of the 140 MLB-vs-spring-training pairs. **1 hour is the operator's choice (2026-09-25)**, and gives exactly the 1-minute and 15-minute result on this corpus; what it gives up is margin (see the residuals). The population is the backtest's: `event_title` is blank on ~95% of its eligible records.

**Where it holds on both paths: games and same-instant listings.** Live `close_time` is the SCHEDULED close — for a game, tip-off plus a fixed number of days, which keeps a doubleheader's two games hours apart — and the backtest's is the REALIZED close. Checked on archived payloads: WIU at EIU M/W 2.5 h apart scheduled (01:30 vs 23:00) and 2.7 h realized; TN Tech at TN St. M/W 2.5 h scheduled; UCL/La Liga days apart on both; Brent weekly/monthly both 21:00Z, identical on both.

**`find_time_series_pairs` is deliberately NOT gated.** Since DR-67 identical wording cannot form a time-series pair (it states the same deadline spans, or none), so a same-title-only gate cannot relabel the trade — the concern that put the one-series rule on BOTH finders (see the dedup note under Key Patterns). A close gate there would also refuse every genuine cross-event deadline pair, whose closes differ by design. The relabel guard is pinned on both paths, undated ("Q") and dated ("by Dec 31, 2026"): `tests/test_scanner.py::TestDeadlineGuardFinders::test_identical_wording_closing_apart_forms_no_pair_of_either_type` (no pair from either finder, nothing out of `main._dedup_pairs`) and `tests/test_backtester.py::TestRunBacktestCrossTypeDedup::test_identical_wording_closing_apart_forms_no_pair_of_either_type` / `::test_nothing_reaches_the_dedup_when_the_closes_differ`.

**Observability.** `find_same_title_pairs` logs one silent-at-zero INFO line, `Same-title candidates refused because the two markets close more than 60 minutes apart (two different games or instants, not one question listed twice): N`, with its own prefix and neither "one event series" nor "same event ticker", which other tests pin as ABSENT. `_extract_pairs` logs that line VERBATIM (pinned by `TestSameTitleCloseGapBacktest::test_the_close_gap_line_is_the_live_finders_verbatim`, on a fixture of two non-combo series that reaches the gate) plus a BACKTEST-ONLY `Same-title candidate pairs refused because a market's close_time cannot be read (fail closed; the live finder drops such markets before grouping): N`. The split exists because the two paths do not see the same population: the cache's closes are all aware (9,156,436 of 9,156,436 end in Z), but a same-title group is not close-filtered — `_can_ever_enter` keeps a market whose close it cannot parse — while the live finder drops a missing `close_time` before grouping. **The two close-gap lines are verbatim twins for READABLE closes only.** A pair whose closes cannot be COMPARED — one naive beside one aware — is refused on both paths, but lands on different lines: the backtest counts it on its unreadable-close line (`_comparable_closes_dicts` returns `None`), while the live finder, whose gate has ONE line, counts it on the close-gap line, whose wording ("close more than 60 minutes apart") is then not literally true of it. That is unreachable today — every Kalshi close string ends in `Z`, so `_market_from_dict` parses them all aware, and every cached close does too — and it fails closed either way; it would first show up on a payload drift that mixed the two formats. `TestOneSeriesAndSameEventCounts::test_every_candidate_is_returned_or_counted_exactly_once` sums both new counts into its same-title identity. `main._no_pairs_msg` names the condition ("same-title: ≥5% price diff on two different series closing within 60 minutes", the bound read from the constant and rendered by `scanner.close_gap_bound_text`). With the same-title branch now refusing an undatable pair, the pipeline no longer hands `_fetch_candles_parallel` a market without a readable close, so BS-07's branch there is defence in depth, kept covered by a direct test (`TestRunBacktestMalformedTimestamps::test_the_candle_fetch_still_resolves_an_unparseable_close_to_nothing`).

**Not changed:** `main._dedup_pairs` and the pairs table, `reporter` and its pinned columns, `trader`, `historical` (no new data), `dashboard` and its goldens, `backtest.py`, `_can_ever_enter`, `SETTLED_PREFILTER_CACHE_TAG` and the assembled caches — the rule acts at PAIR time, so every cache stays valid (the DR-02 precedent). **Test fixtures moved with the rule** — the exact gate broke 18 tests, each fixed rather than loosened: `test_dated_identical_wording_is_same_title_only` (`tests/test_scanner.py::TestDeadlineGuardFinders` and `tests/test_backtester.py::TestRunBacktestCrossTypeDedup`) keeps its name and now holds an ALIGNED fixture closing at one instant — its positive control `mB3` shares that instant too, so the control still differs from the refused case in WORDING alone — while the differing-close expectation, NO pair of either type, moved to the new `test_identical_wording_closing_apart_forms_no_pair_of_either_type` on both paths (the relabel guard above), and `TestRunBacktestCrossTypeDedup` keeps `_MARKETS` as its differing-close fixture beside a new `_ALIGNED_MARKETS`; `TestTimeSeriesTieredThreshold::test_same_title_pairs_ignore_deadline_gap`, which pinned that a pair closing 20 days apart "is still a candidate", was inverted deliberately (now `test_same_title_pairs_ignore_the_deadline_tier_but_not_the_close_gap`); `TestActiveTickerRelease` was redesigned (TY now closes with TX and carries a second-Monday candle at 0.60, because aligning its close alone brought TY/TZ inside the 30-day cap); `_p5_corpus` gives each same-title family's two legs one close, the later of the two draws, with its rng draws unchanged; `TestOneSeriesAndSameEventCounts` gained the two prefixes and its `_fuzz` copies its source's close onto every other re-listed duplicate (keyed on the index, no extra draw); a few same-title fixtures (`TestExtractPairsCanonHandling`, `TestOldCacheToleranceMissingTickAndSubtitleFields::test_extract_pairs_same_title_handles_missing_fields`) gained a shared close locally — never in the shared `_md`; and SS-1's `_ss1_corpus` (read by `TestGroupableSubset`, `TestPrepareCandidatesOverASettledCorpus` and `TestPrefilterLinesSayItRanDuringAssembly`) broke no test but silently lost almost all of its same-title coverage — its fill's 50-day random close spread left 4/5/4 same-title pairs for seeds 0/1/2, against 126/117/93 before the gate — so the fill records whose titles can only ever pair as same-title (`_SS1_SAME_TITLE_ONLY_TITLES`) now close at one shared instant, restoring 78/65/50 while the Rain/Snow fill keeps its random closes (so the gate's refusals stay covered too), the rng stream and every time-series pair unchanged, and `TestGroupableSubset::test_prepare_candidates_equals_grouping_the_whole_eligible_list` now asserts at least 40 same-title pairs so that coverage cannot silently drain again.

**Known residuals of DR-74.** Recorded here, not fixed.
- **Placeholder closes (live only).** Futures that can be decided early carry a family-wide PLACEHOLDER as their live `close_time` — all 30 live `KXOSCAR*` events close at 2027-12-31T15:00Z, and 20 of 34 `KXEARNINGSMENTION*` events at 2027-03-31T14:00Z — so two such listings pass live at a 0 s gap while the backtest separates them by realized close. The one cross-series identical-wording pair on the 2026-09-23 live snapshot is an example: `KXOSCARVIS-27-TIE` vs `KXOSCARMAH-27-TIE`, where the Visual Effects event carries the Makeup event's wording, so these are two different questions and only the price filter stops them today. The competition rule would not catch this class either (no label). The backtest's version of the class: 89 co-open non-game pairs refused only on realized close, 71 of which settled the same way (e.g. `KXNCAAFPLAYOFF` vs `KXNCAAPLAYOFF`: 54 pairs, 43 alike). Not a regression — these pairs were admitted before DR-74 too. An admitted cross-series pair is at most 3,600 s apart by construction, so no dry run can reveal a placeholder close; review each admitted cross-series pair by hand for a family-wide shared close.
- **Simultaneous or close tip-offs (live).** A M/W pair whose scheduled closes fall within an hour of each other passes live: two games at the same time (neutral-site tournaments), or back-to-back games less than an hour apart. The M/W pairs checked were 2.5 h apart.
- **1-hour margin (backtest).** The nearest refused candidate on the 365-day corpus closed 1.17 h from its partner (UMES at NCCU, M/W), ~10 minutes beyond the bound, so a M/W pair whose games END within an hour of each other would be admitted there. None did on this corpus. A 15-minute bound would have left 1 h of margin at no cost in trades; the operator chose 1 hour.
- **Realized-close drift (backtest only).** 4 duplicate listings, `KXTOPSONGTOTALGOL-2..5` vs `KXTOPSONGTOTALGOLDEN-2..5`, are refused because their realized closes drifted 1.5 h apart (all settled "no"; none entered). The album-vs-song-chart pairs are rightly refused (their sibling family settled differently on 6 of 13).
- **Settlement anomaly.** `KXRTBLACKPHONE2` vs `KXBLACKPHONE2` have identical rules and close, yet one settled two months later with the opposite result (2 pairs). No rule can see it.
- **Missed trades.** A genuine listing on two series with different scheduled closes is refused (fail closed) — a missed trade, never a false one.
- **Promotion.** Both finders keep ONE best pair per group, so refusing a group's best candidate PROMOTES its runner-up (pinned by `TestSameTitleCloseGap::test_refusing_the_widest_candidate_promotes_the_runner_up`), and a pairs-table diff is not a pure deletion.
- **Baselines.** Pre-DR-74 same-title baselines (21 trades / +4.7% on the 365-day run) are not comparable.

**`subtitle` is not dependable on a market payload — ingest normalizes `yes_sub_title` into it (2026-08 drift).** Live-verified 2026-08-14: the live `/markets` endpoint, `/events?with_nested_markets=true`, and the signed `/historical/markets` archive ALL served payloads with no `subtitle` key; on 2026-09-15 a verification read observed payloads carrying `subtitle` AND `yes_sub_title`, so the fallback is defence against a field that comes and goes, not the only source. The distinguishing outcome label arrives as `yes_sub_title` (e.g. the candidate names under one shared `title` of "Who will the next Pope be?"), with `no_sub_title` carrying the negated phrasing. This is not cosmetic: `subtitle` is the only intra-title discriminator in BOTH grouping keys — the same-title key `(event_title, title, subtitle)` used by `scanner.find_same_title_pairs()` and `backtester._group_by_exact_title()`, and (since DR-01) the time-series key built by `scanner.time_series_group_key()` for `scanner.find_time_series_pairs()` and `backtester._group_by_normalized_title()`. With every market parsing to `subtitle=""` the key collapsed to `(event_title, title)`, so two **different** outcomes sharing one question title on different event tickers grouped as the *same* contract and were traded under the 95% co-resolution assumption — a real-money false positive (reproduced offline: two distinct papal candidates paired with `tradeable=True`). The fix is at ingest only: `scanner._market_from_dict()` and `historical._market_to_dict()` read `subtitle` with a fallback to `yes_sub_title`, so the `ApiMarket.subtitle` attribute and the `"subtitle"` cache key keep their existing names and shape and no downstream code changed. Use `yes_sub_title`, never `no_sub_title` — the negated phrasing would make the grouping key asymmetric between the YES and NO framings of one outcome. Display fallbacks (`scanner.market_title`, `backtester._pair_key`, the backtester's trade labels) read the now-populated subtitle and silently improve: a title-less market falls back to its outcome label instead of the opaque ticker. **Cache caveat:** backtest day slices written before this fix carry `subtitle=None` and therefore reproduce the pre-fix weakened grouping — this is backtest fidelity only, with no live-money exposure — and `--no-cache` does NOT refresh them (day slices are deliberately reused unconditionally, see the sharded-fetch gotcha). A genuine refresh requires deleting `backtest_cache/archive_days/` and `backtest_cache/live_days/` **and** re-running with `--no-cache` (equivalently, also deleting the assembled `backtest_cache/settled_markets_*` cache files — the streamed `.jsonl.gz` and any legacy `.json`, since a legacy file is served whenever no `.jsonl.gz` exists at all) — on a default run the assembled per-`start_date` cache is loaded first and returns before the day slices are ever consulted, so deleting the slices alone changes nothing.

**Backtest fetch uses direct signed GETs — the pinned SDK has NO historical_api module.** `historical.build_historical_client()` returns a plain prod `KalshiClient`; `/historical/cutoff`, `/historical/markets`, `/historical/markets/{ticker}/candlesticks`, and `/events/{ticker}` are reached via `historical._signed_raw_get()` (KalshiAuth signs timestamp+method+path, the SDK's rest client executes) composed with `_http.fetch_json_page()` for status checks and `api_call_with_retry` for backoff. Hard-won facts about the archive endpoint (all live-verified 2026-07-13): (1) it IGNORES every server-side time filter (`min_settled_ts`, `max_settled_ts`, etc. all return the identical page); (2) its page size is hard-capped at 1000 (larger → HTTP 400); (3) it is ordered by **`created_time` DESC, ticker DESC — NOT by settlement time** (settlements interleave hours out of order, and long-lived markets created before a date can settle long after it); (4) its pagination cursor is a urlsafe-base64 **protobuf of the keyset position** (field 1 = Timestamp of `created_time`, field 2 = ticker), which `historical._encode_archive_cursor()` reproduces byte-for-byte — this is what makes sharded parallel fetching possible. The live `/markets` endpoint honors `min_settled_ts` AND `max_settled_ts` server-side (page size also capped at 1000), but does NOT serve pre-cutoff settlements — once markets migrate into the archive they disappear from `/markets` entirely. Candlestick candles now arrive with the dollar string in `yes_ask.close` (the old `close_dollars` field is gone); the parser prefers `close_dollars` when present so both formats read as dollars, never cents.

**`fetch_all_settled_markets()` is sharded, parallel, and incrementally cached — don't serialize it again.** A sequential walk of the settled-market history is tens of millions of records at 1000/page (live-measured 2026-07-13: 95+ minutes for 8,300 pages without finishing). The fetch therefore: (a) runtime-verifies archive cursor synthesis (`_archive_cursor_synthesis_ok` re-encodes page 1's last record and byte-compares against the server cursor); (b) fetches one slice per UTC **created-day** via synthesized cursors across `SETTLED_FETCH_MAX_WORKERS` (config.py, 8) threads, persisting each completed slice to `backtest_cache/archive_days/<day>.json.gz`; (c) walks a sequential **tail** below `created_time == start_date` — the archive is created-ordered, so markets created before start_date can still settle in-window; the old single walk dropped ~28k such markets (verified live) as a page-alignment artifact, and the tail catches them deterministically (its output is a verified strict superset of the old: zero markets lost, extras are all genuinely in-window settlements); (d) sweeps the post-cutoff range as parallel per-**settled-day** windows via `min/max_settled_ts`, persisting fully-elapsed days to `backtest_cache/live_days/`. Any runtime-assumption failure raises `_ShardedFetchUnsupported` and falls back to the original sequential walks (which rely only on documented behavior) — never delete the fallbacks or the runtime checks. **The whole phase is skipped when `start_ts >= cutoff_ts`** (an INFO line says so): the archive holds only pre-cutoff settlements, so no record can satisfy such a window, and proving that by running the cursor-synthesis probe and the tail walk cost ~18 seconds and ~50,000 parsed records on every post-cutoff run. Note the skip also silences the synthesis probe on that path, so such a run reports on cursor synthesis at all (TS-25). **Both archive walks (`_fetch_archive_tail` and the `_fetch_archive_sequential` fallback) stop on a barren-page counter, not on a settlement comparison.** No exact stop rule exists on a created-ordered walk: a market created arbitrarily early can settle in-window, so no page proves deeper pages are unproductive. Both walks used to stop at the first page whose `page[0]` (the newest-*created* record on it, whose settlement time says nothing about the rest of the page) settled before `start_ts` — since most Kalshi markets are short-lived, that fired after one or two pages and silently dropped exactly the long-lived in-window settlers the tail exists to collect (BS-02). They now stop after `ARCHIVE_MAX_BARREN_PAGES` (config.py, 50) **consecutive** pages containing zero in-window settlements — the same productivity bail-out idiom as `EVENT_TITLE_LISTING_MAX_BARREN_PAGES` / `MVE_MAX_EMPTY_PAGES`, ~50k records of created-time depth past the last productive page. The tail walk also has no chunked `emit` sink — the day workers stream into slice files and the live frontier streams through a prefilter-applying sink into an anonymous spool file (below) — and, unlike the two sequential fallbacks, it applies no prefilter either, so it holds its whole unfiltered result in memory; that is why it carries `ARCHIVE_TAIL_MAX_RECORDS` (config.py, 500k ≈ 1.3 GB) as well. The two caps COMPOSE, whichever binds first: the page cap alone allows 2000 × 1000 × ~`BACKTEST_RECORD_BYTES_ESTIMATE` ≈ 5 GB, the same OOM shape the sharded fetch was rewritten to avoid (BS-15, TS-15). The tail walk (`_fetch_archive_tail`) additionally has a hard backstop, `ARCHIVE_TAIL_MAX_PAGES` (config.py, 2000): a single long-dated in-window settlement resets the barren counter, so without a ceiling the tail could crawl most of created-time history one serial request at a time on every run; hitting it logs a WARNING naming how many pages were walked. Productivity is judged on ANY in-window settlement on the page, deliberately **independent** of the binary-result and created-time keep filters, so a run of all-voided (or, in the tail, day-slice-skipped) pages can't spuriously trip the counter. Don't reintroduce a `page[0]`-based stop, and don't judge productivity on "records kept". The test oracle used to replay the same flawed rule, so the parity tests structurally could not catch this; it is now `_expected_in_window`, a direct predicate over the fixture records (see `tests/test_historical.py`). The cursor-synthesis probe issues a real request, so **any** unexpected exception from it (auth, outage, unparseable body) is wrapped into `_ShardedFetchUnsupported` too — it used to escape the phase and kill the run with no warning logged. Both pool loops call `pool.shutdown(wait=False, cancel_futures=True)` when a worker raises: the executor's `__exit__` would otherwise drain every still-queued day (hundreds of them — hours) before the fallback is even reached. **Progress-line labels are load-bearing for diagnosis:** the sharded and sequential paths emit the same line shape, so they are suffixed `[sharded]`/`[windowed]` vs `[sequential]` — with identical labels (the pre-2026-08-03 state) a log cannot tell you which path a run actually took, and a live run was misdiagnosed exactly that way. Each completed slice also logs `N/M complete` with a rate and ETA; don't drop it, it's the only signal of how far a multi-hour fetch has actually got. Slice-reuse rules are load-bearing: archive day files are stamped with the `cutoff_ts` they were fetched under and are ONLY reused while it matches the current cutoff (a cutoff advance migrates markets archive-ward and OFF the live endpoint, so stale slices would silently miss them); live day files cover immutable fully-elapsed UTC days and are reused unconditionally; the frontier (current) day is always refetched and never persisted. Day slices are deliberately reused even under `--no-cache` (they cannot go stale by construction) — that's what makes refresh runs cheap, and an interrupted fetch resumes at day granularity. **Slice records are never accumulated in memory — not across days, and not within one day.** Each pool worker (`_fetch_and_store_archive_day` / `_fetch_and_store_live_window`) persists its own slice and returns only a count, and each phase then hands back a LAZY `_DaySliceStream` from `_assemble_day_slices()` — nothing is read at that point, and every later walk re-reads the slices off disk one record at a time, newest day first, which is the ordering the caller's first-wins ticker dedup depends on (the live phase returns a `_RecordChain` of its frontier spool followed by its stream, the old `frontier + [...]` order). Don't "simplify" by having workers return their records and flattening them: that is exactly what OOM'd a full-history run (2.7 GB RSS only 17% through, growing superlinearly with GC pressure), and it also drags gzip/JSON serialization back onto the main thread where it can't overlap. **The ASSEMBLED corpus is never accumulated either (SS-1 Commit C, 2026-09-24).** A 7-day window's past days held 18,061,549 fetched records of which 7,260,952 passed the backtester's prefilter, at 3,926 B/record (~28 GB), and the old assembly held them in a ticker-keyed `selected` dict, then a list copy, then one whole-list `json.dumps` — the run reached 19.9 GB of physical footprint in that silent phase and thrashed until killed. One generator, `_assembled_records`, now reproduces the old `_merge` EXACTLY — sources in the old order (archive day slices newest-first then the tail, both below `cutoff_ts`; then live: frontier, then live day slices newest-first, unbounded above), `settle >= start_ts`, the prefilter BEFORE the first-wins ticker dedup, a fresh `seen` set per walk — and `fetch_all_settled_markets` walks it TWICE: walk A (`_count_assembled`) counts what the `Historical endpoint`/`Live endpoint` lines report (the same kept numbers, the same line order — since M9 each line also carries the endpoint's settled records and what the prefilter and the dedup removed, see the prefilter gotcha) and collects the event tickers for `_load_or_build_event_titles` (unchanged); walk B patches `event_title` exactly as before (only when `titles` is truthy) and streams each record straight into the assembled cache through `_DayStreamWriter` (new `write_record`), which is returned as a `SettledCorpus`. Walk B must reproduce walk A — same count and the same order-sensitive `_assembly_identity` over ticker and event_ticker — or `SettledCorpusError` is raised and the writer aborts, publishing nothing (a slice rewritten or pruned between the walks would otherwise write titles resolved for different records). Only tickers are held by a walk, never a record; what stays resident is the tail list and a sequential fallback's list (both as before), the walk's ticker set, the event-ticker set and the title map — the last three scale with the corpus (per-ticker strings, not records). Measured on the 2026-09-24 7-day run (3,989,846 event tickers): the fresh fetch peaked at 2,782 MiB before grouping against 895 MiB for the cache-hit rerun of the same window, and the watchdog placed the fresh run's peak (2,957 MB footprint) at the event-title merge and save, not in either walk — the cost DR-51 removed (see the event-title gotcha). On a synthetic 4,000,000-ticker request, building the set took the process's peak RSS from 51 to 673 MiB, and a steady-state (post-DR-51) title call added 145 MiB on top — mostly the returned title map, one entry per requested ticker (a 4,000,000-entry dict filled one key at a time measures 117 MiB by sys.getsizeof). Pinned by `tests/test_historical.py::TestStreamedAssemblyParity` (the old merge kept verbatim as an oracle, on list and stream sources and on the real phases with each fallback) and `::TestNothingIsMaterialized` (weakref-tracked: zero disk-parsed records alive while titles are resolved, at most the record in hand plus the open file's meta line at every cache write and every corpus step). Don't turn the corpus, a phase's stream or the assembly back into a list anywhere in the pipeline (`list(...)`, indexing, `frontier + [...]`), and don't give a mid-walk read failure a fallback or a skip. A worker no longer holds a whole day either (BS-15): at 2026-08 volumes one UTC day is ~4.4–4.8M markets, and buffering a day per worker sawtoothed RSS 3.4 → 4.3 → 7.89 GB on a 16 GB host. `_fetch_archive_day` / `_fetch_live_window` therefore take an optional `emit` callback; with it set they flush and drop their buffer every `SETTLED_FETCH_CHUNK_RECORDS` (config.py, 50k — the real bound is that plus the page in flight, since flushes land on page boundaries) and return a COUNT instead of a list. No production caller passes `emit=None` any more (SS-1, fixed 2026-09-24): past-day workers stream into their slice file, and the frontier (never-persisted, partial) day streams through `historical._extend_kept`, a sink that applies the caller's `keep` predicate (the backtester's `_can_ever_enter` prefilter) to each batch as its pages arrive and writes the survivors into a `historical._FrontierSpool`, so no frontier record — rejected or kept — is resident beyond the one batch in flight. The frontier used to be accumulated UNFILTERED and filtered only after the whole pool drained — the same records in the same order, but at a peak that grows through the UTC day (1.9M records at 07:30 UTC on 2026-09-24; a full day is up to 9.2M) whatever the window length, and that alone peaked a 7-day backtest at ~5.5 GiB RSS. Don't restore the post-hoc filter. The sink runs on the frontier's worker thread (so `keep` must be thread-safe — `_can_ever_enter` is pure) and the main thread seals and reads the spool only after the frontier future's `result()`. `frontier_future.result()` is LOAD-BEARING: it is the only place a frontier-window failure (an exhausted retry, a non-transient error, `keep` raising on the worker, a spool write failing) surfaces, and the pool's exit waits for the worker either way, so deleting it returns a silently SHORT frontier instead of raising — pinned by `TestFrontierStreamsThroughKeep`'s failure tests. Its return value, discarded until M9, is how many records the frontier window emitted BEFORE `keep`, so the gap to the spool's length is exactly what the prefilter dropped there; that count joins the assembly's `AssemblyCounts` only once the windowed path has succeeded, so a frontier the live fallback discards is never counted beside the sequential walk that refetches its day. **Why a spool and not a list (SS-1 review, fixed 2026-09-24):** filtering as pages arrive still left the keep-passing frontier resident as a list, and its size depends on the WEEKDAY, not on the window's length — `_can_ever_enter` admits every market open over a Monday checkpoint on/after `start_date` that closes at least a day later, so a frontier captured the day after a Monday can be mostly eligible (7,190,452 of the 9,176,306 records settled on Tuesday 2026-09-22 passed `_can_ever_enter(m, 2026-09-17)`, ~28 GB at 3,926 B/record on a 16 GB host). The spool is an ANONYMOUS temporary file (`tempfile.TemporaryFile` in `CACHE_DIR`, unlinked on creation): no later run can find it or reuse it as a complete day, and the OS reclaims it on close or on any process death, so a crash cannot leave a stale one. It carries the day slices' line framing and no meta block, is walked once per assembly walk (a newer walk invalidates a suspended one, which raises if resumed, since they share one file position; a complete walk that ends on a different count raises `SettledCorpusError`), and is closed by `fetch_all_settled_markets` in a `finally` (via `_RecordChain.close()` / `_close_records`) once its two walks are done, on success and failure alike — so the phase's result cannot be walked after the fetch returns. Don't turn it back into a list. Pinned by `tests/test_historical.py::TestFrontierSpool` (no kept frontier record alive at any page request or after the phase; exact old order on every walk; anonymous; closed on success, on a failed assembly and on a failed phase; lifecycle misuse loud). The list-returning `emit=None` form is retained for direct callers; the sequential fallbacks (`_fetch_archive_sequential`/`_fetch_live_sequential`) are separate walks with no sink that apply the same `keep` per record as each page arrives — exact, because the merge re-applies the prefilter before its first-wins dedup, and their progress lines still count records before `keep`; since M9 each also adds what it dropped to the assembly's counts, since the assembly never sees those records — but still hold their whole keep-passing result; a live-phase fallback first closes the partial frontier spool (freeing its disk), since the sequential sweep refetches today anyway. **Two slice formats exist on disk and both must stay readable:** the legacy single gzipped JSON document `{"meta":…, "markets":[…]}` written by `_day_store_save` (hundreds of MB already cached; refetching them costs hours), and the streamed `"jsonl-v1"` format written by `_DayStreamWriter` — meta line first, then one compact market dict per line, in fetch order (server newest-settled-first, which is what preserves the newest-first assembly / first-wins dedup contract). Both writers are kept: the streaming one for the workers, `_day_store_save` for callers already holding a full list, so neither format rots. `_DayStreamWriter` writes to `<path>.tmp` and only `commit()` renames it into place, same atomicity contract as `_day_store_save` — a killed worker leaves no visible slice. `_day_store_iter` is the ONE reader of the format (since SS-1's Commit C; `_day_store_load` is its all-or-nothing list form, `_DaySliceStream` and `SettledCorpus` its streaming users) and routes on file CONTENT (a first line that parses to a dict *without* a `"markets"` key is JSONL), **never on whether a whole-file parse succeeds**: an empty JSONL day is a lone meta line that parses fine as a document, so a parse-success test would misclassify every empty day. It parses JSONL one line at a time and yields each record, applying the optional `keep` predicate per record when a caller passes one (the prescan passes `_discard_all`; since M9 the phases pass none to their day-slice streams — see the prefilter gotcha), so a multi-million-record day is never materialized; the meta block is checked before the first record is yielded, and a fault found later — a malformed line, a truncated or damaged stream, including a damaged deflate block (`zlib.error`, which is NOT an `OSError` and used to escape the prescan as a crash), a non-object record or meta block — raises `_SliceUnreadable` AT the fault, after the records before it were yielded. So `_day_store_load` stays all-or-nothing (the WHOLE slice fails, `None` → refetch), while a streaming walk treats it as fatal: a slice that goes bad after its phase verified or wrote it raises `SettledCorpusError` naming the day and the remedy (re-run; the prescan then refetches it) — deliberately NOT the sequential fallback the old eager assembly took, which would hold the whole range in memory and could not take back records already yielded, and never a silently short corpus. An exception out of `keep` itself is not a file fault and propagates on both formats (before Commit C, a `keep` that raised `OSError`, `EOFError` or `ValueError` — the classes the old `except` caught — read as "slice unreadable" on the JSONL path only; any other exception already propagated). The prescan deliberately loads-and-discards to test slice validity (it passes `_discard_all`, so a streamed slice is fully parsed but nothing is retained), so reused days are decoded three times — the prescan and both assembly walks — which is minutes against a multi-hour fetch, and the alternative (partial-JSON meta peeking) is far more fragile. **The backtester's candlestick fetch (`backtester._fetch_candles_parallel`) is parallel for the same reasons**, across `CANDLESTICK_FETCH_MAX_WORKERS` (config.py, 8) threads, and uses the identical `pool.shutdown(wait=False, cancel_futures=True)` tear-down. It is safe because candle cache paths are strictly **per ticker**, so two workers can never target one path — `_save_json_cache` writes atomically (tmp+replace, so a truncated file can never become visible), but its tmp name is derived from the destination, so a shared cache path would still collide between workers; never introduce a fetch whose cache path is shared across workers. Reads go through `_load_json_cache`, which treats a corrupt/truncated file as a cache MISS (WARNING + refetch) rather than raising out of a worker. Worker exceptions must **propagate** out of `run_backtest`: `fetch_candlesticks` already fail-softs network errors to `[]` internally, so anything that still raises is a real defect (e.g. a market that should have been prefiltered out — the run_backtest tests rely on exactly that KeyError surfacing) and must not be degraded into "this ticker has no prices".

**The old fetch's multi-minute stalls were client-side memory/GC, not API throttling.** The 2026-07-13 live run showed growing pauses (up to ~14 minutes between page-100 log lines, growing roughly linearly with markets held) with ZERO retry warnings logged and consistent ~0.1–0.3s server latency — the cause was accumulating millions of RAW market payloads (30+ fields plus nested `mve_selected_legs` lists) in one Python list. The rewritten fetch normalizes every record to the compact `_market_to_dict()` form at keep-time and streams day slices to disk. Don't "optimize" by deferring normalization or accumulating raw pages again. The same failure recurred on 2026-08-03 in compact form — the phase functions still held every completed slice in a `results` dict *and* flattened it into a second list — so slices are now dropped as soon as they're persisted and re-read at assembly (see the sharded-fetch gotcha above). If a future run stalls again, check RSS before suspecting the API. **The failure class then moved one phase later, into the grouping/pairing step (TS-07).** `_prepare_candidates` (the first half of both `_prepare_entries` and `run_backtest_sweep`) held the whole eligible window as ONE list and built two group maps and two candidate-pair lists over it — until SS-1 (below), which stopped materializing the records that cannot group. Two measurements exist, both on a 16 GB host and both of the same 1,089,165-record FIVE-DAY window (the cheapest run the tool supports) — and they attribute the cost to two DIFFERENT places, so never quote one as if it explained the other. (1) **2026-09-12, cache MISS:** the fetch assembled the streamed day slices into that list and the recorded peak for the run is **6.05 GiB RSS**; that run predates the `_log_rss()` lines, so its own log carries no RSS line to confirm it — the figure was observed outside the log. (2) **2026-09-13, cache HIT:** `fetch_all_settled_markets` returned at its `use_cache` early return (`Loaded 1089165 settled markets from cache`) and assembled NOTHING; the memory went into `historical._load_json_cache`, which does `json.loads(path.read_text())` over the 1.43 GB assembled `settled_markets_*.json` — logged `Peak RSS before grouping: 3816 MiB` and `Peak RSS after pair extraction: 3977 MiB`, i.e. grouping and pair extraction added only 161 MiB to the high-water mark on that path. **That cache-load cost was a real, separate finding** (`_load_json_cache` materializes the whole file as one `str` and then every record from it); **SS-1's Commit C added the streaming reader** — new assembled caches are `settled_markets_*.jsonl.gz`, validated and then walked record by record through `historical.SettledCorpus` — so only a hit on a LEGACY `settled_markets_*.json` still pays it, exactly as before (those files keep loading, whole, as a list). The window is instrumented rather than invisible: `backtester._log_rss()` logs peak RSS in MiB (normalizing `ru_maxrss`, which is BYTES on macOS and KILOBYTES on Linux) at `before grouping` and `after pair extraction`. The `before grouping` line is logged **before** the RAM-budget warning so the two read in causal order: the RSS line is what the fetch or cache load — and, since SS-1, the two passes over the corpus — has ALREADY cost, and the warning (fired when the GROUPABLE subset exceeds `BACKTEST_MARKETS_RAM_WARN`; it counted every eligible market before SS-1) names that subset's share of it and what grouping adds on top. Two rules on that warning: it must not claim to precede the spend — by the time it fires every record it counts is already resident, which is why it counts the groupable records (what stays resident from the warning onward: the corpus is released just before it) and not the eligible ones — and it must print **only the running process's own numbers**, never a figure from another run, because nothing will ever prompt a reader to refresh a measurement baked into a string emitted on every run; the historical figures above belong in `config.py`'s comment and in this file, and nowhere else. `BACKTEST_RECORD_BYTES_ESTIMATE` (2,700) is the PARSED footprint of one cached record — what the list of dicts costs in memory — not its size on disk and not a peak-RSS predictor, since peak also covers whatever transients are live alongside it (the decoded JSON string on the cache-hit path; the group maps and pair lists during pairing). Two measurements on the real 2026-09-07 assembled cache bracket it: ~3.0 KB/record allocated when 195,038 sampled records are decoded under `tracemalloc`, and no more than ~2.4 KB/record resident once that 1.43 GB decoded string is subtracted from the 3,816 MiB pre-grouping peak — so it stays a round estimate between the two. The corpus (`markets`) and the first pass's key index are `del`'d right after the second pass, before any group map exists, and `ts_groups`, `same_groups` and `groupable` in ONE statement as soon as the pair lists are built, before the candlestick pool spawns its threads: nothing below reads any of them, and dropping the group maps ALONE frees no record dicts at all, because `groupable` still references every one of them. That release lowers RESIDENCY across grouping, the candlestick fetch and the `_find_entry` sweep; it does **not** lower peak RSS, which is a high-water mark already reached by that point. All of it is advisory — nothing is capped or dropped. **Known residual of that re-pointing:** when the corpus is a list (since Commit C, only on a LEGACY `.json` cache hit), every eligible record WAS resident up to its release and the `before grouping` RSS line includes all of them, yet the warning does not count them — so a run whose eligible count is far above the threshold but whose groupable count is not (the 7-day window below: 7,260,952 eligible, 184,255 groupable) gets no warning for the list that set its peak; the eligible count is still on the `Markets to analyze` and `Groupable subset` lines, and the cost on the RSS line. **SS-1 (2026-09-24): only the groupable eligible records are grouped, and `_prepare_candidates` builds no eligible list of its own.** A 7-day window (`--start-date 2026-09-17`) found 7,260,952 of 18,061,549 fetched past-day records eligible (99.75% of them MVE combos) at a measured 3,926 B/record under `tracemalloc` — ~28 GB for the eligible list alone — yet both grouping functions drop every single-member group, and only 184,255 of those records share a time-series OR same-title key with another eligible record; the rest can never appear in any pair of either type. `_prepare_candidates` therefore walks the corpus TWICE, treating it as any re-iterable of market dicts. **The corpus itself stopped being a list in SS-1's Commit C.** Until then `fetch_all_settled_markets` returned ONE list, and because the prefilter is applied during its assembly that list IS the eligible set — resident through both walks until `del markets`, and on that window alone ~28 GB. It now returns a `historical.SettledCorpus` (see the sharded-fetch gotcha) that streams the assembled `settled_markets_*.jsonl.gz` cache on every walk, fresh dicts each time, and holds its record count as `len()`: a walk that cannot read the file raises `SettledCorpusError` (a `RuntimeError`), and so does a complete walk that yields a different count. The passes needed no change for it — they were written for any corpus that re-iterates identically — and `tests/test_backtester.py::TestPrepareCandidatesOverASettledCorpus` pins that a real streamed corpus prepares exactly what the list does, in exactly two walks. Only a hit on a LEGACY `.json` cache still hands over a list. What the commit before it removed is the second, filtered copy the old code built and the grouping over every eligible record. Pass 1, `_index_eligible_keys`, counts every record (the `Markets to analyze` figure), applies `_can_ever_enter`, feeds the outcome-label census (an `_OutcomeLabelTally`, so the census costs no pass of its own), and appends `hash()` of each eligible record's two keys to `array('q')` buffers with validity flags — about 27 B per eligible record while the walk runs (187 MiB at 7,260,952 eligible records, measured on synthetic keys, 2026-09-24), plus a transient of about 74 B per record (515 MiB at that count) while `_shared_key_mask`'s `np.unique` computes the keep flags: small beside the records it stands in for, but not "a few bytes" — through `_ts_group_key`/`_st_group_key`, the SAME single definitions `_group_by_normalized_title`/`_group_by_exact_title` now group on (AST-pinned in `tests/test_strategy.py::TestTimeSeriesKellyParity::test_ast_both_finders_group_through_the_shared_key_helper`, which is a two-link chain since this change). `_shared_key_mask` (numpy) flags a hash that occurs at least twice among VALID keys of its kind. **Hashing is safe in exactly the direction that matters:** equal keys always hash equal, so a shared key is always kept, and a 64-bit collision can only keep an EXTRA record, which the exact grouping then drops as the singleton it is — results never move (pinned by forcing every hash to collide). Pass 2, `_materialize_groupable`, re-applies `_can_ever_enter` and keeps the flagged records BY POSITION among eligible records — the normalize_title-dominated keys are never recomputed for the records it drops — and **raises `RuntimeError`** if the corpus did not re-iterate identically in anything the mask was chosen from: a different eligible count, or at any eligible position a different `_corpus_identity` — one hash over the ticker and every field either grouping key reads (`event_title`, `title`, `subtitle`) — so a same-count reorder, or a grouping field changed between walks under unchanged tickers, is refused rather than silently misaligning the mask. No other field is compared, because none can change which records are grouped; eligibility is re-applied by pass 2 itself. Every member of every group of two or more is kept, in its original relative order, so the group maps (keys, members, member order, insertion order), both pair lists, the census and every reported number are exactly those of grouping the whole eligible list; the census still logs from its old position through `_report_outcome_label_coverage`, after the RAM warning. Log lines are unchanged in text and relative order except one new INFO line after pass 2 (`Groupable subset: materializing %d of %d eligible markets — ...`) and the reworded RAM warning (`%d groupable markets (of %d eligible) are materialized for grouping: ...`). Not changed: `_can_ever_enter`, both grouping keys, pairing, Kelly, fees and `SETTLED_PREFILTER_CACHE_TAG`. The per-eligible-record cost is unchanged too — pass 1 computes each key once, exactly as the old full-list grouping did — so a long window's pass 1 is as silent as its old grouping step was. **Pruning each market dict to the keys the backtester actually reads is a specified but UNLANDED follow-up**, deliberately gated on a profiling measurement (accept only if peak RSS ≤ 3 GB and the `Potential pairs` counts are byte-identical on the same assembled cache); don't land a pruning diff without it.

**The live-sweep `min_settled_ts` must track `start_date`, not just `cutoff_ts` (fixed 2026-07-13).** `fetch_all_settled_markets()`'s live phase used to always pass `min_settled_ts=cutoff_ts` — correct for the common case (`start_date` far in the past, e.g. the default `2024-01-01`, where the archive already covers everything before `cutoff_ts` anyway), but catastrophic for a narrow recent `start_date`: live-verified 2026-07-13, requesting the last 7 days scanned 20k+ pages (20M+ records) over ~5 hours because the server was asked for everything since `cutoff_ts` (which can trail `now` by a long way) and the existing client-side `settle_epoch < start_ts` check just silently discarded almost every page. Since the live endpoint DOES honor `min_settled_ts` server-side, the fix is `min_settled_ts=max(cutoff_ts, start_ts)` — strictly narrows the server-side window (never widens it, so it can't skip markets the client-side filter wasn't already going to drop) and collapses a multi-hour scan to a bounded one for recent windows. Don't revert to a bare `cutoff_ts` — that reintroduces the unbounded scan for any caller that passes a recent `start_date` (e.g. a quick verification run or a future `--recent-only` mode). In the windowed live sweep this bound survives as the floor of the settled-day windows at the **phase** level: `live_min_ts = max(cutoff_ts, start_ts)` is computed once and never goes below that bound. The *per-window* `min_settled_ts` the server actually sees can be up to 86,399s below it, though — the first window rounds `live_min_ts` down to UTC midnight (`first_lo = live_min_ts - (live_min_ts % _DAY_SECONDS)`) so windows align on day boundaries. This is harmless: the live endpoint serves nothing before the archive cutoff regardless of what `min_settled_ts` asks for, and the client-side `_merge` step still drops any record with `settle < start_ts`.
