The existing tests use a midnight cutoff, so they cannot observe the rounding; don't read "tests assert the minimum" as proof the per-window value never dips below `max(cutoff_ts, start_ts)`.

**`KalshiClient` monkey-patch pattern:** The SDK does NOT accept `api_key_id` or `private_key_pem` as constructor parameters. They must be set as attributes on the `Configuration` object:
```python
cfg = Configuration(host=url)
cfg.api_key_id = key_id        # detected by hasattr() inside SDK
cfg.private_key_pem = pem_text
client = KalshiClient(configuration=cfg)
```
Do not "fix" this by moving them to the constructor — it will break silently.

**Fee ceiling rounding at small `n`:** `fee_leg_exact(1, 0.5)` = `ceil(0.07 * 1 * 0.5 * 0.5 * 100) / 100` = `ceil(1.75) / 100` = `$0.02`. At `n=1`, fees round up aggressively. The exact payoff check `min_payoff > 0` in `compute_trade()` (`min_payoff` is the profit floor for a same-title pair and the profit in either WIN cell for a time-series pair — the in-between cell loses `total_cost_with_fees`) catches this and returns `None` — this is intentional, not a bug. Also: `fee_leg_exact()` rounds to 6 decimals *before* the ceiling so binary float noise (e.g. `175.00000000000003`) can't bump an exact-cent fee up an extra cent — don't remove the `round()`.

**Ambiguity is resolved by position DELTA, never by absolute holdings.** An exception from `create_order` doesn't prove rejection (a timeout can land after the fill), so `_execute_one` consults the account — but the account's *absolute* position in a ticker is not evidence about a specific order: it may already hold contracts from an earlier run or a manual trade. `_execute_one` therefore reads `_position_count()` immediately **before** each submission (the NO leg's ticker first, then the YES leg's) and again after an ambiguous one, and attributes only `_fill_delta(before, after)` — expected `−no_leg.count` for the NO leg and `+yes_leg.count` for the YES leg. The old absolute checks were wrong in both directions: a stale external YES holding made an unfilled YES leg report `"executed"` (leaving the NO leg unhedged while the log claimed a complete pair), and a stale external NO holding made an unfilled NO leg trigger a `reduce_only` sell that liquidated an unrelated position. Compare deltas with `trader._DELTA_EPS` (`1e-6`) — fills are whole contracts, but `position_fp` arrives as a string and is float-parsed. Never reintroduce a bare `held == 0` / `if held:` test. **A delta of ZERO is not, by itself, a confirmed non-fill (DR-63/DR-64).** That rule is about which QUANTITY is evidence — the delta, never the absolute holding — and it is unchanged; what one un-refreshed read cannot establish is that the quantity has stopped moving. A transport error (the `urllib3.ProtocolError`/`ConnectionError`/`ReadTimeoutError` classes `_http._TRANSIENT_NETWORK_ERRORS` lists) can be raised milliseconds after the exchange FILLED the order, and the positions ledger is read-after-write lagged, so the first post-failure read returns the pre-fill value and `delta == 0`. On the YES leg that sent the reduce-only unwind of a NO leg that was really hedging a live YES fill — selling the hedge, leaving a naked YES position, and reporting `"rolled_back"`, which means flat; on the NO leg it returned `"failed"` ("nothing to unwind") with a full-size UNHEDGED NO position open and no status in `EXIT_TRADES_NEED_ATTENTION` to escalate it, so the run exited 0. Each ambiguous branch therefore re-reads its own ticker ONCE after `trader._V2_MAPPING_RECHECK_DELAY_SECONDS` and judges the RE-READ — the same reasoning `_confirm_v2_no_mapping` already applies (DR-21) and `v2_probe` applies twice over (DR-60, DR-20). An unreadable re-read is the existing unattributable case, i.e. `manual_review` with no order submitted. The NO leg's re-read is SINGLE-SHOT (`_position_count_once`) because it sits in the unhedged window; the YES leg's is the ordinary retried `_position_count`, matching the first read beside it. Both re-reads, like both first reads, stay OUTSIDE the `except` clause for the `__context__` reason above.

**`TradeResult.status` vocabulary** (the legs below are the SUBMISSION legs from `trader._ordered_legs(spec)` — the NO leg first, then the YES leg; which market each sits on depends on the pair type): `"executed"` (both legs filled, or the YES leg errored but the position delta was exactly `+yes_leg.count`), `"simulated"` (dry run), `"failed"` (the NO leg confirmed unfilled — a clean FoK rejection, or an error with a delta of 0 **on both the first read and the lag re-read**, DR-64; nothing to unwind), `"rolled_back"` (the YES leg confirmed unfilled — FoK rejection, or an error with a delta of 0 **on both readings**, DR-63 — and the NO leg's unwind FoK filled), `"rollback_failed"` (unwind did not fill — orphaned position), `"manual_review"` (a leg errored and its outcome could NOT be attributed to this order — either position lookup failed, or the position moved by an amount the order can't explain — or, on the V2 path, the NO-leg mapping was disproven by `_confirm_v2_no_mapping` after a confirmed NO-leg fill). `manual_review` submits no order at all — on the YES leg because unwinding could reverse a real fill, on the NO leg because a `reduce_only` sell against a position this order may not own would liquidate an unrelated holding, and on a disproven V2 mapping because the unwind rests on the very hypothesis just disproven (the YES leg is left unsubmitted and the NO leg deliberately left in place). The error strings say "NO leg"/"YES leg" (the ambiguous-but-confirmed YES-leg case keeps the word "ambiguous"), and the prod Excel row's Notes cell is prefixed `[<pair_type>: <SIDE_A> A / <SIDE_B> B[ nB=…]] ` so a row can be read without knowing which pair type it came from. Don't "simplify" `manual_review` into an automatic rollback in either leg.

**FoK price protection:** Every buy leg must be capped so it fills at or below the scanned price plus a small allowance, or not at all. On the **V2 path (default)** there is no "market" order type: a taker order is a marketable fill-or-kill LIMIT, and **the limit price IS the price protection** — `trader._v2_limit_price(leg_kind, scanned_price_dollars, market)` (`leg_kind` is `"buy_yes"`/`"buy_no"`; the scanned price is the leg's `_Leg.price_dollars` — `nA`/`pB` for same-title, `pA`/`nB` for time-series) computes it as the scanned price ceiled onto the market's own tick grid (`scanner.tick_size_for_price()`) plus `BUY_SLIPPAGE_TICKS` ticks, and the buy-NO leg sends the complement `1 − cap` as an ask. The scanned price is **quantized to 6 decimals (`trader._SCANNED_PRICE_QUANTUM`) before that ceiling**, the same round-before-ceil guard the legacy cap and `fee_leg_exact()` apply: every scanned ask level is `1.0 − float(bid)` and 20 of the 99 whole-cent complements land one ULP *above* the exact cent (`1.0 - 0.43 = 0.5700000000000001`), which un-quantized ceils a whole extra tick and hands the FoK limit two ticks of tolerance where one was configured. No Kalshi grid point has a 7th decimal, so the quantize can only remove float noise — it tightens or keeps the cap, never loosens it (TS-03). The final price is then **re-quantized (ceiling) onto the grid of the band that actually contains it** — the cap can step up into a coarser band, or be mirrored across the book by the complement, where the scanned band's grid is no longer valid — and clamped one tick inside the open unit interval on this market's own grid (0.99/0.01 on a cent market, not the finest-grid 0.9999/0.0001). Ceiling is chosen because a floor could round a YES cap BELOW the scanned price and make the order structurally unfillable. It is **not** purely protective, and this file used to claim it was: when the final price lands in a COARSER band than the one it was computed on, ceiling moves it AWAY from the scanned price and LOOSENS the cap by up to one destination-band tick (reachable: scanned 0.10 on `tapered_deci_cent` submits 0.11 where 0.101 was intended). That residual is bounded by one tick, is the accepted cost of keeping the order fillable, and is recorded as its own open finding — distinct from TS-10, which fixed the other half by making `tick_size_for_price` resolve a boundary price to the FINEST containing band instead of the first one. On the **legacy path** (`ORDER_API_VERSION="legacy"`) the cap is `buy_max_cost` = `count × (scanned price + BUY_MAX_COST_SLIPPAGE_CENTS)` (`trader._buy_max_cost_cents(leg.count, leg.price_dollars)`); that constant applies to the legacy path only, and `_buy_max_cost_cents()` rounds the price product to 6 decimals *before* the ceiling (the `fee_leg_exact()` idiom) so binary float noise — `7 * 0.07 * 100 = 49.000000000000006` — can't ceil the cap a cent looser; this only ever tightens it. Any new order-building code must carry the cap appropriate to its path.


**Order-book LEVELS and MARKET eligibility use different price bounds, and must keep doing so (TS-14).** `scanner._bids_to_ask_levels` drops a level whose complement falls outside `config.MIN_ACTIVE_PRICE_DOLLARS`/`MAX_ACTIVE_PRICE_DOLLARS` (`0.0001`/`0.9999`) — the extreme tradeable levels on Kalshi's FINEST grid. It used to use the 0.01/0.99 MARKET-eligibility bound instead, which silently discarded genuine depth on exactly the regimes whose point is sub-cent ticks: on a `deci_cent` or `center_deci_edge_centi_cent` book, every level under a cent or over 99c vanished before pairing, so a full book reached the sizer as a thin one. The drops are now counted and reported as one summary WARNING per book naming the ticker and the count (silent at zero) — the same idiom as the trading-inactive shard skip count — because invisible drops read downstream as genuinely thin depth. **`scanner._MIN_ACTIVE_PRICE`/`_MAX_ACTIVE_PRICE` (0.01/0.99) did NOT move and must not be unified with the level bound**: they answer a different question — "is this MARKET worth trading at all" — where a sub-cent YES ask means a near-settled market, and widening them would turn a 0.9999 quote into a $0.0001 hedge leg. `backtester.py`'s two mirrors of that market bound stay in step with it for the same reason. Widening the market half is a recorded, deliberately-held operator decision, not an oversight.
**The rollback carries the same kind of protection on the unwind side, on BOTH paths.** Neither unwind may ever be an unpriced order: the legacy `CreateOrderRequest` has no proceeds floor for `type="market"`, and V2 has no market type at all, so a book that collapsed between the NO leg's fill and the unwind would realize an unbounded loss. One bound, two expressions — `trader._rollback_floor_cents(no_leg)` is the NO leg's scanned entry (`no_leg.price_dollars`: `nA` for same-title, `nB` for time-series — the depth-weighted fill after enrichment) less `ROLLBACK_MAX_LOSS_CENTS_PER_CONTRACT` (12), clamped into the API's valid `1..99` cent range, with `round()` applied *before* `int()` because that price is a cent-quantized book price carried as a float (`0.57 → 56.99999999999999`, which `int()` would truncate to 56):
- **legacy** — a *floored* FoK **limit** sell (`type="limit"`, `no_price=_rollback_floor_cents(no_leg)`, `reduce_only`, on `no_leg.market.ticker` for `no_leg.count` contracts — the body is built inline in `_build_rollback_order_any`), never `type="market"`.
- **V2** — a reduce-only YES **bid** (a held NO is a short YES, so closing it is a buy-back), so the same bound is a bid **CEILING** of `1 − floor/100`. `trader._v2_rollback_price(no_leg)` **ceiling**-quantizes that cap onto the grid of the band containing it and then clamps it by `_v2_top_of_grid_price(no_leg.market)` (`V2_ROLLBACK_BID_PRICE_DOLLARS` floored onto its own band — 0.99 / 0.999 / 0.9999 by regime; `v2_probe`'s unfillable-ask step submits at exactly that level, and so does its no-mapping step's closing bid — the probe overrides the loss-floored price precisely because it is verifying a side, not a price). The ceiling direction here is deliberate and is the OPPOSITE trade-off from the buy legs: it loosens the loss bound by strictly under one destination-band tick, because a floor-quantized off-grid cap would sit below every valid level in its band and structurally kill every off-grid unwind — the orphaned-position failure this path exists to prevent.

A book past the bound kills the unwind on either path, leaving the NO-leg position open — reported as `status="rollback_failed"` for manual review (`_rollback_no_leg(client, spec, no_leg, reason)`'s ORPHANED POSITION alerts name `no_leg.market.ticker` and `no_leg.count`, so a time-series rollback failure names `market_b`, not `market_a`), exactly the path an unfilled market unwind already took, so the caller's contract is unchanged and only the loss is now bounded. Don't "simplify" either unwind back to an unpriced (or flat top-of-grid) order.

**Backtest cache semantics:** `fetch_all_settled_markets()` and `fetch_candlesticks()` always persist a fresh fetch to disk even when `use_cache=False`, so `--no-cache` refreshes what the next default run loads. `use_cache` gates only the assembled per-start_date `settled_markets_*` cache; the per-day slice stores under `backtest_cache/archive_days/` and `backtest_cache/live_days/` are consulted regardless (see the sharded-fetch gotcha for why they can't go stale). **Since SS-1's Commit C the assembled cache is streamed:** it is named `settled_markets_<start_date>[_<prefilter_tag>][_nomve].jsonl.gz`, written in the day slices' `"jsonl-v1"` framing through `_DayStreamWriter` (atomic tmp+rename, so it is either absent or complete) with a meta block that repeats its identity (`historical._assembled_cache_meta`: kind, start_date, prefilter_tag, include_mve, format — so a file copied onto another request's name is refused), and served as a `SettledCorpus`. On `use_cache=True` the lookup order is: (1) if the `.jsonl.gz` EXISTS, it is validated by ONE full streaming walk — all or nothing: any decode error, truncation, damaged deflate stream or meta mismatch is a WARNING (`Corrupt or mismatched settled-market cache — treating as cache miss`) and a miss that REBUILDS (step 3) — never a fall-through to step 2; (2) only when NO `.jsonl.gz` exists, a LEGACY `settled_markets_<same stem>.json` — the single JSON document every run before Commit C wrote, GB-scale (measured 2026-09-24: the 2026-05-01 one is 1.30 GB, the largest, 2026-09-14, 3.27 GB) — loaded EXACTLY as before through `_load_json_cache`, whole, as a list; (3) failing that, the fetch. New runs never write the legacy format. **A committed rebuild RETIRES the legacy file of the same stem (SS-1 review, fixed 2026-09-24):** once the `.jsonl.gz` is committed, `historical._retire_legacy_cache` deletes `settled_markets_<same stem>.json` (INFO `Removed the superseded legacy settled-market cache …`; a failed delete is a WARNING telling the operator to delete it by hand, and the run goes on). That restores the old invariant — the old code's rebuild OVERWROTE that file, so a superseded assembly could never come back — and destroys exactly what that overwrite destroyed, only after the replacement is on disk (a rebuild that raises retires nothing). Without it the stale legacy corpus — typically the one the BS-02 or subtitle-drift remedy had just rebuilt — was served again whenever the `.jsonl.gz` was damaged (after a WARNING claiming a cache miss) or missing (silently; until 2026-09-24 this repo lived in iCloud-synced `~/Documents`, where iCloud reverted a committed rename — a failed delete or a hand restore can do the same). Rule (1)'s no-fall-through is the second guard, for a legacy file that survives beside a streamed one anyway (a failed delete, a hand restore). Don't reintroduce either fall-through. Pinned by `tests/test_historical.py::TestStreamedAssembledCache` (damaged, vanished and rename-reverted streamed caches with a legacy file present; retirement only after commit; the failed-delete WARNING). One `Loaded %d … from cache` line reports either kind of hit — worded `eligible markets` when the cache was assembled under a prefilter (the backtester's always is) and followed by the assembly's counts when the cache recorded them, or by a statement that it recorded none (M9, `historical._log_cache_load`); without a prefilter it still begins `Loaded %d settled markets from cache`. **A hit is ANNOUNCED, never silent — and still makes ZERO network calls (DR-13; M2/M3 of the 2026-09-24 7-day-run review, fixed by P2).** An assembled cache is a snapshot: it holds no market settled after it was assembled (the 2026-09-17 cache's meta says `assembled_at` 2026-09-24T12:37:49Z, and its first record — the live frontier is served newest-settled first — settled at 12:18:38Z that same day, a partial frontier day), while every Period line says the window runs to today, and before P2 a hit logged only the count — the `assembled_at` stamp SS-1 wrote was never read. Now the streamed cache's meta carries informational keys the identity check never compares (`_assembled_cache_meta` does not name them, so a cache written before any of them existed is still served): `assembled_at`, since P2 `archive_cutoff_ts` — the cutoff this assembly read — and since M9 `assembly_counts` (see the prefilter gotcha). `SettledCorpus.open_validated` captures the whole meta block from its one validating walk (`_day_store_iter(..., meta_out=)`), never from a second read, and `historical._corpus_provenance` — one pure function of the block, applied to the block just written on a fresh assembly and to the block read back on a hit — builds a frozen `CorpusProvenance(from_cache, assembled_at, archive_cutoff, post_cutoff, assembly_counts)` carried on the returned corpus (its remaining field, `legacy`, is set only on a legacy `.json` hit, below). A hit then logs (INFO, `_announce_cache_hit`) the assembly time and age, that the window nominally runs to today, the cutoff at assembly, and what `--no-cache` COSTS to extend the corpus — never a promise of a cheap top-up: it re-assembles the whole corpus, reusing a day slice only while it is still valid (a live slice once its day has fully elapsed, an archive slice only while the cutoff is the one it was fetched under — on 2026-09-24 none of the 63 archive slices on disk for 2026-05-01..07-02 carried the 2026-07-25 cutoff read that day, and the other 22 of the 85 archive days a 2026-05-01 window then needed, 07-03..07-24, were not on disk at all), and it re-fetches every pair's candlesticks and re-resolves event titles, so for a window reaching back before the cutoff it is close to a full fetch. A legacy `.json` hit logs its FILE TIME instead and says it records no cutoff, and is returned as a `historical.LegacySettledCorpus` — a `list` subclass (equality, `len()`, iteration unchanged; building it copies the pointer array once, 8 bytes per record) that adds only `.provenance` (`legacy=True`, the mtime as `assembled_at`, no cutoff or verdict), so the file time reaches the page too: seven of the eight assembled caches on disk on 2026-09-24 were legacy, including the three pre-cutoff ones (2026-05-01/05-28/07-13, all last written 2026-08-03) whose trades and `k̂` staleness can actually move. **Staleness is announced, not enforced** (operator decision §4 of the 2026-09-15 fix plan): a NON-empty cache is never expired by age. **An EMPTY one is:** `[]` is `is not None`, so an empty assembled cache used to be a PERMANENT zero-network hit — the 2-byte `settled_markets_2026-08-29_*.json`, last written 2026-09-01 00:16 UTC (its file time), was still served on 2026-09-24. `historical._serve_assembled_cache` serves an empty cache (streamed or legacy) only while it is younger than `config.EMPTY_ASSEMBLED_CACHE_MAX_AGE_SECONDS` (one day), with a WARNING; older — or with no readable assembly time, or a stamp in the future — it is a miss that rebuilds (and, for a legacy file, the rebuild's commit retires it like any other). That rebuild is an ordinary MISS, i.e. a full re-assembly of the window, not a quick check: the 2026-08-29 file's is 26 days of full-volume live slices (17 on disk, 5.26 GB gzipped, against 2.10 GB for the 7-day 2026-09-17 window, whose fresh run peaked at 2,916,679,680 bytes max RSS), for a window that is post-cutoff and so 0-trade anyway. Age is measured from the recorded assembly moment, not the UTC calendar date. The provenance rides `backtester._prepare_candidates` (read BY TYPE off a `SettledCorpus` or a `LegacySettledCorpus`, never by attribute probing) → `_Candidates.corpus_provenance` → `BacktestSweep.corpus_provenance`, defaulted like `label_coverage`, and is rendered by `dashboard._corpus_provenance_html` directly under the Period line on EVERY run (DR-66: a healthy run shows its assembly time too), and by `backtest._log_corpus_provenance(sweep)` as the run's closing line; `None` — the Monday-feasibility short-circuit, a test that stubs the fetch with a plain list, a hand-built sweep, no sweep at all — renders "not recorded". Pinned by `tests/test_historical.py::TestCorpusProvenance` (whose hits run with clients and a signed GET that FAIL the test on any access), `tests/test_backtester.py::TestCorpusProvenanceIsCarried`, `tests/test_dashboard.py::TestCorpusProvenanceHeader` and `tests/test_backtest.py::TestCorpusProvenanceLine`; SS-1's `TestStreamedAssembledCache::test_a_legacy_json_cache_is_still_served_whole_as_a_list` now asserts `isinstance(out, list)` and the subclass rather than `type(out) is list`, its one re-pinned assertion. The page header is outside `TestGoldenSections`' digests, so no golden moved. The outcome-label census WARNING (`backtester._report_outcome_label_coverage`) and the dashboard's below-floor banner (`dashboard._label_coverage_html`) name BOTH spellings in their "equivalently, also delete the assembled cache" clause — the streamed `settled_markets_*.jsonl.gz` and any legacy `settled_markets_*.json` — because naming the `.json` alone, as they did until the SS-1 follow-up, left the streamed cache to be served again. That banner's `tests/fixtures/dashboard_golden_main.json` digest was deliberately re-captured after `main` @ `fe0a758` (the fixture's `amended` entry names it, beside the other deliberate re-captures listed there). Every component of the name is part of the RESULT's identity, and the DR-57/prefilter-tag rules below apply unchanged to both formats (they share the name stem). When a `prefilter` is passed, the tag is appended — a prefiltered result is a strict subset, so it must never be served to an unfiltered caller or to one using different filter semantics. **`INCLUDE_MVE_MARKETS=False` appends `_nomve` (DR-57).** The flag changes WHAT IS FETCHED, not just how markets are grouped: it adds `mve_filter="exclude"` to the archive query and to every live page (three sites in `historical.py`), so an unkeyed cache served an MVE-INCLUSIVE assembly to a run configured to exclude MVE, and vice versa — severe in practice because ~99.7% of the assembled cache is MVE combo markets, so an `INCLUDE_MVE_MARKETS=False` run analysed an almost entirely MVE corpus while believing it had excluded them, with nothing abnormal in the output. **Only the False case is marked, deliberately:** every assembled cache currently on disk was built with MVE included (there is a 3.27 GB one), so the default `True` filename stays byte-identical to the pre-DR-57 name and no existing cache is orphaned into a multi-hour refetch — don't "tidy" this into a symmetric `_mve`/`_nomve` pair. The per-day slice stores need no such marker: their meta already carries `include_mve`, so a flag flip invalidates them on the existing meta check — at the cost of refetching and OVERWRITING every slice on each flip, since the slice PATHS themselves are not flag-keyed (before DR-57 the assembled-cache hit masked this, so a flip never reached the slices at all; now a flipped run re-pays the day fetch, and so does the next run that flips back and misses its assembled cache). `_prepare_candidates()` (the band- and k-independent prologue shared by `run_backtest()`, through `_prepare_entries()`, and `run_backtest_sweep()`) passes `config.SETTLED_PREFILTER_CACHE_TAG`, which names `backtester._can_ever_enter`'s semantics: **bump the tag whenever that predicate changes**, or a stale prefiltered cache is silently reused. **P5 bumped it (`monday-eligibility-v1` → `monday-checkpoint-v2`, the checkpoint-instant predicate — see the prefilter gotcha), which ORPHANS every assembled cache written under v1:** all eight on disk on 2026-09-24 — `settled_markets_<date>_monday-eligibility-v1.*` for 2026-05-01, 05-28, 07-13 (the three pre-cutoff ones), 08-29, 09-05, 09-07, 09-14 and 09-17, 8,692,223,608 bytes in all. They are never read again and, because the NAME STEM changed, never retired either (`_retire_legacy_cache` deletes only a legacy file of the rebuild's own stem), so they sit in `backtest_cache/` until deleted by hand. The day slices are not keyed by the tag and are untouched. None of the eight held a usable RESULT: the three pre-cutoff ones were assembled on 2026-08-03, before the subtitle fix, and group strike-blind (the 2026-05-01 one at 1.86% subtitle coverage — see `BACKTEST_OUTCOME_LABEL_WARN_FRACTION`'s comment — and head samples of the other two are almost entirely `null`), which this file already says to rebuild; the 2-byte 2026-08-29 one is empty and more than a day old, which DR-13's rule already treats as a miss; and the other four start after the 2026-07-25 cutoff, so they are 0-trade by construction. What the orphaning costs is the next run of each start date. A post-cutoff window re-assembles from the live day slices on disk plus the frontier day (the 7-day window's fresh run took 2,382 s against 1,040 s from its cache — about 22 minutes more), and fetches any live day not on disk. A PRE-cutoff window's next run is close to a full fetch rather than a cache hit, and its duration is UNMEASURED: on 2026-09-24 it needed 85 / 58 / 12 archive created-days (start 05-01 / 05-28 / 07-13), none of them valid on disk — the 221 slices for 2025-10-15..2026-05-27 were stamped with the 2026-06-04 cutoff, the 36 for 2026-05-28..07-02 with 2026-07-03, against the 2026-07-25 cutoff read that day, and 2026-07-03..07-24 had no slice — plus 35 of its 61 past live days (07-25..09-23; 08-03..08-28, 08-31..09-04, 09-12, 09-13, 09-15 and 09-16 are not on disk), the frontier and the tail walk. The one timing anchor is run 1 of the 2026-09-17 window, whose 7 full-volume live days (22,175,942 records) took 1 h 47 min fetched in parallel (00:30:46 → 02:17:45 in that run's log). That is the rebuild the strike-blind caches needed anyway, and it is not by itself a complete fix: the live slices for 07-25..08-02, 08-29 and 08-30 (written 2026-08-03 and 2026-09-01 UTC) carry `subtitle: null` too and are reused unconditionally until deleted (the subtitle-drift remedy). The chain's own provenance also needs a rebuild to show: a hit on any v1 cache records neither `archive_cutoff_ts` (P2) nor `assembly_counts` (M9, P3), so both read "not recorded" until the start date is re-assembled — which the bump now forces. `prefilter` and `prefilter_tag` must be passed together (`ValueError` otherwise) precisely so an untagged filtered cache can't exist. Candlestick cache files are tagged with the `[open_ts, close_ts]` window AND the `period_interval` they cover — a cache whose window starts later than the request (one built for a later `--start-date`) is not reused for an earlier one, while a market that opened after both start dates is requested from its own open (`backtester._candle_window_open`) under either and reuses its cache, and a cache built under a different granularity (e.g. an old pre-hourly daily cache) is not silently reused as if it matched the current `CANDLESTICK_PERIOD_INTERVAL_MINUTES`. Old caches from before this tag existed migrate automatically on the next fetch, same as the legacy bare-list format. **`event_titles_v2.json` is a cross-run accumulator of GENUINE answers, is written as a MERGE, and is RETURNED as one, so `--no-cache` no longer wipes it OR discards it:** `_load_or_build_event_titles` always reads the disk map (even when `use_cache=False`, which still re-resolves every requested ticker), keeps this call's answers in a map of their own, and merges them into the loaded accumulator IN PLACE: disk entries are preserved, a fresh non-empty title wins, a fresh `""` (the poison pill from a failed lookup, or an event a listing returned without a title) never clobbers a non-empty disk title, and a fresh `""` for a ticker disk has never seen is still stored, keeping poison-pill semantics — but a ticker the lookup cap DEFERRED is never stored at all (DR-51; see the event-title gotcha). The file is rewritten only when the call changed it, and never when an accumulator file could not be READ that call: `historical._read_title_file` tells an `OSError` (a permission error, an iCloud file that cannot be downloaded offline — it may read next run) from content that is not a JSON object, and a read failure writes nothing, so it can neither overwrite the v2 file nor commit the migration marker over legacy titles nobody saw (the next run reads it again, or retries the migration). A v2 file whose CONTENT is bad is replaced, as the pre-DR-51 accumulator was. **The legacy `event_titles.json` is migrated once.** It stored `""` for every deferred ticker too — 3,996,906 → 7,986,570 keys (202 → 374 MB) in the one fresh 2026-09-24 7-day run, 7,918,449 of them KXMVE tickers mapped to `""` — and the old code parsed it whole and held two more full copies of it (a seed and a merge) on every fresh fetch, which put the title phase at the top of that run's peak RSS. `_load_event_title_accumulator` keeps exactly the legacy file's non-empty entries (a legacy `""` cannot be told apart from a never-looked-up ticker, so every one is dropped), `_retire_legacy_event_titles` deletes the legacy file once the v2 file is committed, and the v2 file's EXISTENCE is the marker, so a legacy file an older build writes back later is ignored. A legacy file whose content cannot be parsed is WARNed about and left in place, none of its titles are imported, and the v2 file is written as the marker; the WARNING names the recovery (repair it, delete `event_titles_v2.json`, re-run). A legacy file still present beside the v2 file — that one, a delete that failed or was interrupted after the migration, or an older build's (a checkout from before DR-51, sharing this `backtest_cache/`) re-grown accumulator — is never read and never deleted, since in the last case it is that build's live data, but `_warn_if_legacy_lingers` names it and its size in a WARNING on every call until it is gone. Measured with a synthetic-accumulator harness (a fresh process per call, network mocked, peak RSS over the caller's request set): with 2,000,000 accumulated keys and a 2,000,000-ticker request the pre-DR-51 call added 1,075 MiB, and 1,657 MiB on the next 2,000,000-ticker window (its accumulator by then 286.7 MB); the post-DR-51 call added 497 MiB on its one-time migration and 85 MiB on the next window (accumulator 1.38 MB). The one-time migration of a 7,986,570-key legacy file added 1,412 and 1,424 MiB (two runs) over a 1,000,000-ticker request. Read these as magnitudes: identical fresh-process runs vary — three runs of a 4,000,000-key migration over a 4,000,000-ticker request added 561, 855 and 916 MiB. What a dropped legacy `""` re-opens: nothing unless a later window asks for that ticker. Only 16 of them were non-combo; a re-asked combo is deferred again, never looked up, unless that window's whole miss set fits under the cap. The one standing cost is that a cache-miss re-fetch of an already-seen window no longer returns early — its combo tickers are unknown again, so the bulk listings page to their barren bail-out (the bulk listings took 36 s on the 2026-09-24 fresh fetch, a 2,382 s run). **The RETURN is that merged view restricted to the caller's tickers** (the in-place-merged accumulator read once per requested ticker), not this run's resolution alone. Returning `cached` discarded the accumulator at the last step: under `--no-cache` every ticker the listings missed and the `EVENT_TITLE_FALLBACK_MAX_LOOKUPS` cap skipped came back as the `""` pill even though disk held a real title — 771,601 unresolved against a 5,000 cap, so ~99% of stragglers. Those markets then group by market title alone, collapsing `(event_title, title, subtitle)` toward the bare title (measured: 52 groups with titles vs 133, largest 112, without), which is the direction that manufactures cross-event false positives under the 95% co-resolution prior (TS-11). Restricted to the requested tickers because the accumulator holds every ticker every past run ever resolved; a caller asking about forty must not receive eight hundred thousand.

**The Monday-feasibility pre-check measures its window in UTC.** `_prepare_candidates` decides whether any 09:00 **UTC** entry checkpoint exists in `[start_date, today]`, and `today` is `datetime.now(UTC).date()` — never `date.today()`, which is LOCAL. West of UTC the local date lags for the first hours of each UTC day (7 of every 24 on a PDT host), so a window whose only Monday IS the current UTC day short-circuited to zero trades and reported the run as structurally impossible when it was not (TS-13). `_build_equity_curve` already used UTC for the same reason; `backtest.py`'s "Period:" line and `dashboard.py`'s rendered period follow, or they print a span the run did not simulate. Tests freeze `backtester.datetime.now(tz)`, not `backtester.date.today()` — the seam moved with the fix.

**Backtest windows must start BEFORE the archive cutoff or they find zero trades by construction.** Live-era (post-cutoff) markets 404 on `/historical/markets/{ticker}/candlesticks` — verified live 2026-08-03: a `--start-date` after the cutoff (then 2026-06-04) produced 404s for every one of its pair tickers, so `_find_entry()` saw no candles for either leg and the run could never have entered a trade, no matter how many pairs were extracted. A near-zero trade count on a recent window is this, not a strategy result. **`fetch_all_settled_markets` WARNS on it (`start_date (…) is at or after the archive cutoff (…) — … structurally 0-trade`) and never aborts; and since P2 the verdict survives a cache hit and reaches the page (M2).** The WARNING used to sit after the cache-hit early return, behind the `/historical/cutoff` read a hit never makes, so every cached re-run of a post-cutoff window logged nothing (run2 of the 2026-09-17 window warned; run3, the same window from cache, did not) and the dashboard never carried it on either path. The cutoff is now stamped into the assembled cache (`archive_cutoff_ts`, see the cache-semantics gotcha), one predicate decides the verdict everywhere (`historical._starts_at_or_after_cutoff`), and a hit repeats the WARNING worded "as of this cached corpus's assembly … unless the cutoff has since moved past start_date" — the cutoff has only ever been seen to ADVANCE (2026-06-04 read on 2026-08-03, 2026-07-25 on 2026-09-24), so a True verdict can go stale and a hit does not re-read it; `--no-cache` re-checks. The same carried verdict (`CorpusProvenance.post_cutoff`) drives a red banner under the dashboard's Period line and a WARNING beside `backtest.py`'s closing summary. Both state a BOUND, not a cause — "no trade could be entered whatever pairs formed" — because a post-cutoff window can also fail earlier: the 2026-09-17 run formed 0 pairs (`Potential pairs: 0 time-series, 0 same-title`), so the cutoff was not what bound its zero. **Both renderers read a True verdict beside the run's own trades, through ONE helper, `backtester.max_trades_simulated(sweep)`** (the busiest of the primary, every swept `k`, every band scenario and the same-title point): a stamped verdict goes stale once the cutoff moves past `start_date`, the corpus's markets then have candlesticks (404s are never cached) and the run can trade, and a red "no trade could be entered" beside "Trades found: N" would be false on its face — so any simulated trade turns the banner into an amber "that verdict is stale" line and the closing WARNING into its stale-verdict form; zero proves nothing either way. The page adds the size-cap points its filter walk simulates (`dashboard._MaxTrades`), which the log cannot see — see the size-cap gotcha. **No cache on disk on 2026-09-24 can produce the verdict on a hit:** a streamed cache written before P2 (the 2026-09-17 one) and every legacy `.json` (seven files, including the post-cutoff 2026-08-29/09-05/09-07/09-14 ones) record no cutoff, so each hit says "not recorded" and claims no verdict until that start date is rebuilt with `--no-cache`. **DR-50's planned short-circuit (an open operator decision) composes with this, and must not be taken on a hit:** it belongs on the miss path where the WARNING fires, branching on `_starts_at_or_after_cutoff` after the cutoff read (as the 2026-09-15 plan placed it); a hit's verdict is as-of-assembly and may be stale, so short-circuiting on it could skip a window that has since become tradeable, and a hit already costs no fetch. Its "no simulation" outcome should carry `CorpusProvenance(from_cache=False, assembled_at=None, archive_cutoff=<cutoff>, post_cutoff=True)` onto the sweep, so the banner still renders on that path, where the Monday short-circuit's sweep carries `None`. The window always runs from `--start-date` to today, so even a correctly-chosen pre-cutoff start still has a post-cutoff tail whose 404s are unavoidable; those failures are deliberately NOT cached (a poisoned cache would silence a ticker forever, including after its data migrates into the archive), so they are re-paid on every run — just in parallel now. **Because they are re-paid every run, each one must be cheap to log (TS-02).** `historical.fetch_candlesticks` logs a failure as ONE line — `HTTP <status> <reason>` via `historical._exception_summary()`, never the SDK's `ApiException.__str__`, which emits the status, the reason, the ENTIRE HTTP header dict and the body across five lines (~900 bytes) and whose own first line is only `(404)`. `backtester._fetch_candles_parallel` then reports the per-run count once, just before it returns: `N of M tickers returned no candles`, counted off the RESULT dict (there is no exception to count — `fetch_candlesticks` already fail-softs to `[]`) and deliberately outside its `if work:` block so tickers resolved to `[]` for a missing/unparseable `close_time` are included, silent at zero. `historical._lookup_one`'s event-title failure takes the identical one-line treatment, for the same reason: it fires once per unresolved ticker, up to `EVENT_TITLE_FALLBACK_MAX_LOOKUPS` of them. Don't log an SDK exception whole on any per-ticker path — one ordinary backtest emitted hundreds of MB into `kalshi_backtest.log`'s 20 MB × 3 rotation, 99.5% of the file was that single warning, and the rotation evicted ~419 MB of history including the shard census, prefilter counts and calibration table the same runs had written.

**Daily candlesticks silently produced zero data for most markets — use hourly.** `historical.fetch_candlesticks()` requests `period_interval=CANDLESTICK_PERIOD_INTERVAL_MINUTES` (60, hourly), not daily (1440). Confirmed 2026-07 by direct API testing: `/historical/markets/{ticker}/candlesticks` with `period_interval=1440` only emits a bar for a market whose lifespan crosses a UTC midnight boundary — a market open for 2 hours entirely within one day returned 0 daily candles but 2 hourly candles for the same window. Most Kalshi markets are single-game/few-hour windows within one calendar day, so daily granularity structurally produced zero price data for most markets regardless of liquidity or age, which is why backtest runs kept finding 0 trades even after the pair-extraction logic itself was verified correct. `period_interval=1` (minute) returns HTTP 400 — hourly is the finest granularity actually available. `_candle_at_or_before()` and `_monday_timestamps()` in `backtester.py` are granularity-agnostic (they just scan sorted candles for the latest one at-or-before a timestamp), so switching interval required no change to the entry-detection logic — only the fetch call and the cache tag. Candles are fetched in parallel across tickers (`backtester._fetch_candles_parallel`, `CANDLESTICK_FETCH_MAX_WORKERS`); each worker still paces its own pages with `fetch_candlesticks`'s unchanged `rate_limit_sleep` default (0.15s), so the per-connection rate is the same as before — only the number of connections changed.

**Backtester pair-extraction eligibility prefilter — don't remove it.** At current Kalshi volumes, normalized-title groups can collapse tens of thousands of hourly/intraday markets into one group (a live measurement found a 53k-member hourly-crypto group in a 3-month window), making the naive O(n²) pairing in `_extract_pairs()` infeasible (500B+ iterations observed, hung for over an hour). `_prepare_candidates()` (shared by `run_backtest()`, through `_prepare_entries()`, and `run_backtest_sweep()`) filters `markets` through `backtester._can_ever_enter(m, start_date)` before either grouping call: `_find_entry()` can only open a trade at a Monday-09:00-UTC checkpoint whose DATE lies in `[start_date, min(close_a, close_b) − 1 day]` and needs an hourly candle at-or-before that checkpoint for both legs; a candle's `ts` is its period END and a market has no candle ending at or before the start of the hour it opened in, so each leg must have OPENED STRICTLY BEFORE the checkpoint INSTANT — a market with no such checkpoint on/after `start_date` and dated on or before its own close date minus one day can never appear in any entered pair, as either leg, in either pair type, and can be safely dropped before grouping. **The lower bound is the checkpoint INSTANT, not the opening DATE (M8 of the 2026-09-24 review, fixed in P5).** The predicate used to compare `open_time` by date, so it kept every market that opened later on the checkpoint Monday itself — none of which `_find_entry` can enter at that checkpoint: 2,192,241 of the 7,274,215 records of the 2026-09-17 7-day window's assembled cache (30.1%, the review's streamed `open_time` histogram), and — contrary to the review's expectation that a multi-week window would dilute it — 336,750 of the 570,506 records of the 4-Monday 2026-07-13 legacy cache (59.0%, measured for P5 by streaming that file), every one opened on a Monday at or after 09:00 UTC. `backtester._checkpoint_datetime` is now the ONE definition of the checkpoint instant, read by both `_monday_timestamps` (the scan) and `_can_ever_enter` (the prefilter); the upper bound is still `close_time`'s DATE minus a day, exactly as `_find_entry` builds `scan_end`, and a naive `open_time` (no offset — only a hand-edited cache) keeps the date test, as does an aware one whose UTC instant falls outside `datetime`'s range (a year-1 time east of UTC, a year-9999 one west of it, where `astimezone(UTC)` raises `OverflowError`). The rest is done on date DIFFERENCES, never a date plus a `timedelta`, so the predicate never raises on any parseable timestamp — it runs as the fetch's assembly prefilter (on the frontier's worker thread too) and in both of `_prepare_candidates`' passes, where an exception would end the run rather than keep one absurd record; the date test it replaced could raise near either end of the range (`close − 1 day` on a year-1 close, its advance-to-Monday loop past 9999-12-31). **The boundary is measured, and it is tight.** Re-measured for P5 on the DR-73 calibration corpus's cached candle series (3,542 series, 952,519 hourly candles): 0 candles end at or before the start of their market's opening hour — including the 573 series whose request opened before the market did, so the endpoint had the chance to serve one — and at 13,239 Monday checkpoints a candle at or before the checkpoint always meant an opening strictly before it; 1,233 of the 1,310 series whose market opened off the hour have a first candle ending at the next top of the hour, so a market opened at 08:30 CAN enter at 09:00 and the rule cannot be tightened past `open_time < checkpoint`. On the 2026-07-13 cache, 11,098 of the markets P5 drops have a cached candle series (138,240 candles), and none has a candle at or before any checkpoint it could have been scanned at. That rule is an API behaviour, not a documented contract — the date test relied on a weaker form of it — so if a series ever shows a candle ending before its opening hour, revisit this predicate. **Result-neutral for a given corpus, and pinned three ways.** The checkpoint-instant set is a SUBSET of the date-granular one for every real UTC offset (the date test read the date in the string's own offset; a UTC date trailing the local one means a UTC time of 10:00 or later, past that Monday's checkpoint), and on a fixed corpus the candidate list holds exactly the old pairs minus every pair with a dropped leg — nothing is added, and each group's own pairs keep their order — but a group whose FIRST eligible member was dropped now first appears later in the eligible stream, so its pairs can move later in the list (measured on the real 2026-05-01 corpus: 826 of 2,054 pairs kept, 9 order inversions, the same 3 trades); every removed pair's `_find_entry` is `None`, every kept pair's is identical, and `run_backtest`'s trades and equity curve are identical — list order reaches a result only through `_simulate_at_discount`'s tie-breaks, i.e. two candidates from different groups tied EXACTLY on entry date, `entry_monthly_ratio` and pair type, whose listing order can swap and, only if cash binds between them, which one is funded — `tests/test_backtester.py::TestCanEverEnterAtTheCheckpointInstant` (the boundary to the second, UTC offsets, the naive fallback, and both ends of `datetime`'s range — never an exception, and still a subset of the date test there), `::TestCanEverEnterMatchesTheScan` (exact equality, over a 65,000-point grid, with an oracle built from `_monday_timestamps` and the measured candle rule, plus the subset property) and `::TestCheckpointPrefilterIsResultNeutral` (the real `_prepare_candidates` / `_entries_for_band` / `run_backtest`, ladders on and off, the old predicate patched in as the baseline). At scale (P5's scratch harness, two 30,000-market synthetic corpora, ladders on and off): 1,663–1,819 candidate pairs removed per run, all `None`; 2,508–3,151 entries and 20 trades identical; candle-fetch tickers down by about 30% (10,758 → 7,358 to 12,286 → 8,716). On the real 2026-07-13 corpus, streamed through the real `_prepare_candidates` with only locally cached candles (no network): 273 → 170 candidate pairs, the 103 removed all `None` (22 of them with candle series on both legs), 0 entries either way, and `_prepare_candidates` plus every pair's `_find_entry` took 43.4–43.9 s → 20.0–20.3 s at a 465–493 MiB peak. That corpus was assembled on 2026-08-03, before the subtitle fix (5,983 of the 6,051 `subtitle` fields in its first 3,000,000 bytes are `null`), so it groups STRIKE-BLIND: its pair counts are not what a healthy corpus forms, and with 0 entries it never exercised a kept pair's entry — that equality rests on the synthetic corpora and the unit tests above, and the real corpus shows only that nothing dropped could have entered. What DOES move is intended: every eligible-market figure (`Markets to analyze`, `Groupable subset`, the outcome-label and deadline-phrasing census denominators, so a `below_floor` verdict can move in an edge case) now counts only markets that could enter — the eligible counts this file quotes from before P5 (7,274,215; 7,260,952; the frontier's 7,190,452) were measured under the date test. One assembly-time effect is not a predicate effect and is recorded rather than excluded: a fresh assembly resolves event titles for fewer tickers, and when its unresolved non-combo set exceeds `EVENT_TITLE_FALLBACK_MAX_LOOKUPS` the cap can reach tickers a v1 assembly never looked up — the same run-to-run variation any two assemblies at different times already have. The predicate costs 0.78 µs a call (0.776–0.784 µs) against the date test's 1.46–1.54 µs, over 300,000 synthetic cache-shaped records (two runs, each timing both predicates twice) — cheaper, because it finds the first Monday by date arithmetic instead of the date test's day-by-day loop. **`config.SETTLED_PREFILTER_CACHE_TAG` went from `monday-eligibility-v1` to `monday-checkpoint-v2` with it** — see the cache-semantics gotcha for what that orphans. `open_time` is carried through `historical._market_to_dict()`; **cache files written before this field existed have no `open_time` and read back as `None`**, and `_can_ever_enter()` treats a missing/unparseable `open_time` or `close_time` as "can't prove ineligibility" (keeps the market, no speedup) rather than risking an incorrect drop — re-run with `--no-cache` to unlock the speedup on old caches. The same predicate is ALSO handed to `fetch_all_settled_markets(prefilter=...)` so ineligible records are dropped during assembly instead of being materialized and cached first — at full-MVE volumes the unfiltered list is the difference between a working run and an OOM, and it also shrinks the `_load_or_build_event_titles` ticker set from runaway to bounded. This is result-neutral by construction (filtering during an order-preserving merge yields the same subsequence as filtering afterwards; `test_prefilter_assembly_equals_postfilter` asserts exact list equality), and the day-slice FILES stay complete and unfiltered because they're shared across start dates. **Keep `_prepare_candidates()`'s own post-fetch filter anyway** — it's idempotent, is applied inside both of its walks over the corpus at no extra pass (SS-1), and keeps the guarantee local to the O(n²) pairing that depends on it. **What the prefilter drops is COUNTED and REPORTED, not only dropped (M9 of the 2026-09-24 review, fixed in P3).** The fetch's lines used to count only its survivors while calling them `settled markets` (`Historical endpoint: %d markets`, `Live endpoint: %d recently settled markets`, `Total settled markets from %s: %d`), and the backtester's re-check, run on a corpus the fetch had already prefiltered, logged `Eligibility prefilter: skipping 0/N` on every production path — the 7-day window printed `skipping 0/7274215` beside `Live endpoint: 7274215 recently settled markets` while about 17.3M of about 24.6M settled records had been dropped (the review's estimate, an upper bound on the prefilter's share since it also covers boundary duplicates and out-of-window records), so a prefilter that worked and one that rejected nothing logged the same lines. The assembly's FIRST walk now counts, per endpoint, every record settled in the window and splits the ones not kept into `rejected` (the prefilter) and `duplicates` (a repeated or blank ticker) — `historical._AssemblyTally`, frozen as `historical.AssemblyCounts(settled, rejected, duplicates)` with `kept` derived — never a second walk; walk B counts nothing. For that the phases hand their DAY SLICES back UNFILTERED (the read-back `keep` saved no memory — a walk holds one record at a time either way — and hid every rejection from any count); the three filters that must drop records before they are held — the live frontier's sink, and each sequential fallback — report what they dropped (`note_rejected`; the frontier as its window's emitted count minus the spool's length, only once the windowed path has succeeded). `_assembled_records` tests the prefilter BEFORE the window (pure filters, so nothing yielded changes) so a walk that counts nothing still skips the settlement parse for every rejected record. Counting therefore MOVES cost between records rather than only adding it: walk A must parse a rejected record's settlement to know whether it lies in the window (a read-back-filtered slice never showed it that record), while a record the prefilter passes now pays one prefilter call per walk instead of two. Measured over the two walks together on real day-slice records streamed through `_DaySliceStream` (the first 150,000 of the 2026-09-17, -21 and -22 live slices, split by class, the 392 passing ones replicated under unique tickers; two runs): +0.15 to +0.46 µs per rejected record, −1.46 µs per passing record, and −0.12 to −0.18 µs/record (−1.5% to −2.3%) on a blend at 32.6% passing, with break-even at 10–24% passing. 32.6% is about the review window's past-day pass rate — at most 7,274,215 assembled of its 22,175,942 past-day records, at least the frontier note's 7,190,452 on 2026-09-22 alone — so there counting costs nothing measurable; a window where almost nothing passes (a head-of-slice sample passes 0.09%) pays at most the rejected-class figure, about 10 s at 22,175,942 records against that window's 2,382 s fresh fetch. The lines now read `Historical endpoint: N eligible markets of M records settled in the window before the archive cutoff (R rejected by the prefilter <tag>, D duplicate or blank tickers)`, `Live endpoint: N eligible markets of M recently settled records in the window (…)` and `Assembled N eligible markets of M records settled since <start> (…)` (`settled markets` in place of `eligible markets` and no rejected clause without a prefilter). The total is stamped into the assembled cache as the informational meta key `assembly_counts` and read back into `CorpusProvenance.assembly_counts` — `None` for every legacy cache and every streamed one written before it (the 2026-09-17 cache on disk), and dropped with a WARNING on a hit whose counts do not keep exactly the records its validating walk counted. The backtester's two lines are `backtester._log_corpus_prefilter`: with provenance (a fetched corpus) `Markets to analyze: N eligible — the eligibility prefilter (<tag>) ran during assembly and rejected R of the S records settled in the window (…)` (or that the cache records no count), then `Eligibility prefilter re-check: 0 of N markets rejected — none expected, …` — and a re-check that rejects anything on such a corpus is a WARNING, since only a `_can_ever_enter` change without a `SETTLED_PREFILTER_CACHE_TAG` bump, or an altered cache, can make it (the first line then reads `Markets to analyze: N assembled as eligible, E still eligible after the re-check below — …`, so it never calls all N eligible above a WARNING that says some are not); without provenance (a plain list, i.e. a test stub) the old `Eligibility prefilter: skipping X/N …` line stands, since that corpus's assembly is unknown. The counts are RECORDS, not markets: a repeat the prefilter passes is a `duplicate`, but a repeat it rejects is counted in `rejected` once per arrival — telling them apart would mean holding every rejected ticker, the residency SS-1 removed. The dashboard's census wording (`N of M eligible markets`) was already right and is unchanged; the page does not render the assembly counts. Pinned by `tests/test_historical.py::TestAssemblyCounts`, `::TestStreamedAssemblyParity` (hand-worked counts on the old-merge fixture), `_assert_counts_cover_the_prefilter` inside `TestShardedFetch::test_prefilter_assembly_equals_postfilter`, `TestSequentialFallbacksApplyKeep::test_prefilter_through_the_fallbacks_equals_postfilter` and `::test_a_frontier_that_rejects_records_is_counted_once_on_every_path` (the settled count is the same with and without a prefilter on the sharded/windowed path and on every fallback; the first two run at the real date, whose frontier day holds no fixture record, so the third pins "now" inside the fixture, making the frontier's own sink reject records, and forces the live fallback to discard that frontier only after it has spooled — a frontier counted anyway reads 14 settled records instead of 11), and `tests/test_backtester.py::TestPrefilterLinesSayItRanDuringAssembly`. The fetch's per-100-page progress lines (`N markets kept so far`) are unchanged and still count what the fetch itself kept (binary results in its window) BEFORE the prefilter: they measure fetch progress, not eligible markets, and renaming them is an open follow-up. (A market that passes it but shares neither grouping key with another eligible market is not grouped either — since SS-1 `_prepare_candidates` never copies it into the list it groups, and since SS-1's Commit C the fetch streams the corpus off disk, so it is resident only as the record in hand — except on a hit on a LEGACY `.json` cache, which is still one list resident until the second walk ends; see TS-07.)

**Time-series pair extraction is close-time-windowed, not naive O(n²).** For string-keyed (time-series) groups, `_extract_pairs()` sorts members by `close_time` and sweeps a nested loop with an early `break` once the window closes, bounded by `MAX_DEADLINE_GAP_DAYS + 1` days of margin — the outcome is O(n·window), not the naive O(n²), but it isn't a classic two-pointer sweep since the inner loop restarts from each outer index rather than advancing a shared second pointer. The window exists because `_find_entry()` unconditionally rejects any time-series pair whose close dates differ by more than `MAX_DEADLINE_GAP_DAYS`, so pairs outside it are skipped without ever being materialized as a candidate (counted since M10, never enumerated). The `+1` day is slack only (it can only add harmless boundary candidates that `_find_entry`'s exact cutoff then rejects, never drop a legitimate one). Same-title (3-tuple-keyed) groups have no deadline-gap concept and stay naive — the eligibility prefilter above is what keeps those groups small. Both branches also drop two events of one series (`_same_series_dicts`, plus `_identical_wording_dicts` on the time-series branch — see the one-series gotcha); grouping is deliberately left alone, so the rule lives in exactly one place on this path, as it does on the live one. The same-title branch additionally refuses, per visited candidate, a pair whose closes cannot be compared or sit more than `SAME_TITLE_MAX_CLOSE_GAP_SECONDS` apart (DR-74 — `_comparable_closes_dicts`, then `scanner.closes_apart`; see the close-gate gotcha). That is a pairing rule, not a window: the branch stays naive, and its groups are not close-filtered, so a member whose `close_time` cannot be parsed stays in its group and is refused per candidate on its own line. Since M10 each of those drops, and each same-event skip, is counted on its own silent-at-zero INFO line, and so are the two things the sweep sets aside before visiting anything — members without a readable `close_time`, and the pairs its window excludes (counted from the index at the window's `break`, once per outer member, so the sweep's cost is unchanged) — see the DR-67 observability paragraph. `config.LARGE_GROUP_WARN_THRESHOLD` (1000) logs a warning (not a cap) whenever a post-filter group is still large, as a canary that the prefilter/windowing are working as expected.

**`secrets.json` may be missing outer braces.** `auth.py` wraps it if needed. New code must call `auth.build_client()` rather than reading `secrets.json` directly.

**`CandidatePair.max_contracts = 0` means NOT ENRICHED, and nothing else.** Before `enrich_with_orderbook_prices()` it is the dataclass default (`depth_levels` is `()` alongside it) and `strategy.compute_trade` reads it as "no depth cap", taking the single-shot sizing path. After enrichment it is the affordability-bounded qualifying depth — **how many contracts the pair's written leg prices are valid for** — and enrichment never writes 0: a pair whose book holds under one contract at the gap, or whose budget cannot afford one contract pair, is dropped with `tradeable=False` and its own INFO line. That closes the older trap, where `max_contracts` came from `int(total_qty)` over a `float` quantity sum and a qualifying book with `0 < total_qty < 1` enriched to `max_contracts=0` with `tradeable=True`, which `compute_trade` then read as *uncapped*. A pair marked `tradeable=False` may still carry `max_contracts=0` from never having reached the writeback (unavailable orderbook, no qualifying depth, unaffordable) — check `pair.tradeable` first, as before.

**Backtest uses prod credentials**, not sandbox. The Kalshi historical API endpoint doesn't exist on the sandbox. `historical.py` always calls `build_prod_live_client()`.

**Dev mode skips `get_held_tickers()`** because the sandbox account is separate and has no positions from production. Don't add held-ticker filtering to `_run_dev()`.

**`normalize_title()` pattern order matters.** `_DATE_PATTERNS` in `scanner.py` applies patterns sequentially. Longer/more specific patterns must come before shorter ones to avoid partial matches leaving date fragments. If adding new patterns, prepend them before similar shorter ones.

**Event-title resolution is bounded, parallel, paced, and must stay loud.** `_load_or_build_event_titles` resolves `event_ticker` → title in three phases: bulk `get_events` listings (settled/closed/open), the MVE listing (capped at `MVE_TITLE_LOOKUP_MAX_PAGES`), then a per-ticker `/events/{ticker}` fallback. That fallback costs **one HTTP round trip per ticker** and was written for a handful of stragglers — but a 21-day window measured **289,235 unresolved tickers** live (2026-08-03), and the 2026-09-24 7-day run 3,987,139, where the original sequential, uncapped loop ground for hours emitting nothing, which is indistinguishable from a hang. It is now capped at `EVENT_TITLE_FALLBACK_MAX_LOOKUPS` (5000) and run across `EVENT_TITLE_FALLBACK_MAX_WORKERS` (8) threads, each pausing `EVENT_TITLE_FALLBACK_RATE_LIMIT_SLEEP_SECONDS` (0.15 s) after every lookup, success or failure — the `fetch_candlesticks` idiom (DR-51). Unpaced, 5,000 lookups drew 268 retried HTTP 429s in 2m03s; pacing lowers the rate but does not guarantee zero 429s — the candlestick fetch paced the same way ran at about 24 requests/s and still drew them on 4–5% of requests. **How the cap is spent (DR-51):** when every still-unresolved ticker fits under it, all are looked up (sorted); when they do not, the cap goes to NON-combo tickers only, in ticker order with the tickers the accumulator has never answered first (then its stored pills, then tickers it already titles — only the first group exists with the cache on), and every combo ticker (the KXMVE family, read through `scanner.event_series`) is deferred. A combo's event title has no measured effect on which pairs form — DR-54/55 refuse every combo-vs-combo same-title pair, and on the 2026-09-08/09 day slices no combo shared even the time-series key with a non-combo — while a non-combo's does (TS-11). The old single sorted list spent the whole cap on whatever sorted before "KXMVE" plus the head of the combo block, and on the 2026-09-24 run skipped `KXNFLEVERYWEEKCOMPETE-27` (34 markets) among the 3,982,139 tickers it skipped. This decides which LOOKUPS to spend, never an MVE exclusion: combo markets are still fetched, cached, grouped and paired by the same rules. **What it does change is an input to those rules.** A combo the old sorted cap reached was looked up and titled — that run's census logged `event_title` on 27,934 of 7,274,215 eligible records, 18,671 of them among its 18,705 non-combo records, so 9,263 combo records carried a title — and a deferred combo now stays blank unless the accumulator already holds its title. That moves those markets' grouping keys and the `event_title` and deadline-phrasing census figures, so a post-DR-51 fresh backtest's outcome-label census is not comparable with a pre-DR-51 one; it does not change which pairs form, because the 2026-09-08/09 measurement above was taken on day-slice records, which carry no `event_title` at all (it is patched in at assembly). **A deferred ticker is not looked up and nothing is stored for it (DR-51)** — its return value is whatever the accumulator already holds (`""` unless an earlier run titled it), and a later run tries it again. For non-combo tickers that later run advances past this one's slice either way: with the cache on because every ticker this call answered is no longer unresolved, and under `--no-cache` (which re-resolves every requested ticker) because the tickers the accumulator has never answered take the cap first — a plainly sorted cap looked up the same head on every `--no-cache` run and never reached the tail. Only a genuine answer is stored: a title, or the `""` poison pill for a lookup that failed or an event listed without a title, so a genuinely unresolvable ticker is not re-paid every run (no failed lookup appears in any retained backtest log). A deferral that includes non-combo tickers is a WARNING naming both counts; a combo-only deferral, expected on every bulk window and without pairing effect, is INFO, so the WARNING keeps meaning something. Every phase logs progress, and one closing INFO line partitions every ticker the CALL was asked about — resolved by this run (bulk listings / per-ticker lookups), answered from the accumulator, recorded as unresolvable, deferred — and gives the accumulator's size before and after (DR-42; the old line printed the whole seeded accumulator as "this run" — 7,986,570 on a run that resolved at most about 7,525 — and "answered from the accumulator" as 0 by construction whenever the cache was on). **Never make this phase silent again, and never remove the cap** without replacing it with something else bounded. The bulk listings are bounded too, by a productivity bail-out (`EVENT_TITLE_LISTING_MAX_BARREN_PAGES`, 50 consecutive pages resolving nothing → move on), the same idiom as `MVE_MAX_EMPTY_PAGES`. That matters because a listing is an O(all events) scan for a specific ticker set, and `get_events` **excludes MVE events by API design** — so for an MVE-heavy miss set it can never resolve them no matter how long it pages. Live-measured 2026-08-03: the `settled` listing resolved 8,696 of 289,235 tickers in its first ~500 pages, then 9 more across the next 400.

**MVE event-title plumbing.** The live scanner attaches an `_event_title` attribute to each `Market` object inside `fetch_open_events_with_markets()`. The backtest stores it as the `event_title` key in cached market dicts. Both pair-finder paths (`find_time_series_pairs`, `find_same_title_pairs`) and their backtester counterparts (`_group_by_normalized_title`, `_group_by_exact_title`) require this to be set for MVE markets — without it they fall back to grouping by market title (plus, on the time-series side, the outcome label) alone, which would let cross-event option-label collisions through. If you add a new entry point that produces markets, attach `_event_title` (or `event_title` in dict form) before passing into the grouping functions. Old backtest caches written before this change have no `event_title` field; re-run with `--no-cache` to refresh.

**Tick structure drives the V2 order price cap (2026-08).** Live market payloads now carry `price_level_structure` (a string naming the tick regime — observed: `"linear_cent"` = 1c ticks, `"tapered_deci_cent"`, `"deci_cent"` = $0.001 ticks, and `"center_deci_edge_centi_cent"` = the regime all MVE/combo markets migrated to on 2026-08-17, with $0.0001 ticks below $0.01 and above $0.99 and $0.001 ticks in between) and `price_ranges` (an array of dollar-string bands `{"start", "end", "step"}` — a tapered market has multiple bands, e.g. finer ticks near 0/1 and coarser in the middle). `scanner.ApiMarket` now carries both (`price_level_structure: str = ""`, `price_ranges: list[PriceRange] | None = None`, parsed by `scanner._parse_price_ranges`, fail-soft to `None` on anything malformed), and `historical._market_to_dict` passes both through **raw** (not parsed) into the backtest cache, since that dict is JSON-serialized directly. **`scanner.tick_size_for_price(market, price_dollars)` now reads both** to derive the tick grid a V2 order price cap must land on: it returns the **FINEST** `step` among the `price_ranges` bands containing the price, as a `Decimal`, short-circuiting to `Decimal(config.DEFAULT_TICK_SIZE_DOLLARS)` (`"0.01"`) when `price_level_structure` is `""`/`"linear_cent"` or the bands are absent, and falling back to that same default with a `logging.warning` when no band contains the price or every containing band's step is nonpositive. **Finest, not first (TS-10).** A price sitting exactly on a band boundary belongs to two bands, and first-match is only "finer" at a band's LOWER edge — at an UPPER edge the earlier band is 10x COARSER, so `BUY_SLIPPAGE_TICKS` was multiplied by a 10x tick exactly there and the buy cap, which is a bid, was LOOSENED (`tick_size_for_price(m, 0.99)` returned 0.001 where the finest containing band is 0.0001). Scanning every band also means one malformed band no longer discards the valid ones after it, which the old `break` did. The fallback is always safe because the grids are nested ($0.01 ⊂ $0.001 ⊂ $0.0001) — a coarse-grid price is valid on any finer grid. The reverse is NOT true: a fine-grid cap that steps up into a coarser band (or is complemented across the book) can land off that band's grid, which is why `_v2_limit_price` re-quantizes the final price onto the grid of the band containing it. Old backtest cache records (written before this change) lack both keys entirely and read back as `None`; every reader goes through `.get()`, so this is not a crash risk. **On 2026-08-17, all MVE/combo markets migrated from $0.001 to the center_deci_edge_centi_cent regime described above.** That is what made the legacy cap untenable: `trader._buy_max_cost_cents`'s FoK price protection (`BUY_MAX_COST_SLIPPAGE_CENTS`, config.py) is denominated in whole cents — 1c of tolerance, roughly "one tick" back on a $0.001 grid, now means roughly 10-100 ticks of permitted drift depending on where in the band the price sits. **Resolved by the V2 migration:** order submission now defaults to the V2 endpoint `/portfolio/events/orders` (`config.V2_ORDER_PATH`, `ORDER_API_VERSION="v2"`) — dollar-string fill-or-kill LIMIT prices, fixed-point counts, bid/ask sides on the single YES book, and an explicit `exchange_index` — where the tick-aware cap `_v2_limit_price()` (scanned price ceiled to `tick_size_for_price()` plus `BUY_SLIPPAGE_TICKS` ticks) is finally expressible. The legacy `/portfolio/orders` path (deprecated June 2026, 5x rate-limit tokens, integer-cent `buy_max_cost`) is retained **unmodified** behind `ORDER_API_VERSION="legacy"` as the instant rollback; the side/price mapping lives in `trader._V2_LEG_SIDE`. Do not attempt a cents-based tick-aware cap on the legacy endpoint — it cannot express one. Fractional-contract sizing remains a deferred follow-up (`_format_count` always emits `.00`).

**V2 order semantics — assumptions to verify at the first live submission.** The V2 mapping was built from the docs, not from an observed live order, and every piece of it is deliberately arranged so a correction is a one-line change:
- **Side mapping** (`trader._V2_LEG_SIDE`, the single point of correction): buy YES = `bid` at the capped YES price; buy NO = `ask` at `1 − cap` (selling YES you don't hold IS a long NO); the rollback closes a held NO by buying the YES short back, i.e. a **reduce-only `bid`** at the highest tradeable level of the market's own grid (`trader._v2_rollback_price()`: the finest-grid target `V2_ROLLBACK_BID_PRICE_DOLLARS` = "0.9999" floored onto the market's grid — 0.99 on linear-cent, 0.999 on deci-cent, 0.9999 on a centi-cent edge band — emulating the legacy capless `type="market"` unwind; a flat 0.99 would fail to cross asks resting in (0.99, 1) on sub-cent regimes, and $1.00 is not a tradeable level). If the exchange reads a leg the other way round, flip the value in that dict — no builder logic moves.
- **Runtime backstop on the NO-leg mapping** (`trader._confirm_v2_no_mapping(client, spec, no_leg, before_no)`): the side mapping above cannot be proven offline, so the **first V2 NO-leg fill of each process** is checked against the account's own signed positions ledger before the YES leg is submitted. It is judged by the **position DELTA across the fill, never by the absolute sign** — the module's standing rule (see the delta gotcha above). `_execute_one` hands in the NO-leg baseline it already took before submitting, and the backstop compares `_fill_delta(before_no, after)` against `-no_leg.count`. Absolute sign was wrong in both directions: an account already long +100 YES on the NO leg's market that buys 10 NO nets +90, a *positive* reading that would have declared the mapping disproven and stopped the pair at `manual_review` with a real unhedged NO leg, while an account already short −100 masks a genuinely wrong mapping and latches a false confirmation for the rest of the process. A delta of **−`no_leg.count`** confirms the hypothesis (an `ask` really did open a NO position) and latches the module-level `_V2_NO_MAPPING_CONFIRMED`, so the check costs one extra positions read per **process**, not per trade — the mapping is a property of the exchange, and one confirmation proves it for every later trade in the run; the latch is deliberately not persisted, so a fresh process re-verifies. For a time-series pair the NO leg is `market_b`, so the read is of `market_b`'s ticker; the latch is **shared across pair types** because it verifies the exchange's side mapping, not a market — a same-title confirmation covers later time-series trades and vice versa. **Any other delta** means the mapping is DISPROVEN live; a delta of **zero** is re-read once after `_V2_MAPPING_RECHECK_DELAY_SECONDS` (1s) before that verdict, because an unmoved ledger immediately after a confirmed fill is usually read-after-write lag, not disproof (a persistent zero — fill reported, ledger flat — is still contradictory and goes to manual review). On disproof: the pair stops at `status="manual_review"` with **the YES leg unsubmitted** (it would hedge a position we don't hold) and **the NO leg deliberately left in place** — the unwind is a `bid` resting on the same disproven hypothesis, so an automated unwind could double the error rather than reverse it (`reduce_only` would make a wrong unwind fail safe into `rollback_failed`, but a human must decide); a `logging.critical` names the remedy, `ORDER_API_VERSION="legacy"`. An **unknown delta** (either snapshot missing) is not disproof: it warns, proceeds to the YES leg, and does NOT latch, so the check re-arms on the next NO fill — one flaky positions read must never stall trading. **This read is ONE OF THE TWO SINGLE-SHOT position reads in that window** (`trader._position_count_once`, sharing `_read_position`'s parse with the retried `_position_count`; the other is `_execute_one`'s NO-leg ledger-lag re-read, DR-64, which also pays a 1s pause): it is a blocking call inside the window where the NO leg is filled and unhedged, `api_call_with_retry` can hold a call for ~62s of sleeps, and because a failing endpoint never latches, **every** V2 trade in a 429 storm would pay that stall with a naked NO leg — the same bounded-wait reasoning `_await_transfer_settlement` uses for its unretried balance reads. Don't retry-wrap it, don't make this check unconditional per-trade (it is a per-process property), and don't turn the disproven branch into an automatic rollback.
- **Fill classification:** a full FoK fill is assumed to report `fill_count == count` and a kill to report **2xx with `fill_count == 0`**. `_v2_fill_status()` maps only those two to the legacy `"executed"`/`"canceled"` vocabulary; a partial fill (a FoK invariant violation), a missing `fill_count`, or anything else logs `logging.critical` and **raises**. That is the design, not an oversight: raising routes into `_execute_one`'s existing ambiguous-exception path, which consults `_position_count` (the account's ground truth), so a mispredicted response shape degrades safely into `failed`/`rolled_back`/`manual_review` instead of being misread as a clean fill or a clean kill. Never "simplify" it into returning a guessed status.
- **Response shape:** the order object may arrive wrapped under an `"order"` key or flat, and counts as either `fill_count` or the fixed-point string `fill_count_fp` — all four combinations are accepted (`_parse_fixed_point` prefers the `_fp` variant).
- **Sandbox V2 support is unknown.** Dev mode never submits orders, so it cannot verify this; the smallest possible prod run is the real check — and `python3 -m kalshi_betting.v2_probe` is the ~1-cent way to verify the NO-leg mapping, FoK kill semantics, and the transfer centicent unit before real size flows. If anything misbehaves, set `ORDER_API_VERSION="legacy"` — that is the whole reason the legacy path is retained intact. **The probe verifies a SIDE mapping, not a price, so two of its own prices are deliberately NOT the builders' (DR-04).** `v2_probe._no_close_body` overrides the rollback builder's price with `trader._format_price(trader._v2_top_of_grid_price(market))` — the same level `_step_unfillable_ask` submits at. Exactly TWO keys are overridden, the price and the count (the V2 fractional minimum, `PROBE_COUNT_STR`); every other key — side, `reduce_only`, time-in-force, ticker and shard — stays the builder's. The builder's price is a loss floor derived from the leg's scanned entry, and the probe's placeholder entry of 0.5 puts that floor at **$0.62 on every market and every tick regime**, so the close was structurally killed on any market whose YES ask is above 62c: the one gate deciding whether the bot stays on V2 FAILed for a reason unrelated to the mapping, told the operator to downgrade to legacy, and left a real 0.01 NO position open. `reduce_only=True` is what bounds the top-of-grid bid — it can only close the 0.01 contracts just opened. Same commit, same reasoning one step earlier: a position of exactly 0 after a reported full fill is **re-read once** after `trader._V2_MAPPING_RECHECK_DELAY_SECONDS`, BEFORE any sign branch, so a re-read that comes back positive (disproven) or `None` (lookup failed) still takes its own branch (DR-21); a still-zero ledger keeps its FAIL, now worded "still 0 after a re-read".
